                                FOREWORD

Preparations and plans are progressing for our 75th Anniversary of Java,
South Dakota.

We honor the early pioneers who had the courage to settle in this area.
Many of the pioneers are gone but memories linger- of those precious
years.

Many thanks to the people of Java and the many others who participated
in submitting information for this Jubilee Book.

Incomplete records sometimes gave us vague information but we tried to
the best of our perception; even so some mistakes and omissions may
occur. The Jubilee Book committee does not claim to be without error, as
perfection was not our goal, and any mistakes are unintentional.

Because of publishing costs, some material has been edited and shortened
and we ask your understanding.

We have tried to make this 75th Jubilee Book as interesting as possible
and hope you will cherish it in the forthcoming years.

The 75th Jubilee Book Committee

Mrs. Elmer W. Schlepp (Chairman)
Mrs. Chris J. Pfitzer
Mrs. Reuben Schmierer
Mr. Harold F. Spiry
Mr. LeRoy Bender
Mr. Bruce Clement
Mr. and Mrs. Fritz Clement

History of Early Java

From its inception in the autumn of 1900 shortly after the Chicago,
Milwaukee & St. Paul Railway Line was extended from Bowdle to the
Missouri River, and for approximately three years thereafter, the
status of Java was simply that of a village, possessing no special form
of government. Its growth had been steady during this period, the
population being estimated at about 150 souls.

Early in the fall of 1903, after a mass meeting of the citizenry, it
was decided to incorporate from a village to the status of a town, with
a regular form of government suitable to its size and class. On
September 5, of that year, State Surveyor George Merriman of Bowdle,
was in Java to survey the townsite preparatory to incorporation. Early in
October, the following notice was advertised in the local newspaper:

                     NOTICE OF ELECTION

Notice is hereby given, that an election will be held on Monday,
October 19, 1903, by the legal voters of the proposed incorporated Town
of Java, Walworth County, South Dakota to vote upon the question,
whether or not said proposed incorporated town shall be incorporated
or not as such Town of Java. The voters who favor the incorporation of
such Town shall have upon their ballots the words "For Incorporation,
Yes", and the voters who do not favor such incorporation, shall have
upon their ballots the words, "For Incorporation, No." Said election
will be held at the store of W. P. Winkley within said proposed
incorporated town and polls will be open from 9 o'clock a .m. of said
day, the 19th day of October, 1903 until 4 o'clock p.m. of said day.

        Dated at Bangor, Walworth County, S. Dak. October 7, 1903.

SEAL
By Order of the Board of County Commissioners of Walworth County, S.
Dak. Geo. W. Ryan, County Auditor.

The results of this election showed 47 votes being cast, 45 for
incorporation, 2 against, which was not unexpected, since a great
majority of the citizens had previously favored incorporation. After
the polls were closed, the Java Cornet Band rendered some fine music
out on the streets in honor of the event. During the evening, the
Cornet Band serenaded each business place in Java, completing a day of
celebration, with all the citizens participating.

On November 23, 1903, another election was held for the purpose of
electing a Board of Trustees. Selected to serve on the first town board
were: David Blinderman, 1st Ward; William F. Ewald, 2nd Ward; John
Hofmeyer, 3rd Ward. The Board of Trustees of the Town of Java held its
organizational meeting on November 27, 1903, at the store of John Hof-
meyer, with all the elected trustees and Christian Huether, clerk
attending. The following is a brief outline of the business transacted
at this meeting: Upon motion by William Ewald, John Hofmeyer was
elected president and thus the Board of Trustees was organized. The
president received instructions to order the necessary books and proper
blanks for recording their future transactions. William Ewald was
charged with selecting a permanent meeting place for the Board. Upon
motion, duly seconded and carried, to engage the services of an
attorney to draw up the necessary ordinances as well as executing the
other legal affairs of the new corporation. Carl F. Clement was hired
at a salary of $30 per month; to serve as long as deemed necessary by
the board. The clerk was instructed to purchase a star and billy for
the marshal's use.

The Board of Trustees met again on December 3, 1903, at which time
ordinances number 1 and 2 were read and approved, with Jacob Schafer
being appointed as the town fire warden. The following day, December 4,
the board again met, designating the office of the Mississippi Lumber
Company as their regular place of meeting. At this meeting, W. F. Dyer
was named treasurer; Martin Gehrki Jr., assessor; Jacob Schafer, as
Marshal, Frederick Krause, Justice of Peace; and Joseph Bosch, Overseer
of Highways. Prior to adjournment, ordinances number 3 and 4 were read
and approved.

It was at this time that the small town of Java began to prosper and
its development and expansion was rapid for a number of years. The
first notable improvement after organization came when the Dakota
Central Telephone Company installed a telephone booth and switchboard
at The Java House, early in 1904, as well as a number of new telephones
within the town, filling a long felt need.

Other improvements followed during the spring and summer of 1904, one
of which was the erection of the first municipal building, a jail,
being located on the lot west of the present municipal building. Jacob
Fiechtner built the jail building at a bid price of $239, while another
bid submitted by John Hill, (known as the old Captain) lost out being
six dollars higher. Later the jail building was moved to the lot in
back of the old city hall, and for a time served as a dwelling and shoe
repair shop before being sold and moved to an area farm. Also in 1904,
streets were laid out, graded and graveled; a new engine house was
built and equipped with a fire engine, hoses and related items. In the
course of time wooden sidewalks were added, a water reservoir was con-
structed, a town hall erected, and a town well dug and equipped with a
windmill. These early improvements were paid by warrants issued by the
town, however, in 1906 a bond issue was passed in the amount of $2000
to remove the indebtedness caused by these needed improvements.

                          The
                       NEW TOWN
                        of JAVA
   Is an incorporated town in Walworth County,
   South Dakota, on the Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul
   Railway about 70 miles west of Aberdeen. Although
   only three years old, Java has about 280 inhabitants
   and supports a fine graded school, one church, five
   general stores, drug store, bank, newspaper, two
   liveries, four implement warehouses and various
   other places of business. Java has three large grain
   elevators, a grist mill and two lumber yards.

   The town is growing fast in its pace to keep up with
   the demands of the large and rapidly developing
   territory tributary thereto.

   If you are seeking a new business location, look
   well into the inducements offered by Java. For prices
   and terms of sale, call on
                    J. B. Corson, Local Agent, Java, S.D.
   or,
   C. A. Padley, General Land Agent
   Milwaukee, Wisconsin.

--Early day Milwaukee Railroad land
  advertisement describing Java

In 1905, the Kupp Brothers, J. C. and George, supervised the
installation of a more permanent telephone system in the town, doing a
masterful job for the Dakota Telephone Co.

The town of Java had at one time been in contention as a site for the
courthouse, having filed a petition with the County Commissioners. Java
later withdrew its petition when a legal question arose concerning the
registration of qualified voters within the county.

By the year 1907, the town had progressed considerably, having provided
such things as fire protection, organization of a cemetery association,
adequate water supply, municipal buildings, improved telephone
facilities, met with educational needs, to name a few, a very
commendable achievement for a small town in those times. Another in-
dication of growth was noted when the Milwaukee Land Company had their
property north of the track surveyed and another addition to the town
was platted.

During 1908-1909, the railroad company rerouted its track, passing
north of the town approximately one-half mile. This event was
considered a rather severe setback to the town. The citizens had
received a proposal from railroad officials to move the town north,
however, this proposal was refused, the town thereafter being served by
a spur line constructed by the company.

From a population of 277 in 1905, the citizenry of Java increased to
473 in 1910, and showed a decline to 410 by 1915. The intervening years
between 1909 and 1915 were relatively quiet ones. A major improvement
occurred in the town during the summer of 1915, the wooden sidewalks
along Main street were removed, and replaced with cement sidewalks,

        Reproduction of an early day Java picture postcard

the first along the business places of August C. Schlepp and Emanuel
Baer. Many residents also improved their properties by replacing wooden
walks with cement. Later that year the town hall was remodeled, the
jail was moved the work being performed by George Berreth. In addition a
bell tower was constructed that year, with the bell serving as a fire
alarm and to announce the curfew hour.

A petition circulated in the spring of 1916 called for a special
election by the qualified voters, to determine whether or not the town
should issue bonds amounting to $8000 to be used in constructing a
municipal light plant. The date of the election being June 4, saw 80
votes cast, 48 in favor and 32 against the bond issue. In July bids
were opened, with A. F. Kaufman of Appleton, Minn., submitting the
successful bid for the plant in the amount of $4,730, the successful
bidder for the building being Gunder Johnson, at $875. Near the end of
September all work was accomplished and the electric current turned on.

During the period from 1918 through 1921 perhaps the greatest building
boom was witnessed, primarily in the residential portions of the town,
when many of the finest homes in town were built. The Java Cooperative
Creamery was launched in 1920, and during the year 1921, the
educational needs were met when. a fine new fireproof schoolhouse was
constructed, many of the town streets were re-shaped and re-surfaced
with gravel. It was at this time that a change in the town's status
took place, when Java was incorporated into a city, and adopted the
Mayor-Alderman form of government which has continued to the present
time. Serving as the first mayor under this type of government was Carl
F. Clement. Others were: W. C. Hicks, Philip Spiry Sr. Albert Lange,
Martin Gehrki Jr., Charles Schlepp, William Kunz, August C. Schlepp,
Col. E. C. Weatherly, I. R. Krause, Joe Zarka, Wesley Frank, Paul
Jacobs and John Zeller; the present term of mayor being served by
Wesley Frank.

In 1923 a larger engine was purchased to power the electric light
plant, necessitated by an increased demand for electricity, and 24 hour
service provided. The following year improved fire fighting was added.
During 1925, the building housing the electric light plant was
renovated when yet another new and larger engine-generator was
purchased to update and adequately meet ever increasing demands for
electric service. Also at this time the city streets received
additional gravel.

The next ten years passed rather uneventfully. A compressed air
whistle had been installed at the Java Auto Garage, to function in
sounding the alarm in case of fire. This was later moved to the city
power plant.

An extensive street program was instituted in 1936, which included the
opening of Chicago street, leading to the nearby county highway. In the
autumn followed the strategic placement of hydrants to provide improved
fire protection, in connection with the beginning of a waterworks
system. Soon a project was undertaken to enlarge the waterworks system
in order to include commercial and private users. This planning was
culminated in 1939, when a Public Works Administration project was
begun. Another outstanding milestone was reached with the completion of
this new waterworks system.

Another improvement to provide additional fire protection was noted
with the purchase of a truck in 1939, which updated the fire fighting
equipment of the Java Volunteer Fire Department.

During the year 1940, attention again focused on the need for further
municipal improvements, and plans were formulated by architect Roland
Wilcken for a new municipal building. With the approval of plans and
specifications, a W.P.A. project began in the autumn, with work
continuing during the winter. This building of reinforced concrete,
measuring 75x50 feet was completed in 1941. Electric plant equipment
again was updated with the purchase of a more adequate light and power
system. In 1946 an election was held which carried, resulting in the
decision to sell the power plant equipment to Montana-Dakota Utilities
Company, a 20-year franchise was approved with that company; they then
constructed a complete transmission system in the town. The town is
currently being served under a second 20-year franchise, by M.D.U.

Also during 1946, street improvements were implemented with Main street
receiving major attention. The old city hall had been sold to a Mr.
Moore of Leola, who in turn resold it to a group of concerned Java
citizens, they then turned the site and building over to the LaVerne
Bauer Legion Post, where this organization functioned until erection of
their present facility .

The year 1947 saw the creation of a park board who carried out its
duties by establishing a city park on a piece of property just west of
the school house. The park later was improved with playground
equipment, picnic facilities, water piped in, and is regularly
maintained for use by the public. Also during this year earnest
attention was given to a project to provide a much needed and sought
after sewer plant and disposal system. A bond issue passed and revenue
bonds were issued, construction beginning in 1948.

Marking 50 years of progress, Java celebrated its Golden Jubilee on
June 14 and 15, 1950. Extensive plans had been formulated for this
occasion which was largely attended. Programmed events included: Most
excellent parades, baseball games, rodeo, street sports, orations, free
stage shows, band concerts, whisker judging contest, movies, dances,
and a carnival, providing everyone in attendance with a most enjoyable
time.

                       The Advent of Java
                       By LeRoy C. Bender

"Hear that train a'comin, Comin 'round the bend." These words from a
well-known song may very well be reminiscent of the events preceding
the founding of the town of Java.

It is not exactly known when the officials of the Chicago, Milwaukee,
and St. Paul Railway came to the decision to extend their railroad line
from Bowdle to the Missouri River, however, it appears such a decision
was reached shortly before the turn of the century, or just after. This
extension of the line ultimately resulted in the rise of four new towns
along its course.

Early in February, 1900, a survey crew comprising of nearly twenty men
commenced the survey of the new grade for the planned extension,
heading due west from Bowdle. Rumors and speculation ran rampant as to
where the new railroad would be built. One faction claimed that the
shortest distance between two points is a straight line and surely the
survey crew would follow such a line of thought. By the 23rd of
February, the survey crew met its first obstacle, the hills north of
Spring Lake, and the survey skirted these hills, going north where
easier grading and less construction expense seemed to justify the
additional distance. Continuing in a westerly direction, the survey
crew found a gradual decline to the Missouri River by way of Blue
Blanket Creek, which proved to be the only acceptable route to the
river.

About the middle of April, 1900, grading began at Bowdle, and on the
21st of the same month, grading work began at the western terminus,
where the town of Evarts was to be founded. In June, it became known
that four new towns were being planned along the Milwaukee extension,
the first on the McDowell claim, which had been purchased by the
Milwaukee Land Company for townsite purposes. An additional purchase
of the Payne claim, adjoining the McDowell claim was consummated,
enlarging the proposed townsite property.

Early in July, the settlers in the northeastern portion of Walworth
county were circulating petitions praying for the immediate platting of
a town upon the newly acquired townsite property. It had been found
that the McDowell claim was encumbered by a school mortgage and efforts
were underway to legally lift this mortgage in order to plat the land
and offer lots for sale, for it was contrary to railroad regulation to
sell any lots not absolutely perfect in title.

Later in July, on the 17th, the laying of rails began west of Bowdle.
By the 28th of the month, the new townsite was being surveyed and
advertisements in area newspapers alerted the pubic to watch for the
opening date for sale of lots, and encouraged prospective business
men and others, to purchase and locate on them. Applications were to be
made to the Land Department, C.M.&St.P. Ry., Milwaukee, Wis.

During the grading and rail-laying period, a large encampment of
laborers was situated on a portion of the townsite property. According
to one of the older residents of the present town, much fighting
occurred among the men, and deaths were not uncommon. It is most
interesting to note that these laborers dubbed the prospective new town
"Java" in honor of the good coffee served at a small shack which was
situated where the town's stockyards were later built. By October 20,
the name "Java" was made official, and so it has remained these
seventy-five years.

The Dedication Plat for the town of Java was filed October 23, 1900,
and the following day, lots were placed on sale, at the home of James
C. Corson, the Townsite Agent for the Milwaukee Land Company. From
available information, it appears the first purchase of lots was made
by John Hofmeyer, who had been operating a pioneer store at Rosella,
Post Office, in Highland Township. Mr. Hofmeyer, by his own claim, was
the first to break ground for a business establishment, however, the
distinction of having the first building and business in the new town
belongs to Henry Thielen, of Bowdle, who had a small store in the area
where the stockyards came to be erected, and this building and business
may well be the same one that served that "good coffee" to the railroad
crews.

On November 23, 1900, a route inspector for the Post Office
Department visited Java, gathering information relative to prospective
mail routes, not only in town, but for the surrounding areas of
settlement. Among the first applicants for the postmastership of Java,
was Mr. T. B. Bowlus, a pioneer resident of the vicinity, and it was
Mr. Bowlus that received the appointment as the first postmaster, near
the end of December, 1900.

By December 1, 1900, three buildings were in the construction
process, by January 5, 1901, several businesses had been established
and the new town boasted of having 24 buildings. Java was booming, with
the boom lasting for several years. The town did not draw its business
houses and citizenry only from other defunct towns in the immediate
area, but was made up largely of those from outside areas wishing to
embark on business ventures of their own, by local settlers, and by
operators of pioneer stores and Post Offices such as Rosella, Spiry,
Beaulieu, and others, which had given service to the various
settlements. Several branch offices from business establishments in
other communities also began operation in the new town. The growth of
Java was phenomenal. One of the pioneer settlers of the area, Mr.
Hessel Dyk, whose name and person is remembered by many, stated in his
remembrances that, "I had never seen a place grow like Java did the
first years of its existence. It seemed like we would go to bed at
night and wake up the next morning to find that three or four new
buildings had gone up overnight."

As early as the middle of March, 1901, Java became linked with the
outside world, not only by the railroad and Post Office, but by
telephone connections as well, when a telephone line was completed from
Buchecker's Junction.

An article which appeared in the Jan. 5, 1901 issue of the Walworth
County Record, authored by a gentleman, presumably of the Java area,
who signed himself only as "Farmer Joe", and extolling the very early
virtues and accomplishments of Java, contained an ending which is
appropriate now as then: "Long Live Java!"

                  Tales of Early Java
                     By Hessel Dyk

In the spring of 1889, finding I could not get enough work to keep me
as busy as I wished to be, I left my old home in Holland and sailed for
the United States, going directly to Grand Rapids, Michigan, where I
had a cousin living. I stayed with this cousin for about ten days after
which I proceeded to Bowdle, S.D. At this time I was taken to the home
of a friend of mine, by the name of Van der Vorm, whose farm was
located east of the present Haux' Grove. I stayed here a short time and
then hired out for the summer to some folks by the name of Roper Bosch,
I received the top wages for my work, which were at that time $18 a
month. To a boy just over from the old country, I thought I was getting
lots of money, and I was for those days. I worked for these people for
two years and managed to save up quite a little money. I had in the
meantime taken up land, my pre-emption being the quarter upon which
John Pregitzer is now living.

After leaving Roper Bosch, I batched it for a year with a man by the
name of John Hegert and farmed my quarter at the same time. Our shack
was located a little northwest of the Garret Noble farm. Later on
finding that it would cost me $200 to prove up on this quarter, and
realizing that the land was so cheap I let it go back to the
government. Later, however, I took up my homestead which is located
about two miles south of Java.

I farmed with two oxen and two horses. I had a fine buggy which was
considered "some class" in those days. I believe that a good buggy in
those early times was considered more than an automobile is today, for
they were by no means common. Our crops were fair, but we had to haul
every bit of our grain to Bowdle and when you consider our motive power
was oxen, you will imagine that this was anything but a pleasant job.

My first impression of South Dakota was anything but pleasant. All I
could see for miles and miles was prairie, with no towns and only a few
houses and farms to break the monotony. I thought to myself that I
would get out of here as soon as I could, but being broke I had to go
to work and earn the necessary money. During the time required to earn
this money, however, I grew to like the country, a fact that is self-
evident, because I am still here, and intend to remain here until I
pass out.

When one steps into a steam heated train or a fine automobile, a matter
of a few hundred miles doesn't worry one, but I can recall how, way
back in February, 1895, Roper Bosch and I drove to Andover to visit
some friends. It was an awful trip and on the way back we drove from
Aberdeen to Bowdle against a stiff northwest wind, with the thermometer
registering around twenty below zero. Talk about your pleasant
trips,--it took us two days to make Andover and the same coming back.

In the fall of 1900, things being kind of dead here I went back to
Michigan to visit my cousin, where I remained until the following
spring. During my absence the railroad had been extended to Evarts and
upon my arrival here everything was a hustle and a bustle. The town of
Java had seemed to spring up overnight and I decided that I would live
in town and farm at the same time. I have done this ever since and the
past twenty years have seen Java grow from nothing to a fair sized and
prosperous little city.

In the earlier days of the town, things were sort of wild with gambling
houses wide open. It was not until a few years later when the town had
established a stable form of government, that things quieted down
somewhat. I had never seen a place grow like Java did the first five
years of its existence. It seemed like we would go to bed at night and
wake up the next morning to find that three or four new buildings had
gone up overnight.

Shortly after the Java Herald was started and for many years
afterwards, I used to help them print and fold the papers. I can recall
how many times we used to work all night to get the English and German
papers out. It was tedious work, printing all those papers on the old
hand press and them fold them afterwards. When we used the old hand
press we had to roll them off by hand, ink by hand, a very slow and
tiresome Job. It was always a big relief to all of us when the paper
was out and in the postoffice.

I have lived in Java ever since it was started and in the United States
for thirty-four years, and my residence in this country and town has
been very pleasant. When I left Holland I had to break all my home
ties, but I have never regretted coming, to become a citizen of the
best and biggest nation in the world, and I am perfectly contented to
live in South Dakota and Java until my time on this world is up. The
early days were hard ones, but those hardships make these days of
comparative luxury more pleasant.

        HESSEL DYK, PIONEER OF JAVA, PASSES AWAY

Funeral services were held in Java, Wednesday, Dec. 29, 1948, for
Hessel Dyk, about 88, who passed away at an Aberdeen hospital on
Friday, Dec. 24, after an illness of a few days duration. He had been
staying at the Fairview Rest Home for a number of years, due to the
infirmities of old age.

Mr. Dyk was one of the early pioneers who homesteaded near the present
site of Java, and after the town was started made his home in
Java until a few years ago. He disposed of his land, southeast of the
town, and had not been actively farming for many years.

He was born in Holland and his mother passed away when he was 7 years
of age. After his father's remarriage, he left home and came to this
country to make his home. He was never married, and his only known near
relatives are a nephew in this country and a niece in Holland.

When the Java Herald was established in Java in 1903, he worked in the
print shop for a number of years as a pressman. One of the colorful old
pioneers of this end of the county, he was known by most of the older
residents.

He attended the Presbyterian church in Java for many years. Funeral
services were held in the S.D. Adventist church in Java at 2 p.m.,
with Rev. Ford of the Presbyterian church of Pollock, officiating.
Interment was made in the Java cemetery.

Pallbearers were: John Witlock, Joe Zarka, Albert and Donald Stangl,
John Lutz, and G. W. Hicks. Special music was rendered by a girls'
quartet.

Hessel Dyk's remembrances were taken from The Java Herald of September
7, 1923, and his obituary also from the Herald.

        Java Was Booming in the Year 1913

Java, "The Coffee Town", was a booming bursting-at-the seams,
energetic, progressive town in the year 1913. The streets were filled
with shops, stores, garages, hotels; city shoppers were busy buying
merchandise for the coming week. Farmers, with their buggies and
wagons, were swarming into town on this warm Saturday afternoon.

The town at this time, 1913, had a total population of 473, according
to the official "South Dakota Gazetteer". The village was located on
the CM&St. P. Railway. Its spiritual life, too, was not forgotten; it
had a Catholic, a Presbyterian, a German Lutheran, and a German
Congregational Church. The business establishments included 2 banks, a
hotel, an opera house, 4 grain elevators, and a food mill. Two weekly
newspapers, the Java Herold and the Java Herald were published. Express
was distributed by the Wells, Fargo Company; the telegrams, by Western
Union; the mail by Jacob Stoller, postmaster.

The residents of the area hurried from business place to business
place, from store to store, from bank to bank, - everyone hurrying to
get their weekly business completed on this celebration day. Then back
home, to the farm, before the setting sun.

People of German, Russian, Dutch, English and Hungarian ancestry were
hurrying to the following businesses to complete their weekly
transactions:
  Atlas Lumber Company, E. E. Lehman, Agent: Emanual Baer, Real Estate,
  the Bagley Elevator Company, William Ewald, Agent; John Banik Farm
  Implements, Bauer & Stoller (John Bauer, Jacob Stoller) General Store;
  David Blinderman, General Store, Henry Buckenberger, Harness Shop; the
  Central Hotel, George Zeller, Proprietor; Central Lumber Company, Henry
  Thielen, Agent; Carl F. Clement, Lawyer; William E. Cole, Railroad
  Express and Telegraph Agent; Henry J. Cook, General Store; the Crown
  Elevator Company, Henry Bauder, Agent; the Karl Doerr Saloon; the First
  State Bank (capital, $10,000), W. C. Hicks, President and Cashier; the
  German State Bank (capital, $10,000) Philip Spiry, President and
  Cashier; the Helm Mercantile Company, Ludwig L. Helm, Manager; Helm &
  Schlaht (Christ Helm and John Schlaht) Saloon; Robert M. Hennings Drug
  Store; Wilson C. Hicks, Insurance; the Java Herold ( German ), C . F .
  Clement, Publisher; the Java Herald (English), Hud C. Mains, Publisher;
  the Java Opera House, Albert Lange, Manager; the Krause Mercantile
  Company, I. R. Krause, President, Fred Krause, Secretary and Treasurer;
  the Albert Lange General Store - the Marshall Oil Company, Rochus Aman,

  Martin Gehrki Agent, Sigmond Rosenthal, Physician; August C. Schlepp,
  Barber; John C. Schlepp Photographic Studio; Christ Schnaib]e Feed

  Automobiles; and the Zeller Implement, George Zeller, Proprietor .

The young and vigorous midwestern town was celebrating its "Lucky
Thirteenth" anniversary--and business was booming!     --BY HAROLD F. SPIRY
