Java Business Histories

This history, concerning the early-day and later houses of business in
the town of Java, has been compiled mainly from newspaper files,
personal remembrances (both written and oral), and whatever other
sources were at hand. In the event that some former place of business
has not been mentioned, let the reader be assured it is unintentional
as certain business ventures such as the dray lines, cream stations,
liveries and others frequently changed ownership; and such changes were
not always noted in the various research sources.

No doubt among the earliest transactions connected in any circumstance
with the town of Java would be the acquisition of the railway
right-of-way and townsite property, followed by the sale of townsite
lots by the Milwaukee Land Company through their townsite agent, James
C. Corson. Mr. Corson conducted the sale of lots at his home located
about a half-mile east of the town, which is presently the farm
occupied by the Harold Goetz family.

First among the actual townsite businesses was that of Henry Thielen,
of Bowdle, who commenced his building about November 8, 1900. This was
soon completed and stocked with a variety of goods. It is also probable
that Mr. Thielen operated a refreshment shack located near the railway
siding, which served the railroad construction crews during the summer
of 1900. But whether he owned this business or managed it for the
railway company has not been definitely ascertained.

John Bauer, Jacob Hoff, and Jacob Stoller came from Eureka, constructed
a large store, 26x50, and stocked a fine line of general merchandise,
opening for business in December of 1900 under the firm name of Bauer,
Hoff, and Stoller. In 1905 an addition was built on the south side in
order to expand the business. Mr. Hoff left the partnership in 1908,
with Bauer and Stoller continuing the venture. From about 1904 until
the middle of 1913, this store housed the post office, as Mr. Stoller
had been appointed postmaster; and, with his efficient deputy, Miss
Annette Stoller, had given the patrons very creditable service. In
1915, Jacob Stoller dissolved the partnership. The firm then operated
as Bauer & Sons for many years,--in later years by Miss Evelyn Bauer
and her brothers, John Jr., and Fred E. When, in 1950, Java celebrated
its fiftieth anniversary, Bauer & Sons was the oldest pioneer store
remaining, not only in Java, but in Walworth county as well. A
disastrous fire in the later years of the store's existence severely
damaged the original portion of the store; the salvaged portion was
remodeled, and the family has been the site of Pfitzer Creamery and
later Paint Store, operated by Mr. and Mrs. Chris J. Pfitzer,--and for a
time, housed Dee's Beauty Salon.

Shortly after Bauer, Hoff and Stoller opened for business, another
general mercantile was completed south of Bauer, Hoff and Stoller, that
of John Hofmeyer, who came to Java from Highland Township. He erected a
two-story building, also 25x50. Mr. Hofmeyer was best known for his
genial way of doing business and continued in business until early in
1912 when for a short time the business was operated by William Curran
who, in turn, sold to Albert Lange in April of that year. Mr. Hofmeyer
was the first to break ground in the town of Java and possibly the
first to buy lots in the new town. He was a member of the first town
board; the original business meeting of the town of Java was held at
his store. The Hofmeyer family left Java in 1912, settling in an
eastern state ( possibly Virginia ); but he retained great interest in
the town he helped organize. The Hofmeyer Store was a landmark for many
years. Following the death of its final owner, Albert Lange, the
building was sold and dismantled. During its existence this building
was the center for many civic and social events, the upper story
serving the community for lodge meetings, dances, movies, Iyceum
programs, local community meetings, stage shows, church services, and
Sunday schools. This building was located just across the street, south
of Bauer & Sons, on the west side of Main street.

The Spiry Brothers, Philip and Fred, erected a 26x50 structure which
housed their machinery business in the year 1900. They carried line of
general farm machinery, buggies, and stated that no farmer need let his
crops go to waste as they handled the type of machinery needed for
trouble-free service. This business continued until January, 1905, when
the Huether Brothers, Henry and Christian, purchased the premises. The
Spiry brothers had the distinction of being the first farm machinery
business in the new town.

By early 1901, several other business houses and services had begun.
John Banik opened and operated Java's first blacksmith shop; later they
added a line of farm machinery, buggies, pumps, and windmills. In 1904,
Simon Bitzer became a partner, and a wagon shop was added. John Erbe
became the town's earliest dealer in lumber and coal, having on hand a
fine stock of quality merchandise. H. A. Taylor, Java's first hotel
man, moved a building into Java, remodeled it, and served the needs of
the early community. It should be noted that the building Mr. Taylor
moved into town for his hotel came from Campbell county, being moved
entirely with horses providing the motive power and with considerable
difficulty for all concerned. This building had been situated in the
area adjacent to what was known as "Feinstein Lake" and was a major
stop along the "Ipswich Trail", the route used by many of the early
settlers in the 1880's. Mr. Taylor sold the hotel property in May,
1901, to his brother-in-law, ex-commissioner H. P. Hall. Mr. Hall
operated "The Java House" in conjunction with a feed barn and stable
until January, 1904, when Fred Diegel purchased the property. This
hotel was located on the east side of Main street, opposite from
Hofmeyer's Store.

        Main Street - Early Day Java

Phillip Flemmer had the distinction of being the town's first drayman,
also early in 1901; he operated a large scale business, hauling
supplies as needed in the bustling new town. Blinderman Brothers
advertised for business before having a suitable structure in which to
operate, erecting their business building in February of that year.
They dealt in groceries, general merchandise, and scrap iron.

Lakeman, Cook & Company had begun erecting a building in the fall of
1900. The walls had been set up when a severe winter storm tore down
all that had been done; this forced them to begin anew, and it was not
until about mid-March of 1901 that this establishment was opened for
business. Arrangements were made to hold Java's first masquerade ball
there on the evening of March 22, 1901. Music was supplied by a
two-piece band, one a Mr. Jarvis, a Bowdle fiddler. Business was brisk
at Lakeman, Cook, and Company, often taking in from $250 to $300 cash
in a single day as more and more settlers moved into the area. After
six months, an addition was built on the north side of the original
store to house a farm machinery section. Later, another addition was
erected on the south side of the original building; this addition
housed a full line of furniture,--and soon this store was selling
everything from needles to a threshing machine,--with a full stock
carried at all times. For several years, from five to seven clerks were
employed to handle the business. In 1910, the elder Lakeman retired,
and the firm was known as Cook and Lakeman. In 1911, Mr. Cook conducted
the business; sometime later the store was forced to close for lack of
patronage. While Dirk Lakeman Sr., served as Java's second postmaster,
the postoffice served its patrons from this store. Located across the
street to the north of The Java House, this building also housed
Sacketts Drug Store, the John H. Wolf Garage, Spiry & Sons Garage,
Ole's Electric in later years,--and presently the remaining portion is
owned by the Walworth County Highway Department.

        Lakeman, Cook, & Co., Furniture, Hardware, Groceries, etc.

        Lakeman & Cook later became John H. Wolf Garage - the north part
        of the building held a bowling alley.

Java's first meat market was opened and operated by John (Jack) Feeney
sometime in 1901 and remained in business until 1904 when the property
was sold to Andrew Naasz, who opened a saloon, the building was commonly
known then as the Naasz building.

Dr. Sigmund Rosenthal came to Java from Evarts and became the first
doctor to serve Java and community. He operated the Java Medical Drug
Store in a small building where the Java Equity Exchange Lumber Yard
now stands; he moved to other quarters at a later date. Dr. Rosenthal
remained in Java for a lengthy time, serving well in his chosen
profession.

Another new business opened in May, 1901, a law and real estate office
housed in a small building erected by Senator LeClair and his partner,
a Mr. Ham. Its location is uncertain.

The inevitable saloon soon made its appearance. It is generally
conceded that August Martell of McPherson county, opened the first such
establishment on the west side of Main street, north of Bauer, Hoff and
Stoller. In 1904, Doerr Brothers purchased the business, and Mr. Martell
engaged for a time as a cattle buyer; later he returned to McPherson
county, assuming a business there. August Martell was a colorful
figure, a veteran of the Spanish-American War, an intimate friend of
President Theodore Roosevelt, serving, according to reports, as
Roosevelt's personal messenger, being with Roosevelt during the Rough
Riders' savage and bloody assault up San Juan Hill.

During the spring of 1901, a construction crew came to Java to erect a
depot to provide service to patrons of the railroad. They commenced
work about mid-May, completing the structure approximately July 15. In
service for the Chicago, Milwaukee, and St. Paul Railway at the depot
were F. A. Miller, general passenger agent, and A. D. Mashek, regular
station agent. This depot continued in service for many years; a number
of years ago this service was discontinued. After being vacant for a
period of time, the building was purchased by the Java Equity Exchange,
remodeled, and now serves as a warehouse.

        Depot and Elevators in Early Day Java

At about the same time grain elevators were being erected along the
course of the railway. These were the Bauer, Hoff & Stoller Elevator,
the Bagley Elevator Company, the Crown Elevator, and the Potter,
Garrick and Potter Elevator. Prior to the establishment of banking
facilities in Java, Bauer, Hoff, and Stoller paid their own grain
checks at their store, while the Bagley Elevator Company and Crown
Elevator made arrangements with John Hofmeyer to pay their grain
tickets. In time, the Bauer, Hoff, and Stoller Elevator was moved to a
point east of the Potter, Garrick and Potter Elevator being operated by
that firm. McCabe Brothers acquired this combined facility and
continued in business for many Years. Then it was sold to the Java
Equity Exchange finally purchased the business, now being a part of the
latter firm. The Crown Elevator was purchased by the Java Equity
Exchange, being used until just a few years ago when it was dismantled
and a larger and more modern facility constructed at the site. A smaller
building located west of the elevators, which was used for a storage
and cooling warehouse for beer, was purchased by Carl Jacobs. In
preparation for moving, this building was loaded upon moving dollies,
however, before it could be moved, tornadic winds toppled the structure
and reduced it to debris. The Bagley Elevator was consumed by fire about
twenty years ago. Other grain-handling firms were Hunt & Son Elevator
and the Java Grain Company, which for a time operated in the Potter,
Garrick and Potter Elevator.

        Blinderman & Naasz Bros. Java Cash Bazaar - note the board 
        sidewalks!

William P. Winkley, another of Java's early-day businessmen, conducted
a grocery store in his building located on the west side of Main street
on Lot 1, Block 3. This building later housed businesses of Blinderman
Brothers, L. A. Gershin, the Gershin Brothers, E. R. Kary Grocery, the G
& L Store, operated by Mr. and Mrs. Gilbert Schlepp, all being grocery
concerns. After setting vacant for some time, it is now a part of the
Java Equity Complex and used as a warehouse.

Early in March of 1901, the Central Lumber Company was preparing to
begin a lumber yard at Java, with Henry Thielen in charge. Mr. Thielen
continued his general mercantile interests, later selling to Bauer,
Hoff and Stoller.

The town still lacked a newspaper and a bank, two necessary businesses
to make a live town. Finally, in 1902, the Java State Bank was begun
with C. Buechler, president; B. Buechler, vice president; and C.
Buechler, cashier. This bank failed and went into receivership,
becoming defunct in November of 1903. The building, housing this bank,
was located just south of the Hofmeyer Store on the west side of Main
Street. Immediately after the collapse of the Java State Bank, another
banking institution was started in the same building, doing business as
the First State Bank of Java, with E. G. Hicks, president; F. B. Pease,
vice president; and J. E. Carroll, cashier. By September 1,1904, Ward
McCafferty was in charge of the bank with E. G. Hicks, president; John
Hofmeyer, vice president; and W. C. Hicks, cashier. The bank continued
operation until about 1932. The building has been used since as a
residence, and later as a barber shop, and presently Emil Boehler's
Barber Shop.

It was in September, 1903, when Java was blessed with its own
newspaper. Carl F. Clement, who had been employed by "The Frei Press"
at Eureka, in partnership with George P. Vick, purchased the printing
equipment of the Hoffman Brothers at Bangor. Moving the heavy equipment
with horse and wagon proved quite a task; however, on September 10,
1903, the first edition of the combined Java Herald and Rising Sun of
Bangor became a reality. The Herald is still being published by Fritz
Clement, a son of the founder.

The town now had grown and was in the midst of an expansion boom
which lasted for several years. Other businesses had been started in
the town. These included the following: Schlaht Brothers in 1902,
saloon and pool room; Mississippi Lumber Company, a Mr. Wheelock as
business manager in September, 1903, and Kurt Eisentraut, manager in
October; Jacob Schaffer, jewelry, watches and watch repair; C. F.
Clement, German lawyer; P. H. Merkel, blacksmith, located where Anton
Weisgram later operated a blacksmith shop; Gottfried F. Fiedler,
operated a harness and saddlery shop, the Blinderman-Wolff Company and
Wolff and Freimark, Merchants; Gebr. Hermann, livery barn; Krause
Brothers, general merchandise and agents for the Aberdeen Creamery
Company; Sonnenfeld & Permann, later Sonnenfeld & Bauder, buggies,
machinery; Gross Brothers of Java, Selby, and Bowdle, John Deere buggies
and phaetons.

        Krause Busy Bee Store - in later years became Job's Cafe and the
        first City Liquor Store.

In 1904, other businesses continued to appear: H. C. Buechler sold
organs and pianos; Blinderman Brothers were agents for the Red Wing
Butter and Egg Company of Milbank; Goehring & Hindemith operated a
livery; Herman Lentz offered his services as an auctioneer; Kate P.
Clement rendered service as a notary public; Frederick Krause operated
a photo gallery, featuring Cabinet Photos at the Krause Brothers
Mercantile; John Jahnle operated a meat market, selling to John Lutz in
April, and Lutz selling to W. P. Winkley. T :mil Mueller offered the
shoemaker trade; Java Land Company and Insurance Agency operated by
Vick and Clement; Ed Rose operated a barber shop in the Winkley
Building; Mrs. H. P. Hall had a millinery business; Dr. Goodmanson
served as a part time dentist; Diegel Brothers worked a livery barn; L.
J. Muentz operated a lunch and fruit counter in the Andrew Naasz
Building; J. S. Hart Lumber Company purchased the Mississippi Lumber
Company; Henry Huether opened a meat market and bought hides and furs;
Michael Goehring and Fred Hindemith had purchased the Hall Livery &
Feed Barn; Riede & Noble operated the first licensed drayline after the
organization of the town of Java; they, in turn, sold the business to
Fred Diegel, Jr.

The year 1905 brought other new business ventures. George Raba had a
shoemaking establishment; George Flashman had been operating a bowling
alley during the winter of 1904-05 on the lots of The Java Herald,
moving to Bowdle at a later date; A. I. O'Reilly sold insurance for St.
Paul Fire & Marine; John Ehret made and repaired shoes; Henry Herman
became manager for the Aberdeen Creamery Company; John C. Seeley
followed the barber trade; Siegele & Bamesberger sought carpenter work;
August Martell bought livestock under the firm name of The Martell Com-
pany; Rose & Witz offered services as carpenters; the Java Cigar
Factory began production on November 13, 1905, with its brand name,
"Java Product".

In 1906, W. C. Hicks became agent for the St. Paul Fire and Marine
Insurance Company; A. G. Dunham sought employment as a mason and
plasterer; Naasz & Rosin were engaged in buying livestock; Daniel Riede
operated a shoe repair and harness shop in the Fiedler Building, moving
later to the Gross Building; Jacob Fiechtner built and operated the
Commercial Hotel, north of Schlaht Brothers, holding a grand opening on
September 15; Dr. W. C. Boteler specialized in ailments of the eyes,
ears, nose, and throat; Philip Spiry, Fred Spiry, and John Bieber
incorporated the German State Bank, which was located several doors
north of Bauer, Hoff & Stoller; the Java Cigar Factory reopened in the
building next to Heuther Brothers warehouse, producing a brand called
"The Java Band," A. D. Mashek, owner George Hedrick operated the City
Livery and Transfer line, purchased the barber shop of A. E. Rose,
which he operated for a short time, again disposing of the barber
business to A. E. Rose, at which time he accepted a position as an agent
selling Buffalo-Pitts threshing machines; F. A. Miller, General
Passenger Agent, Chicago, Milwaukee, & St. Paul Railway; Elnathan B.
Adams was practicing as an attorney at law in his office located south
of the Java House.

Other businesses appearing in the year 1907 were these: George E.
Hedrick operated an establishment called the New Butcher Shop; Anton
and Rochus Aman were working the drayline, formerly owned by Fred
Diegel Jr.; Gottlieb Knodel was buying cream; Jacob Merkel established
himself as a blacksmith; W. P. Winkley bought hides, wool and tallow at
a location between the engine house and Banik's Shop; Stuart & Wischman
offered their services as painters and paper hangers; Martin Gehrki
managed a warehouse for the Montevideo Mill Company; Holandsworth-Hart
Lumber Company advertised their materials and supplies; Victor O. and
Merle Corson opened a new butcher shop; P. S. Jensen was a traveling
salesman operating in Java for the Union Savings Association; Perman
and Lutz were proprietors of a saloon; Laura Clement advertised for
music lessons; J. M. Stewart was engaged in the furrier's trade to the
rear of Frank Dyer's feed mill; R. F. Hutchinson offered his services
as a plasterer and cement work; John Eisenbeisz worked a drayline; Fred
Krause operated The Busy Bee Store; Blinderman and Gershin opened The
New Store; Pete Frazier worked the drayline which he purchased from the
Aman Brothers; Ed J. Mashek opened a new butcher shop; Henry Heuther
served as agent for the M. E. Gehon Creamery; Wolf, Himmerich and
Knodel operated a store.

        Early Java Cream Station - 1909

The year 1908 saw the opening of the Java Opera House, featuring
Knight's Moving Pictures; H. W. King followed the barber trade; E. A.
Witz worked a drayline; Hindemith and Bauder advertised as contractors
and builders; Victor O. Corson sought employment as a painter and paper
hanger; the Mashek Brothers re-opened the Java Cigar Factory, offering
two brands of cigars, "The Java Product" and "Country Trade"; R. E.
Sullivan opened a new butcher shop; George Riede offered his services
as a mason and plasterer; Blinderman and Naasz Brothers operated a
store; John C. Schlepp specialized in photography; the Central Hotel,
formerly the Taylor and Hall Hotel, was under the proprietorship of
Samuel Anderson, with A. E. Rose assuming the business later that year;
George and John Zeller purchased the Heuther Brothers implement
business, operating under the firm name of Zeller Brothers Implement;
Pete R. Schenkenberger purchased the Sullivan Butcher Shop, doing
business as The City Meat Market; Schafer Brothers opened a new harness
shop; L. H. Heuther opened a butcher shop; W. L. Sackett and H. E.
Yaukey were proprietors of the Java Pharmacy; John Kies repaired shoes;
Schott and Nyman opened an implement shop.

At about this time in the business history of the town, the building
boom began to subside and business settled into a more stable pace. The
period between the years 1903 and 1908 indicates the time when greatest
expansion occurred. An example may be derived from the fact that the
town's assessed valuation during the year of 1903 amounted to $45,800.
Approximately a year later the amount of valuation had increased to
$85,000, indeed a most creditable showing for the young town. Java was
already becoming a mecca for traveling salesmen as early as 1904. They
stated that more merchandise was being bought and sold in Java than in
any other place of comparable size within the state of South Dakota.
Great building activity occurred during 1905, especially during the
summer when the town's lumber dealers were open in excess of 18 hours
daily, attempting to meet the demand. Building contractors were equally
extending their efforts, one particular foreman and crew having
erected a residence to the stage when plasterers were called in, in the
elapsed time of four days.

Additional businesses during 1909 were as follows: a new furniture
store, the W.P.S. Furniture Store under the proprietorship of William
P. Stoddard; G. C. Pilkington, practicing veterinarian, located first
in the Java Pharmacy, later having his own office; two implement shops
were operating under the firm name of Dakota Implement Company and The
Java Implement Company; F. H. Pratt and W. M. Hott were renting the
pool hall from John Schlaht, known as The Java Billiard Hall; Walter H.
Rhodes succeeded A. E. Rose as barber; Mr. and Mrs. S. B. Click were
the new owners of the Central Hotel; John G. Horstink took over
publication of the town's newspaper, renaming it The Java Reporter;
Charles Schott managed the Java branch of the Selby Implement Company;
Dr. A. J. Kraft practiced his profession in the John Fischer residence.

In the year 1910, William P. Stoddard served as embalmer and funeral
director in the Himmerich Store; The Real Estate & Investment Company
with E. B. Adams, president, Theo. Wosnuk, vice president, A. F.
Wosnuk, secretary; Henry Buckenberger operated The Java Meat Market; R.
M. Hennings conducted The Java Pharmacy; Rochus Aman worked the
drayline; Christ Schnaible purchased the Dyer Feed Mills.

        Harness shop in east Main Street in the early years of Java.  
        Operated by Emanuel Schaefer (on sewing machine).  He was
        married to Karoline Fiechtner, the next older sister of Mrs.
        Louise Stangl.

Among the newcomers in 1911 were Baer and Knodel, engaged in real
estate; The Java Equity Union was organized with Ernest Noteboom as
president; E. P. Brandt, vice president; Axel E. Borg,
secretary-treasurer; Jacob J. Wolff managed the Central Lumber Company;
Emanual Baer became representative for the American Bankers Insurance
Company; Wm. F. Ewald managed the George C. Bagley Elevator; I. R.
Krause handled jewelry and serviced clocks and watches; Helm and
Pfitzer purchased livestock; August C. Schlepp assumed the barbering
business and also operated a billiard and pool hall; Adolph Schaffer
operated the Java Meat Market; William Curran purchased Hofmeyer's
Store very late in that year.

The year 1912 saw George Zeller purchase the Central Hotel; this hotel
was operated by his two daughters, Mrs. George (Lena Zeller) Wolff and
Mrs. C. F. (Rosie) Spiry; Jacob J. Wolff bought out the P. H. Merkel
blacksmith shop; George Wolf purchased the livery barn from Naasz & G.
Fiechtner; Philip Spiry purchased the butcher shop from Adolph
Schaffer; Christ Helm purchased the J. H. Wolf Saloon; Albert Lange
assumed ownership of William Curran's Store; H. F. Crippes managed the
Central Lumber Company; John Blinderman was operating a general store.

        Mr. and Mrs. Carl Hepper in their meat market, about 1915.

In 1913, H. C. Cook was in the grocery business; Pfitzer, Helm &
Schlaht were buying livestock; and the Standard Oil Company was begun;
E. E. Lehman was managing the Atlas Lumber Company.

New to the scene in 1914, Helm & Schlaht were operating a saloon; John
Horner, J. Bosch, and Carl Doerr conducted a bowling alley; A. C.
Schlepp advertised as a portrait artist; Anton Weisgram was in the
blacksmith business; Carl Hepper operated the Java Meat Market.

In the year 1915 came Besanson's Restaurant; Jesser Brothers, General
Merchandise; John Banik operated the Yellowstone Garage; William Kunz
purchased cream; S. Ferche was engaged in photography; Horner and
Schnaible operated the Java Cash Store in the former Krause Mer-
cantile Building.

The year 1916 saw Mrs. M. M. Minton operating the Java Cafe; Mr. and
Mrs. W. E. Campbell owned the Central Hotel; Hicks & Helm were in the
garage business; and during the year a movie theater was opened.

Business ventures in 1917 were as follows: John Horner's Pool Hall and
Soft Drink Parlor in the John Schlaht Building; Oscar S. Larsons'
Electrical Shop, Gershin Brothers bought out H. C. Cook; Millard Aasved
sold wines and liquors; Henry Buckenberger operated a harness hop, Mr.
and Mrs. G. H. Habe purchased the Central Hotel; Java Grain Company
was formed, formerly Potter, Garrick & Potter Elevator.

During the year of 1918, Christ Naasz was in charge of a refreshment
parlor; George Schott managed the Atlas Lumber Company; Spiry & Sons
purchased the Wolf Garage; and Louis Freedman opened a vulcanizing
works two doors north of the Herald office.

        Carl Schlepp and Ed Gill sold Buicks at the Java Auto Garage

The year 1917 had B. H. Symanowski buying cream, Wolf & Knodel worked a
drayline, selling to Falkenberg and Haller later that year; John Haller
operated the City Meat Market, selling to Gottlieb Schock Jr., in
October; Carl Hepper purchased the pool hall from John Schlaht; Carl W.
Clement worked at the electrician's trade; Carl Schlepp Jr., and Ed
Gill purchased the Hicks & Helm Garage, doing business as the Java Auto
Garage; Christ Bader sold Case tractors and Dodge automobiles, the Java
Cooperative Creamery Association was formed, John Banik served as agent
for LaCrosse tractors Eisenbeisz & Pfitzer operated a billiard parlor;
T. A. Koipacoff sought employment as painter and decorator.; Aaron
Bodschatzky sold dry goods: Arie W. Cook & D. M. Paul owned and
operated the Java Battery Shop; Clyde R. Stuart sold wallpaper,
varnishes, enamels and stains; I. R. Krause took orders for tailored
clothing; Carl W. Clement was proprietor of the Clement Electrical
Company.

Changes during 1921 included these: L. L. Helm owned the Java Oil
Company, formerly Hicks & Helm; Charles Schlepp owned the Java Auto
Garage; Edward Gill had a battery shop next to the Java Auto Garage;
George H. Jackson sold auto insurance at the Java Auto Garage; the
Misses Cook and Dufloth operated The Waffle Kitchen, also specializing
in cakes and pastries; Julius Pfitzer was engaged in the barber's
trade; Dr. D. L. McCaw, a dentist specialist; Mae M. Campbell operated
the Campbell Hotel.

George W. Hicks, attorney at law, appeared to be the only newcomer in
the year 1922.

In 1923, Daniel Blinderman opened the Java New Store; Mrs. J. E. Spiry
(Bertha Aman) and Bertha Spiry (Mrs. Arthur Hay ) operated a new
restaurant known as the Bluebird Inn; Wolff & Horst worked a drayline;
The Electric Shop operated as a subsidiary of The Java Herald at their
office.

The year 1924 had Ben Bender managing the Turner Creamery Company; Mr.
and Mrs. E. G. Carlson operated the Java Cafe.

During 1925, Ella J. Taylor owned a millinery business in the Schnaible
Building; Arend E. Lakeman purchased the Java Cafe.

Other businesses to follow were the M. K. T. Store, managed by Philip
W. Quashnick, featuring clothing and dry goods; Ben Stiegelmeier and
Christ Bader operated on Auto Sales & Service; A. & P. Clothing
Company, affiliated with Standard Clothing of Aberdeen, bought out
Daniel Blinderman; Perman Implement Company sold Oakland and Pontiac
autos; John Schlaht managed the Farmers Cream & Produce Company; Edward
Schafer was in the Sterling Oil Company at the south end of Main
street, later managed by John Lutz & Edward Hochstetter; this building
later housed a Cities Service Station, managed by Clifford Silverson and
now is owned by the Java School District; Carl Hepper operated a
grocery store and meat market known as Hepper's Market; Adam Lorenz
was the shoe repair man at about this time and repaired shoes and sold
a varied line of shoes and boots.

        Adam Lorenz Shoe Shop - Fred Walther, Anna Marie and Adam Lorenz.
        Taken in 1934.

        Pfitzer and Eisenbeisz Pool Halt - that's Julius Pfitzer behind
        the bar.

Of Java's business places when the town celebrated its 50th Golden
Jubilee, Bauer & Sons was one of the oldest businesses which was
operated by the Bauers: Fred, John and his sister, Evelyn. After their
death, it was vacant for a time and in 1960 it was sold. Chris Pfitzer
purchased it and operated a cream station and also sold veterinary
supplies, but later discontinued this practice and restocked his shelves
with paint. Also part of the building was remodeled as a beauty salon,
which was operated by Dee Fiedler.

Two other buildings north of Bauers, were a saloon and the American
Bank building, both were destroyed by fire. A new building has been
erected which is presently the Java Municipal Liquor Lounge, with
Christ Naasz, Jake Schlepp, Carl Hepper and Butch Wolf as previous
managers and is now being managed by Larry Goetz. The German State
Bank, operated by the Philip Spiry family was changed over to the
American State Bank. The American State Bank building was replaced as
Hicks Exchange and Insurance was operated by C. L. Hicks for many
Years, until his death. The business was purchased by Art Eisenbeisz
and presently is Eisenbeisz Exchange & Insurance.

Pfitzer & Eisenbeisz Pool Hall was formerly a saloon and pool hall
which were previously operated by Naasz, Lutz, Martell, Doerr and
Schlaht. This was later owned and operated by Eisenbeisz and Son until
1965. It was sold and used as a Recreation Hall operated by Elmer Wolf.
A short time later it was closed.

        Combining time - around 1950. McCabe, Java Equity and Bagley
        Elevators.

The buildings occupied by the previous Municipal Liquor and the Job
Cafe were the former Schnaible and Krause Mercantile Store. In 1942,
Minnie and Tillie Job started operating the Job Cafe until in the
1960'S when they purchased the former Lakeman Cafe. Both buildings
have been dismantled after their vacancies.

Another former building which was previously Baer Hardware, was then
sold to Hepper & Sons for a grocery and Meat Market. Years later E. R.
Kary took over ownership and after World War II Elmer Biel purchased
the building and fixtures and later managed it as Elmer's Red Owl. In
1959, Marvin Pfitzer took over ownership and managed it as the Java Red
Owl. Bob Pfitzer is presently owner and operator of the Java Red Owl.

Christ Wolf, Christ Himmrich and Gott. Knodel were former owners of the
furniture building located north of the Red Owl Store. It was also
known as the Lakeman Cafe and later Gills Hardware until Ervin Wolf
became owner and operated as Wolf Hardware. After Wolf sold out, it was
purchased by Tillie Job and operated as Job's Cafe until 1969 when
Claudia Pfaff became owner and operated a cafe for two years. At the
present time the building remains vacant.

A small building, next to the Lakeman Cafe, had served as a cream
station for owners such as John Schlaht, John Dufloth, Joe Bosch, Art
Freimark, Art Schumacher and Chris Pfitzer. This building was later
destroyed by fire.

Another one of Java's oldest businesses was the Winkley building, later
taken over by D. Blinderman. Other businesses housed in the same
building were: Gershin Mercantile. Kary's Furniture, Speidel
Recreation, Hirsch, Oster, and Neumiller Recreation. In 1953, E. E.
Schlepp took over ownership and operated as Schlepp's Cafe until 1959
when a nephew, Elmer Schlepp became owner. In 1963, extensive
remodeling was done and as of this date is still operated by Elmer and
his wife.

The building next to Schlepp's Cafe was also the former David
Blinderman building, which in turn was sold to H. J. Cook, and under
the operation as H. J. Mercantile. In 1917, L. A. Gershin also became
owner of the business, making vast improvements and operated it as
Gershin General Store until Jan. 1, 1943, when the store was closed. In
1944, E. R. Kary purchased the vacant building and started a grocery
store, which he operated for many years. Gilbert Schlepp took over
ownership and operated it as G. & L. Grocery until 1964 when it was
closed. It is presently owned by the Java Equity and used as a storage
complex.

On the next block, south of the former Bauer store was the site of
Langes Mercantile Store, which has been dismantled and is now a vacant
lot. Next to the lot, still remains the first State Bank building,
which now houses Emil Boehler Barber Shop. The next structure south was
known as the Telephone building, which housed the telephone switchboard
in the early days of Java. In 1972 it was dismantled.

A large building known as the Hicks and Helm garage was later known as
the Charlie Schlepp Garage. Many years later Hepper & Son became owners
and also had a Ford sales franchise. The building has been dismantled.

A building south of the Garage was known as The Java Printing Office,
which was later moved to Selby.

The former E. G. Heerwald building still remains. It served previously
as a Millinery and Hairdress Parlor, operated by Ida Spiry and Anne
Banik. In 1935, Lydia Wolf (Shaver) operated it as a Beauty Salon.
Presently it is being occupied as the John Schlepp residence. Other
Beauty Shops being operated from their homes were: Emma Stiegelmeier
Marge Mueller and Viola Schadler.

Across the Main street, east of the John Schlepp residence is the Otto
Schnaible residence, which had served as a dress shop in the early days
of Java. In later years, it became a Doctors Office.

The building north of the Otto Schnaible residence was the site of the
Old Fire Hall. Years later it served as the Java City Hall until 1941
when a new City Hall was built. Meetings of the Java Legion were held
in this building until a new structure was erected. Fred Permann
purchased the building and operated as Permann Plumbing & Heating. In
1965 Wilbur Kary purchased his inventory and moved it to his farm home,
which is still in operation.

Adam Lorenz Shoe & Harness Shop was north of the Old Fire Hall building
and was in operation for many years. Due to automation, Adam was forced
to close his shop in 1940. This building has also been dismantled.

North of the Lorenz Shoe & Harness shop was a large structure which was
the former Banik Garage, who in turn sold it to Spirys who operated it
as Spiry & Son Ford Garage. In 1945, Chris Pfitzer took over ownership
which he operated as a garage and machine shop for four years. After
being vacant for several years the building was dismantled.

The next building was known as the Library, which was operated by Mrs.
Noble. It now is owned as a residence by Mrs. Ella Buckenberger.

The Post Office building near the Buckenberger residence still remains
as a service to the Java Patrons. I. R. Krause carried out his
appointment as Postmaster for 22 years in this building. Julius Pfitzer
was the next appointee who served as Postmaster until his retirement.
Mrs. Agnes Jensen acted as Postmaster, and with the resignation of Mrs.
Lois Wolf, Mrs. LaVina Perman is currently acting Postmaster .

A small creamery was located north of the Post Office and was served by
operators such as F. C. Pietzke, Schell, Kate Spiry, Jake Nehlich, Fred
Krause and Brown. The building was dismantled in the early forties.

The old building on the corner north of the Post Office was known as
the Central Hotel, ,which was one of Java's first hotels owned and
operated by H. A. Taylor. Other businesses housed in the building were
a cafe, pool hall and a garage with other owners such as George Zeller
and John H. Wolf and Helm. Spirys re-located their garage and later it
was operated by Francis Jensen. It is now being used as storage for bee
hives. Across the alley remains another one of Java 's oldest buildings
which was known as the Blacksmith Shop. The shop was purchased by Anton
Weisgram in 1913 from J. H. Wolf and was enlarged two years later.
Within a few years, a brother, Fred, became co-owner and later Frank
took over complete management, which was operated as one of the best
Blacksmith Shops in the surrounding area. Upon Frank's death, it was
closed. The building still remains with all the contents.

        Ferdinand and Anton Weisgram in blacksmith shop.

The old structure on the corner across the street of the former Central
Hotel was the Cook and Lakeman establishment, which later served as the
Wolf Garage owned and operated by J. H. Wolf, who had the first Ford
Dealership in this area. J. C. Perman took over ownership and housed an
implement business until he moved to a new location east of the present
site. In 1945, Ole Naasz purchased the building and operated it as
Ole's Electric. It is now occupied by the Walworth County Highway
Department for storage. Across the alley to the east was a Livery
Stable, owned and operated by Diegel and Fiechtner, which was later
sold to George S. Wolf. Wolf merged with Adolph Pfaff Drayline and
after the Livery Stable was discontinued in the later twenties, Pfaff
remained as owner of the Drayline, until he sold it to Adolph J. Horst
in 1946. The Livery Stable was dismantled and presently the city pump
house is located on the lot. Perman Implement was also located on the
next lot.

North of the County Highway Department building was a large structure
erected by Louis Horst and Fred Dufloth. In 1920, this building was
operated as a movie hall. J. F. Perman took over ownership and
continued operating it for movies and other social functions. J. L.
Fetzer and son became owners in 1937 and became known as the Grand
Theatre. Others owners were: Ed Perman, Ben Stiegelmeier, Herman
Fiedler and in 1947 the Frank brothers, Art and Wes became owners and
continued to operate until 1957 when it was closed. It was later
dismantled and the lumber was used for the new Legion Hall, which was
built on the adjoining lot.

The former Carl Schlepp building was purchased by his son A. C. Schlepp
which he operated as a Poolhall, Bowling Alley and Barber Shop. The
Bowling Alley was discontinued and was later known as A. C. Schlepp
Drug and Barber Shop. In 1958 the Store was closed and after many years
of vacancy, it was dismantled.

The Schock and Spiry Meat Market north of the Drug Store was dismantled
and the lot was purchased by Adolph Schuh, who erected a new building
in 1949. It is presently owned and operated as Adolph's Repair Shop.

The former Campbell Hotel, next to the Meat Market served as a girls
dormitory in the late thirties and early forties. The building was sold
and moved to Leola.

The late Gottlieb Zeller residence served as a dentist's office in the
early years of Java, and is now the Paul Raba residence .

Several new businesses have sprung up in this small community of Java,
which are: Schmierer Garage established in 1957 on the west end of
Java. Business was expanded in 1958 and is still being operated by
Rueben Schmierer. Jensen Excavating was established in 1958 with five
pieces of equipment. Presently, he operates from his home in Java.
Oscar Carlson established his Blacksmith and Electric Shop in 1961,
which he operates from his farm south of Java. Jerry Goetz repair was
established in 1969 and is located west of Java on Highway 103. Christ
Hochstatter & Son established an Antique Shop, which was located near
their home. It was operated until 1973 when the son sold out and moved
to Washington. Mike Kary Salvage, located northeast of the railroad
track, is still being operated. Hazel Zeller established her business
The Flower Garden in 1970. Her sales include many places all over the
world. Vivian Witlock established her Beauty Salon in 1973 with Susan
Goetz as assistant operator. The site of her Salon is west of Main
street in her home.

The remaining businesses and residents of Java have strived to keep
this community alive.

                JAVA TELEPHONE OFFICE

The Java telephone line moved into the area about 1899 as Dakota
Central. In 1900 they moved the office into town, and was completely in
operation by 1915, and converted to all relay dial in 1940. Mrs. Erna
Hochstetter Werre started as an operator in Java on March 18, 1936. On
March 25, 1939, she was Agency Chief operator in Java and worked for
two weeks as an operator in Selby, where she had been transferred. From
Sept. 15, 1940 to Oct. 8, 1947 she worked as an operator in Eureka, and
was transferred to Aberdeen as an operator on Oct. 10, 1948, where she
is still working.

        Early day Java telephone office. Myrtle (Ewald) McCoy at the
        switchboard; Bertha (Spiry) Hay, standing.

        Java Telephone Office in 1929. Kathryn Hochstetter (Mrs.
        Ed Gill) at the switchboard.

                SCHLEPP DRUG STORE

August C. Schlepp purchased the building which housed a recreation
parlor on the east side of Main street in the year 1910 and installed
barbershop fixtures, this business was operated by the family until
1927 when the fixtures of the recreation parlor were sold and replaced
with drug store fixtures. The drug store and barber shop were operated
until the year 1956 when the stock and fixtures were disposed of and
the business closed. The building was razed a few years later.

                FIRM OF SCHLAHT BROTHERS

Among the early-day businesses in the booming new town of Java was the
partnership known as Schlaht Brothers under the proprietorship of
Jacob, George and John Schlaht. 

First locating on Lot 9, Block 2, of the original town plat, the
brothers engaged Alex Heiser of Selby, their brother-in-law, to erect
a building in January of 1902. The business consisted of a saloon and
billiard hall, and the new establishment grew and prospered.

In 1904 brother George left the partnership to take up a homestead 9
miles east of Mound City, in Campbell county and the firm continued as
Schlaht Bros., with Jacob and John at the helm. Aside from the usual
line of refreshments, sundries and entertainment, an occasional bowery
dance was held.

As the volume of business increased it became apparent that a larger
and more modern building was necessary to house the business, and in
June of 1907, the pool room building was moved to the rear of the lot,
and a fine new two-story building was erected, being 24 by 30 feet with
16 foot posts. On July 4, 1907, a Grand Ball was held to dedicate the
new building, with a fine lunch served throughout the celebration.
Later in July of the same year, new fixtures were received, and by
year's end elegant new bar fixtures were installed and two new pool
tables were added; these improvements were adjudged by many as being
the finest in this part of the state.

The saloon room and billiard room were so arranged that it was possible
to pass from one to the other via a walkway between the two. The upper
story of the new building contained rooms used as living quarters. In
later years this building housed another business familiar to many
Javaites, the drug store operated for many years by August C. Schlepp.

In July of 1908, the business was advertised for sale, the property
consisting at this time of two business houses, a fine new 8 room
residence, and two business lots and three residence lots, however, the
business continued under the Schlaht Bros. until January 23, 1909, when
by mutual consent, the partnership between Jacob Schlaht and John
Schlaht, under the firm name of Schlaht Brothers, was dissolved; John
retiring from the saloon business, with Jacob as successor of the firm.

Jacob Schlaht continued in business until February, 1910 when he sold
the saloon business to Jacob and Rudolf Weiszhaar, father and son, who
took possession on that date. John Schlaht, continued his residence in
Java for many years as a prominent businessman.

Jacob Schlaht, after disposing of his business interests, remained in
Java for a time, then removed to a farm 9 miles east of Mound City,
adjoining the farm of his brother George. The Jacob Schlahts resided on
the farm for many years, retiring first at Eureka, and later at
Aberdeen, where they lived out their remaining years.

                MONTANA-DAKOTA UTILITIES

Electricity got its start in Java in 1916, when a municipal light plant
was constructed after a bond issue. The electricity was turned on near
the end of September, 1916. In 1923, the plant was enlarged, and 24-hour
service provided. In 1925 the building was renovated, and another
larger electric generator was purchased.

In 1927 Emil "Shorty" Wolf went to work for the City of Java Power
Plant. The capacity of the plant at that time was as follows: Two 35kw
Fairbank-Morse generators. These generators were powered by one 50
H.P. and one 60 H.P. Diesel operated motors.

The history of production and distribution of man's greatest servant,
electricity, at Java and throughout South Dakota has been a very
colorful one. There are still those among us who recall when electric
service was provided for only several hours a day and only on certain
days of the week from small independently-owned generators. It is they
who marvel at the fact that this area is presently served by a
transmission and distribution system that is capable of accommodating
virtually any power demand thrust upon it. The next time you look at
that dependable electric clock or refrigerator that you know operates
24 hours a day it will help you appreciate how electricity got its
start in Java and this area many years ago.

In 1917 a Mr. Bert Hill, then superintendent of the Milwaukee Railroad
in Mobridge, struck out to give that city electric service with a
minimum of expense. To accomplish this purpose, he ran a steam line
from the boilers of the roundhouse to a generator located in a building
in the approximate location of the roundhouse to a generator located in
a building in the approximate location of the present Mobridge Power
Plant. A franchise was granted to Mr. Hill and Gus Smith and they chose
to call the newly-formed company the Mobridge Light, Power and Heating
Company.

In 1917 Ferris brothers purchased the existing franchise and built the
present power plant building in the city of Mobridge. Ferris brothers
changed the name of the company to The Mobridge Electric and built
their first transmission line from Mobridge to serve the communities of
Glenham and Selby. This new transmission line marked the beginning of a
central power source to serve many electric distribution systems soon
to be built in the northern part of South Dakota. With the advent of
this new central power source, residential and commercial consumers
enjoyed a reduction of electric rates from 16 cents to 15 cents per
kilowatt hour.

After the first world war, L. P. Runkel and G. A. Stephens acquired the
Mobridge based company and its property and reincorporated it as the
Northern Power and Light Company. This new firm expanded the business
rapidly until they finally served 58 communities in North and South
Dakota extending from Lemmon, South Dakota in the west to Ellendale,
N.D., in the east; and from Blunt, S.D., in the south to Steele, N.D.,
in the north. Altogether approximately 225 miles of transmission line
were used from east to west and 200 miles to connect the north and
south part of this property. Runkel and Stephens later sold their
interests to the United Public Service Company which held the property
for only a short time. In 1929 it was again sold to a new firm who
called themselves the Middlewest Utilities Company.

In the early 30's these same properties were purchased by the United
Public Utilities Corporation and placed under the management of the
Northwestern Public Service Company with general offices at Huron,
South Dakota. Under this last management the Northern Power and Light
Company was known as the Mobridge Division and served some 58 towns in
North and South Dakota. This entire area was divided into eight
district offices each serving approximately seven towns and involving
approximately 796 miles of transmission lines to accommodate almost
7-1/2 thousand customers. E. P. Harrison was Mobridge Division Manager
in the 30's and under his capable direction normal expansion continued
until 1939 when the company was consolidated with North Dakota Power
and Light Company forming the Dakota Public Service Company.

In 1941, with the completion of a new municipal building, the Java
power plant was updated and moved into the new building.

In 1945, Montana-Dakota Utilities Co. purchased the Dakota Public
Service Company.

M.D.U. Co. purchased the Java power plant and distribution system from
Java, in the year 1946. Mr. Wolf became a permanent employee of M.D.U.
Co. at this time and operated this same plant under M.D.U. Co., until
the shut down of the plant in the spring of 1950. In the fall of 1950
Mr. Wolf moved to Selby, S.D., and served the communities of Java,
Akaska and Selby for M.D.U. until his retirement in the year 1967. Upon
Mr. Wolf's retirement in 1967, Victor Baker moved into Selby and the
service area was expanded to include Glenham.

MDU elected to make Selby a district headquarters town in 1972 and
appointed M. L. "Radar" Bauer to assume the role of district manager.
The number of towns added to this newly-established district include:
Bowdle, Hosmer, Gettysburg, Hoven, Lebanon, Onaka, Seneca, Tolstoy,
Eureka, Artas, Greenway, Herreid, Pollock, Mound City, all in South
Dakota; and Zeeland in North Dakota.

The average cost of electricity in 1910 for a resident of the area was
almost 600 percent greater than the same amount of electricity used
today. Electric service has come a long way in the past 65 years.
Instead of a small generator the area is now served by two separate
41,600 volt transmission lines, one from the east and one from the west.

In the spring of 1972 construction began on a new district office
building in Selby. On August 1 of that same year, work was completed on
the building and an open house was conducted in the new office building
on October 27,1972.

                THE FLOWER GARDEN  
        Owned and operated by Mrs. John Zeller

Java's smallest business, The Flower Garden, started almost without
plan. In 1967 I sold a few glad bulbs out of state when North American
Gladiolus Council members ordered Gaytime, a fragrant gladiolus,
immediately after I had registered it. The sale of bulbs and perennials
continued on a strictly hobby basis until 1971 when I requested that my
garden be state inspected because of overseas orders I had been
receiving. Since the first inspection, I have maintained a state
licensed garden. Although it is still a small business, glad bulbs,
bulblets and-or glad seed have gone to England, New Zealand, Russia,
Canada and eighteen or twenty states, especially among the northern
third of the United States. In 1972, I took the name "The Flower
Garden" for I also sell cut flowers, perennials and unusual house
plants on a local basis. All of the stock I sell has been home grown.

                JAVA EQUITY EXCHANGE

The first Equity meeting held in Walworth county took place at Selby in
July of 1911 and was conducted by C. O. Drayton, who came here on a
return trip from the territory along the Milwaukee railroad west of the
Missouri river, where he had made a number of addresses and interested
many farmers. About a week after the Selby meeting a similar gathering
took place in Java at Lange's Hall, and sufficient interest was aroused
so that an organization was formed. This first setup as such
organizations are now called, was known as a Local Equity Union and the
number of the Java charter from the National Union was 9, which shows
that Java was a real pioneer in the cooperative movement. It was social
and educational and much like our community clubs of the present day.
At every meeting there was talk of cooperation and the day when farmers
could unite to buy and sell collectively and save the exorbitant
profits often taken by dealers.

On December 2, 1911, the Equity Union elected Arne Strand, G. W. Ryan,
H. C. Erbe, Martin Thorson and Thomas Murray directors, evidently with
the intention that a start should be made in business. These men met
and applied for a charter from the state which was granted March 1,
1912. However, nothing more was done until November when a group of
determined farmers met at the Erbe school house southeast of Java and
pledged a dozen shares of stock at $25.00 each in the new enterprise.
H. C. Erbe was elected president, and G. W. Ryan, secretary-treasurer.

The annual meeting was held on December 6, 1912 and A. R. Gordon became
president and A. E. Borg secretary-treasurer, with Fred Bieber, G. W.
Ryan and Ernest Noteboom as the other directors. Nothing further was
done until the summer of 1913 when twine, elevator site and similar
subjects were mentioned much in the minutes of the meetings. The Crown
Elevator was negotiated for in a meeting held June 28 and on July 5 the
contract was completed. The price was $3,500.00. The keys were received
from Henry Bauder and on July 21 the board hired A. E. Borg as manager.
It is interesting to note that notes for $1500.00 were part of the deal
for the elevator.

Late in 1913, William Kunz was hired to assist A. E. Borg. When the
implement business was purchased, Mr. Borg took over the management of
that while Mr. Kunz stayed in charge of the elevator. During his years
of managership, in addition to his elevator duties, he also handled and
shipped thousands of head of livestock. Mr. Kunz continued as manager
until 1945, when he retired after 32 years of faithful service .

        Many thousands of head of livestock were shipped from Java by
        Java Equity in years gone by.

The implement business was purchased from George Zeller, February 10,
1916. Again borrowed money was required and the autumn of that year the
elevator was mortgaged. In 1917 the main building of the implement
handling building burned down. Courageously the stockholders voted to
rebuild. In 1918 a new elevator was built to meet the needs of the
growing business. The implement business never proved to be a success
and was sold to J. F. Perman December 15, 1925.

In April of 1937 a special stockholders' meeting was held to discuss
the possibility of going into the gas and oil business. A vote taken
showed that the stockholders were in favor of this move, and the bulk
plant and service station belonging to J. F. Perman were purchased for
$4,500.00. A loan for this amount was secured from the Federal Land
Bank. The Consumers Cooperative Association, now Farmland Industries,
was given the oil and gas contract. This business prospered, and soon
the need for a new service station became apparent. This was built in
1946 at a cost of $25,000.00. In 1959 the bulk plant was also enlarged
with the purchase of the Cities Service Oil Co., at a cost of $3,500.00.
When Fred Helm retired as manager of the Central Lumber Co. Java was
left without a lumber yard as the company decided it was not feasible
to remain in the business here. In 1956 a representative of the company
offered to sell the building to the Equity at a cost of $1,200.00. The
old elevator was torn down in 1971 and replaced with a new and more
modern $82,000 facility with a 38,000 bushel capacity. A bulk
fertilizer and blender were added in 1974. Merlyn Melcher, the present
general manager, started on March 15, 1965.

        Wm. Kunz (right) with Henry Buckenberger in early Java Equity
        office.

        Java Equity Service Station, built in 1946.

        Aerial view of Java Equity Exchange - 1972.

JAVA CHURCHES

                JAVA UNITED CHURCH OF CHRIST

The true history of any church, regardless of whether or not it
originated as a member church of a denomination, is often difficult to
secure, unless it is written as the church is organized and developed
in the community.

This Java UCC Church was initially planned in the minds of individuals
who were concerned with a place to worship in a free way without
authority being imposed upon them by some higher echelon of the
denomination. Add to this the complications of time; for there has been
almost a century since the first German Congregational Churches were
organized. Further complications were the religious persecutions in
another land: what reports are available are largely in another
language and there seem to be few today who can read and translate the
beautiful script of the German language in which notes and minutes
are written. Then too, there is a lack of the records of many
congregations, the reports of these are found in the statistical
reports, but individual church reports are unknown. It is hoped that
some one some day will find even yet another book of minutes and
reports and will be careful to save them or hand them to some UCC
minister.

There are some written records of what was named the German
Congregational Churches, but even the collective records are very
scarce.

The first of the German pioneers had come to South Dakota by 1872 and
it was in 1881 that the first arrived in this area of the State. By the
next year there was an organization of German Congregational Churches
and in 1884 they had a full time Superintendent.

The Java UCC is a continuation of at least seven churches that at one
time or another were in the Java area. The exact location of each of
these churches is unknown to the writer, but their names are a matter
of record, with one exception.

In writing the religious history it is necessary to keep in mind the
political and civil conditions of the time. Most of the travel was by
horse power, occasionally there was an oxen in use. Until the turn of
the century or a little later there were no cars. Churches, towns,
political sub-divisions all needed to be close together; close enough
at least that the trip to and from could be made easily within one day
of travel. This is why there were so many churches, even among the
Congregational people.

"Herr Pastor" rather than driving to as many as six or seven churches
at different locations and very irregular services could with the
coming of the auto go to up to three places each Sunday and the people
could all come to these locations to hear the proclamation of the Word
of God. This lightened the load of the minister and also brought more
comfort and Christian training to the people.

Hoffnungsfeld Zwei German Congregational Church seemed to be the oldest
of the direct forerunners of Java UCC. It was started in 1889 following
services in homes. Seemingly it was never fully organized or became a
definite part of the German Congregational Conference. Later when
Bethesda was organized in 1896 most of the members 'who were nearer to'
left the HZGCC and became a part of Bethesda Congregational Church.
This church was located about one mile north of the farm residence of
Wilmer Boschee and perhaps a little east. There is still a grave yard
where the church used to be. The building was moved in 1929 or about
then.

Israel's German Congregational Church was organized in 1897. I have not
been able to locate exactly where the church stood. In 1927 when the
church was 30 years old there were 16 members and that year their
Mission offering for others amounted to over $13 per member.

Andreas German Congregational Church was organized in 1901. There is a
conflict as to where this church was located.

The Siloa Church had been in existence for a while but in 1901 it was
recognized by the German Conference and is listed in the records for a
few years and then is dropped. Both the Andreas and the Siloa Churches
had Java, S.D., as a postoffice address.

The Sutley Congregational Church was organized in 1930 and continued to
function until after the congregation moved to Java and they worshipped
in the Presbyterian building for a while. Later the former ALC building
was purchased and this has been the home since.

The German Congregational Church Conference was one of the several
uniting church bodies that in 1957 became the United Church of Christ.

It is impossible to list all the names of the ministers that served the
various churches named before, impossible for some are not known,
others there is a difference of the spelling of their names, and in a
few places there seems to be some question as to the veracity of the
records.

Ministers and the approximate date when they closed their ministry at
Java Congregational since 1930 are:

        Reverend Theodore Radach left fall of 1933
        Reverend Chris E. Maedche left late fall 1938
        Reverend Phillip Reich left summer 1941
        Reverend Elmer Bettenhausen summer 1943
        Reverend Jacob Nein left spring 1947
        Reverend S. H. Sprenger left summer 1950
        Mister T. R. Bader student summer 1951
        Reverend Jacob Rath left summer. 1956
        Reverend Raymond Schatz left 1962

Then there were several student pastors and a short pastorate again by
Reverend Nein.

In August, 1966, Reverend Douglas Barrows came and is still serving as
Pastor. This incidentally is the longest time served by any one
minister in the history of the church.

This is definitely not the place or time to tell of the doctrinal
beliefs of the congregation but it is necessary to mention some things
to show why the identity of the congregation has been kept all these
years.

        Java United Church of Christ

The pioneers were desirous of having a church in which they had a
definite voice, both in government and belief.

The church still enjoys the congregational form of government and all
matters in which the Java UCC is concerned are decided upon by the
local church. They enjoy a complete freedom of worship and acknowledge
only the Lordship of Jesus Christ.

The church is ready to reach out in love and concern to anyone who
might need its service in any way. The Lord's Supper is always 'open'
for any visitor to partake if they will confess to themselves their
faith in Jesus Christ as Lord. There is no creed or articles of faith
for any one to sign before becoming a member, for each one has the
freedom of individual belief before God.

There is a Statement of Faith of the United Church of Christ which the
church accepts and often reads aloud during service. At the present
time there is an active church school which meets each Sunday morning,
a women's organization which meets monthly' a confirmation class with
over 100 hours of class time is held each even numbered years.

Services of worship are held each Sunday, the hour of service changes
each four months.

Any information concerning any of these early churches will be
appreciated by the writer as I am also a member of the Historical
Committee of the South Dakota Conference of the United Church of Christ
and we are trying to get as complete a history of the development and
growth of the Congregational and United Church of Christ in South Dakota
as possible. Any old written records, printed programs, or any such
will be appreciated and preserved. Thank you.--
Rev. Douglas Barrows, Selby, S.D.

        Java Presbyterian Church

                PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH

On August 3, 1902, Sunday School Missionary, Isaac Perry called a
meeting at the schoolhouse for the interest of organizing a
Presbyterian Church. A few months later a Presbyterian Church was
organized by Rev. C. C. Todd Aberdeen, pastor at large, and assisted by
Dr. Carson and H. Grant. Part time services were being held for three
years by Rev. Todd and Rev. Fischer of Evarts. The first members
received into the church were: Mr. and Mrs. Boyland, Mrs. J. C. Corson,
Mrs. Consolver, Mrs. Grace Noble and Mrs. Laura Harris.

In 1905, a student pastor, J. B. Foster of Delaware, supplied the
church for the summer and later became resident pastor and served until
1908.

A lot and a cash donation of $1000.00 was received from another member,
Wm. H. Payne toward a church building and the wooden structure was
erected in 1905. The new church was dedicated on Dec. 29, 1907, free of
debt. The membership continued to grow and other members joined the
church; among them were Gerrit and Alice Noble. The late Mr. Noble was
elected and ordained as the first Elder of the church. Alice Noble was
a resident of the Good Samaritan Center of Selby, for a number of years
until her death in January of 1974. Rev. Oltmans served this
congregation for many years and later neighboring pastors conducted the
services. In 1930, the church combined for a time with the Reformed
Church, so as to be able to pay salary of a resident pastor. This
arrangement was later terminated.

Misfortune struck this little congregation when, in 1936, the church
burned to the ground. Due to the depression in the early thirties, when
many members left Java, it was not feasible to rebuild at this time.
Later, an unused building from Edmunds county was donated to the Java
Congregation by Aberdeen Presbystery. This was dismantled, moved and
placed on the old foundation. However, this building proved to be in
poor condition and a weed fire damaged it further. This compelled the
congregation and Sunday School to continue their services in the
basement but later the Seventh Day Advent Congregation shared their
church building.

The 50th Anniversary of the church was commemorated in September of
1952, with a dinner and special service which was attended by former
members. Hope was never given up by this congregation and with
sufficient funds, they undertook the project of rebuilding. A fine
modern church was erected with much of the credit for this achievement
belonging to Rev. Leslie B. Lewis, who was pastor from 1951 to 1954,
and gave so generously his advice, time and labor. Dedication services
were held on Nov. 29, 1953 with the Rev. Lewis in charge and Dr. Kann
of Aberdeen Presbyterian Church as main speaker.

Besides the Java Congregation, the Rev. Lewis also served the Roscoe
and Pollock churches. Later Rev. E. Freeman took over the pastorate and
served until 1959. After 1959, Rev. Cedar and Rev. Rosendaal alternated
their services for a few years until Rev. Rosendaal retired.

The membership dwindled with the younger people leaving the Java area
and others deceased, so with some reluctance the church had to be
closed. The Rev. Cedar, who served the Pollock Church for 25 years,
continued as supply pastor until February, 1969 when the Java Church was
disbanded by the Aberdeen-Huron Presbytery.

                LUTHERAN CHURCH OF JAVA

A number of small churches had sprung up in the northeast part of the
county which were as follows:

Hoffnungstahl which was built in 1887 by a membership of 22 families.
Pastor Fredrick Braun served this small congregation with the other
three parishes. It later was disbanded and became part of the St. Pauls
Lutheran Church of Java.

Friedens Lutheran Church was built in 1895 which was located 6 miles
northeast of Java. It had a membership of 18 families. This Parish was
also served by Pastor Fredrick Braun and later by Pastor G.
Kirchdoerfer.

Neuglueckstal Church was built in 1901 which was located 9 miles east
of Java. Many members from the Hosmer Parish started this church and
before too long the membership grew to 35 families. This church was
served by Pastor Attersburg from the Hosmer Parish.

The first St. Pauls Lutheran Church was a one-room wooden structure
built in 1897 on a hill near the Karl Schnaible farm. Most of the work
was done by charter members. In 1900, this congregation, together with
Hoffnungstahl and Friedens Church formed the Java Parish. Rev. G.
Schmidt served for a short time as he was called back to Germany.
Pastor Fredrick Braun took over and incorporated the Java Parish. When
the railroad was built and the city of Java formulated, it was decided
to move this little church to Java. In 1901 the church was moved to
town on four horse-drawn wagons and placed on a lot and later a
parsonage was built beside it. In 1902 Pastor Braun ended his ministry
and the vacancy was filled by Pastor Kirchdoerfer.

The Parish grew with abundance and several Missionary meetings had to
be held in the Blinderman-Wolf Hall. Perceptive plans were being made
to build a new church. The previous structure was moved to the back of
the lot and building started immediately. In 1909 the 28x60 church was
completed at a cost of $4800.00. The old building was used for a
schoolhouse. In 1932 they found it necessary to expand and placed a
basement under the church.

After 40 years of active ministry Pastor Kirchdoerfer retired July 8,
1942. Our vacancies were filled with Pastor Westenberger and Pastor
Haacke until 1957. Membership had grown tremendously which made it
possible for our congregation not to be vacant too long. Pastor A. H.
Schmitt filled our vacancy in 1958. Shortly after his arrival new
developments were made and plans incepted immediately for a new church.
One year from groundbreaking services, May 10 1959 to May 8, 1960 the
congregation was able to worship in the new church. Pastor Schmitt
remained with us until 1965. Membership was beginning to decrease as
families were moving and farmers were expanding. Pastor Parlow filled
our vacancy and served us for 2 years. Neighboring Pastors served the
congregation until Pastor Schulz filled our vacancy in 1968, and to
this date he is still serving us faithfully.

        St. Paul Lutheran Church 1909 - 1960

        Present St. Paul Lutheran Church

                CATHOLIC CHURCH

A committee of John Kormendy, George Kormendy, Ladislaus Kormendy,
Stephen Raba, Frank Hagadus, Gaza Kormendy, Mike Zanka and Ferdinand
Alexander were appointed to organize and choose lots for the purpose of
erecting a new Catholic Church.

In May, 1905, a building committee was selected and they purchased 2
lots south of the C. F. Clement residence. Cornerstone ceremonies were
held June 14, 1905, and building got underway shortly thereafter. There
were many volunteers who helped faithfully and a year later the new
wooden structure was completed. On Monday, May 14, 1906, the first mass
was celebrated in the new church with the Rev. Father Schell from
Herreid, officiating. Rev. Father Schell served this parish for four
years and it was steadily attended by the congregation. In 1910 Father
Krause became the priest for this area, including the Selby and Bowdle
Congregations.

A few years later the church was disbanded and the congregation joined
with the Selby Parish.

Years later the church was taken over by the Reformed Congregation and
much later the Eureka pastor held services for the Baptist congregation.

Many years later the church was purchased by John A. Gross of Bowdle,
and it was moved to Bowdle.

Early Sporting Groups

                BASEBALL

Java's baseball enthusiasts met on Thursday, April 13, 1905 and
organized a team with the following members: V. S. Varnum, J. C.
Seeley, I. A. O'Reilly, I. R. Krause, Chas. Fehrenbaker, Emil Mueller,
Henry Erbe, Wm. Jarvis and Wm. Ewald.

                BASEBALL TEAM OF 1909

The baseball team for the season of 1909 had the following players: Ed
Aman, John Schlepp, L. Huether, Oscar Peterson, I. R. Krause, Theo.
Meinz, V. Varnum, Fred Bauer, Aug. Schlepp and Henry Huether. Oscar
Peterson was the manager. The first game was played with Spring Lake,
and defeated them by a score of 8 to 6.

        Baseball Club of 1923 -- Back, left to right, Ed Kunz, Reinie
        Moser, George Goetz, Fritz Clement, George Hicks. Seated, John
        C. Wolff, Albert Hoven, Julius Pfitzer, Carl Hepper and Frank
        Manders. In front, Henry H. Wolff and Art Neanstead.

                JAVA GUN CLUB

The Java Gun Club of 1905 had 23 members. They practiced every Sunday
afternoon, each member having ten shots at each meeting. Whoever
scored the highest in the 60 shots, got a rifle. On Sunday of June 18,
1905, the Gun Club finished their sixty shots, showing that the
gunners were not all expert marksmen: John C. Seeley 43, Emanuel Baer,
34, G. F. Fiedler 34, John Hofmeyer 31, Daniel Riede 28, Christ Huether
23, Jacob Schafer 22, Christ Wolf 18, P. L. O'Reilly 18, Fred
Sonnenfeld 11, Ole Strand 9 and A. B. O'Reilly 4. The prize of a rifle
was captured by Mr. Seeley.

The Java Gun Club was again organized in the spring of 1921 with
Reinhold Baker as president. Others in the membership were Jake Helm,
Fred Helm, P. H. Spiry, C. F. Spiry and A. C. Schlepp.

        Baseball team of 1907 Left to right, Johnny and Henry Huether,
        C. F. Spiry, F. E. Bauer, A. C. Schlepp, Louie Huether, Ed Aman, 
        I. R. Krause, Peanuts and manager Frank Moore.

        The Java Bloomers - (standing, left to right, maiden names):
        Gertie Banik, Lorraine Schott, Anne Banik, Hulda Bauer, Bertha
        Spiry. Seated, Martha Bauer, John Spiry (catcher), Hertha Bauer
        and Pauline Schlepp.

                JAVA BLOOMERS

In May of 1909, a number of Java ladies organized a ladies ball team.
This newly organized team was known as the "Java Bloomers". On Sunday,
May 30, they played the "Heavy Weights", a team of nine 300 pounders in
and around Java.

Java Bloomers: Vina Erbe, p. and capt.; H. Huether, c; Ethel Hicks, 1b.
and Mgr.; Anette Stoller, 2b; Mary Beckman, 3b; Cora Stewart, ss;
Vina Varnum, r.f.; Elna Dyer, l.f.

Heavy Weights: L. H. Hughes, p and capt.; A. E. Rose, c; J. Stoller,
1b; H. C. Erbe, 2b; Kurt Eisentraut 3b; W. C. Hicks, ss.; B. P. Adams,
r.f.; C. F. Clement, c.f.; Christ Helm. l.f.

The "Bloomers" beat the "Heavy Weights" 14 to 13 when darkness fell.

Early Musical Organizations

                JAVA MUSICAL CULTURE CLUB

In February of 1906, a number of music loving people met at the home of
C. F. Clement to organize a musical society. Members were: Mrs. K.
Eisentraut and Mrs. C. F. Clement, sopranos, Mrs. W. C. Hicks and Mrs.
A. D. Mashek, altos Mr. A. E. Rose, tenor; and Mr. K. Eisentraut and
John Hofmeyer. basses. C. F. Clement was the director.

                JAVA QUARTET

On Monday, April 12, 1909, a male quartet was organized. First tenor,
W. L. Sackett, second tenor, A. E. Rose, first bass, Carl F. Clement
and second bass, John Hofmeyer.

In December of 1911, Java again had a male quartet composed of Messrs.
Spiry, Doerr, Krause and Bauder. C. F. Clement was the director.

                THE JAVA BAND

The Java Band held a business meeting on Monday night, October 5, 1903.
It was decided to hold two rehearsals a week, Monday and Friday nights.
Also, to engage an instructor, Mr. H. C. Buechler for $30.00 a month.

THE JAVA ORCHESTRA

This group was organized November 4, 1903, with the following members:
Edward Rose, violin; I. R. Krause, cornet; Henry Thielen, clarinet or
cello and C. F. Clement piano. Ed Rose was the leader; I. R. Krause,
secretary; and Henry Thielen, the treasurer. A fine Whitney piano was
purchased from the music house of John McArthur and Son of Aberdeen.

                THE JAVA CORNET BAND

The Java Cornet Band held its annual election of officers on Monday,
December 7, 1903. The officers elected were: President, S. Rosenthal;
vice president, Joseph Bosch; secretary, C. F. Clement; treasurer,
Henry Thielen; marshal, Jacob Schaffer; trustees, Jacob Stoller, Christ
Huether and John Hofmeyer. This meeting showed much enthusiasm, the
indications are that the band will be running smoothly again .
