                REMEMBRANCES of EARLY DAY JAVA
                          JOHN BANIK

In the fall of 1899 our father, John Banik, moved our two room house
from Bowdle, to Java, near what was later to become Main street. He
built a small blacksmith shop nearby. Then he sent for his family. Our
mother, Katie Banik, was visiting her parents with their three small
children, Gertie, Anna and Elsie.

At that time there was no depot in Java. There was only a junction,
about a mile north of Java, and a spur had been run in. When Katie and
the children arrived, they got off the train. It backed out and Katie
looked around. It was all prairie! She turned around and looked up the
hill. At the bottom of the hill she saw a grain elevator (Ewald's), and
a small building a ways away (Thielen's Lumber Yard). On top of the
hill, she saw a larger building (Hofmeyer's Store), and a ways away and
across from Hofmeyer's she saw the little two room house she
recognized. Near by was a smaller building. (John's shop).

John came running down the hill and gathered up his family. They
trudged up the hill. From up there Kate saw two houses, (Thielen's and
Ewald's). Katie was on the verge of crying. "Is this where we're going
to live? " she asked.

"It will build up," John said, "You'll see". "There are a lot of
farmers around and no blacksmith. You'll see. This will be a town."

She went inside. One room of the house held two beds, a cradle for
Elsie, a dresser, a sewing machine and a wardrobe. The other room
served as a kitchen and living room. A range to cook on and to heat the
house in winter, a table and chairs.

Two years later a boy was born, Albert. He died of convulsions and
was buried in the Adventist cemetery, south of Bowdle. There was no
Adventist church near Java then; though later, one was built in Sutley,
about nine miles north of Java.

As John had promised, the town began to grow.

In 1902, John moved the house closer to what was later to become Main
street. He sold his shop to John Weisgram and built a larger one on
front of the house.

His business had grown so much he needed to enlarge. He and Simon
Bitzer went into partnership. Simon did carpentry work. He made wheels,
spokes, sled runners, etc. John did the blacksmithing. John bought a
large engine on which was attached a huge flywheel. On this flywheel
was a wide leather belt, which extended across the room to run other
pieces of machinery.

One day we will never forget. Somehow my father's clothes got caught in
the flywheel. He grabbed a bar and hung on for dear life. The powerful
wheel tore most of his clothes from his body. When Simon finally got
the engine stopped, all my father had left of his clothes was a strip of
heavy underwear over his shoulder and part of one leg. His flesh was
torn, mangled and bleeding. If he hadn't been so strong, he would
surely have been killed.

John brought the first car to Java, a small red Franklin. He taught
Gertie, who was about ten, to run the car and they drove up one street
and down another to show that even a child could run it.

He sold an International to Fred Dufloth and Mrs. Dufloth came, raging.
"It goes too fast! You'll get him killed!" But Fred drove it and never
did tip over.

John began selling threshing machines about the countryside. He sold
many rigs and began operating a rig himself. He hired men on header
boxes to haul sheaves from the field, men to pitch them into the
separator, men to sack the grain as it came from a huge spout and men
and wagons to haul it away. Then there was a waterman, a fireman for
the steam engine. John was the engineer.

        John Banik in front of Banik Garage

Hofmeyer's Store was the only store for miles around for a long time.
In it one could find almost every necessity from thread to harnesses.
There were bolts of cloth, even laces, on shelves and showcases. Men's
overalls and jackets, shirts shoes for men and women, underwear, etc.
Groceries lined the opposite wall. Flour, sugar, candy in large glass
bowls, spices, salt in cloth sacks, etc. There was no such thing as
canned foods. You canned your own.

I, Gertie, remember running across to the store for a penny's worth of
candy. Those days are gone forever.

Some flour stood about in 50 pound sacks, as well as the sugar. Also
Hofmeyer kept some in bins from which one could buy smaller amounts.

In the back were farm tools, rakes, hoes, spades, etc. hanging from the
wall, or standing in racks. Hand tools, etc. from hammers to nails and
screws. Harnesses hung from the wall, bridles, even a saddle or two.
One could smell the leather throughout the store.

Hofmeyer's was Java's "Trading Post", in the early days.

The town began to grow and grow fast. In 1902 Cook and Lakeman built a
store kitty corner from Hofmeyers, (Later owned by John Wolf ) . Their
homes were either built or moved in. John Bauer built a store across
the street from Hofmeyer's. Bauer's had the first postoffice in Java.

Halls moved a large building in across the Main street from Hofmeyers
and made a hotel out of it. Later, Fred Diegel bought it and Halls
built across Main street from our place, and later installed a
telephone office on the first floor. Mae Campbell bought Diegel's hotel
and later had the building moved down to the foot of the hill in the
next block. This she ran as a hotel for many years.

John Banik built onto his blacksmith shop. Also, he added two more
rooms to the house.

In the northwest corner of the implement shop, John built a room
downstairs and a large room above it. In the downstairs, with only a
dirt floor, Dr. McCaw started the first dental office in Java. He later
built across the street and had his office there. Years later, Dr.
McCaw was killed when the car he was riding in plunged off the high cut
bank of the Missouri near Mobridge.

Carl Clement built a building on Main street and started the first
newspaper in the vicinity. His home was established at the northeast
outskirts, across from what would later be the grade and high school.

A saloon was built next to Bauer's store.

Dave and John Blinderman built a small store near the railroad tracks,
with living quarters in the rear. He later moved next door to the
saloon.

Mr. Winkley started a meat market next to Mae Campbell's hotel. In
those days you got liver and kidneys for free !

Homes were built or moved in and trees planted. Grandma Harris' small
house was moved in next door to John Banik's implement shop. She held
the first school in town. Who will ever forget Grandma Harris. She was
nurse and teacher for all of us then. When illness struck, she was
called. There was no doctor then. It was much later that Dr. Rosenthal
came to town. John Banik and Simon dissolved partnership and Dr.
Rosenthal's family moved into the Bitzer house. Dr. Rosenthal also
started a drugstore on Main street, across the street from Mae Campbell.

A firehall was built a bit east and next door to our place. A large
fire bell was installed and whoever discovered a fire ran to pull the
rope to the bell. Every man was a fireman in those days. Later Java
bought a fire engine.

Behind it was a small jail which was built for the unruly and behind
that, next to the alley, a tall windmill had gone in, which supplied
the town with water. At the huge, round trough the city's stock was
watered.

A lot of homes had cisterns, but we carried every drop we used from
this well. In the evening, before washday we carried water to fill two
barrels. Lye softened it during the night. Maybe this windmill is still
standing, though many changes have taken place in the years gone by.

A Lutheran church was built, with the parsonage behind it, on the
street behind Hofmeyer's store. Rev. Kirchdoerfer taught many of Java's
children during summer vacation to read and write German.

On the hill farther west, a Presbyterian church went up. There most of
us children went to Christmas programs and received a small sack of
goodies.

Fourth of July was a great day then. A big parade, fireworks,
firecrackers popping all day and night. Dancing all day and far into
the morning. A decorated haywagon was loaded with the youngsters of
Java and they rode in the parade, waving their small flags. By then we
had a Java band and they marched and played rollicking marches. As I
remember it, August Schlepp was the leader of the band.

Willis Banik was born on the 4th of July, and is well celebrated to
this day. It is a day I'll never forget. Relatives had poured in and
they slept on the floors, in wagons and wherever there was room. I was
not allowed to ride in the parade. Dr. Rosenthal was there and soon
there was a new baby in the house. That was Willis.

Allie, Harry and Elmer came along later.

John tore down the old house and built a new two story house on the
same location. John now sold cars, threshing machines, tractors and
farm machinery.

The family grew up, were married and moved away.

At this time, Gertie (Goodmanson) has one son, two grandsons and 8
great grandchildren.

Anna (Storrs) has no children.

Elsie (Reiswig) has two children, and two grandchildren.

Willis lives in Lodi, and has no children.

Allie became an ophthalmologist and author of several books. He has one
daughter and two granddaughters.

Harry has four sons and one daughter and four grandchildren. He lives
at Mobridge.

Elmer lives in Rapid City. He has no children.

Our father, John Banik passed away in October, 1936. He was buried at
Aberdeen.

Our mother, Katie, resided for many years at Wakpala, S.D. She
passed away in March, 1971, at Mobridge. She was buried in the Mobridge
cemetery. She lived to the ripe old age of 93.

Many changes have taken place in Java, but the wonderful memories we
have will always be with us. For there we spent the happiest days of
our lives.


                    History as I Remember...
                By Fred G. Bauman and wife, Rosina

About 200 years ago, after the Turks had left the Ukraine territory in
South Russia by the Black Sea, this large country was in need for many
willing workers to come and settle and build up this land.

The Russian czar and his wife, a German girl from Germany, called
Katherine the Great, called on the German people in Germany to come to
South Russia and settle this great land, the Ukraine. She was a smart
wife to him.

Then our ancestors from Wuerttenberg, Germany followed this call, left
their homes in Germany and started on this long and dangerous journey,
in large hand-powered boats. They took their belongings with them. They
traveled on the Danube river and overland routes to the Kherson state
or territory and established a village called Hoffnungstal (in English,
Hope Valley), about 75 miles from Odessa. As an inducement, the czar
had promised them freedom of religion and freedom from military
service, and I was told, a small loan. These people nearly all got poor
after this hard journey; some got sick, and some even died on their
way to Russia.

        Mr. and Mrs. Frederich Bauman and Fred

But they worked hard, cultivated the land, planted fruit trees and
vineyards, and built up this country, also, later paid taxes. Their
village was growing fast; in later years, they built a very large
Lutheran church in the center of the village, also large schools, and a
courthouse. They elected their own village government officials as
follows: a Schulz, and an assistant Schulz. This is like a mayor and
assistant mayor. A so-called Bittel was the village messenger (as there
were no telephones), a judge, and a police, and a secretary.

The fine for minor offenses usually was from 5 to 25 hard lashes with a
stiff twisted rope, or with a willow rod on the person's seat. Sometime
the judge gave jail sentences. A major crime was punished by district
or federal courts.

Their church services were mostly in the German language. Their schools
were half the time, the German religious language, and half the time in
the Russian language. The children were required to attend school the
sixth and up to including the fourteenth years. Then they were
confirmed and became members of their church.   The village was laid
out something like our towns, with wide streets with each family place
about as big as a city block, and was surrounded by a four-foot high
nice straight stone wall, as most farmers in later years owned a few
work oxen or horses, a few milk cows, chickens and geese. They kept
their livestock on their places in the village which was called a dorf.

In the summer time, boys or men were hired to take all their cattle to
the pasture every morning, and bring them home every evening. A boy
even had to drive their geese out to pasture and bring them home
evenings.

Their grain fields were a distance of a few miles from the village, as
well as their melon patches. They planted usually in one place together
some distance from the village. But when the melons got ripe, then they
hired a watchman to guard the melons so nobody would steal them.

In the years about 1870, the Russian government required the
able-bodied twenty-one year old boys to serve in the army. Each man or
boy could draw a number; those who drew a lucky number did not need to
serve in the army. My mother's oldest brother was one of the first who
had to serve to help fight against the Turks. He was a medical man and
so was required to give first aid to the wounded Russian soldiers
behind the battle lines. The Turks were driven back again.

The Black Sea port, Odessa, by then had become a large shipping center.
The farmers hauled their wheat to Odessa by team. There much of it was
loaded into big ships to be exported to foreign countries.

        Mr. and Mrs. Fred G. Bauman

Our village had its own flour mill and most people in Russia used whole
wheat flour to bake their bread.

There were not many machines, so nearly all farm work had to be done by
hand labor. The fields were plowed with oxen or horses, seeded by hand,
and then dragged in. The grain was harvested by hand with a scythe,
then gathered by hand and made into bundles, and bound with long,
twisted rye straw, put into shocks to dry then. When dry, it was hauled
home into the village, spread on a clean place; then their horses ran
around on it to thresh it out. In later years they got some threshing
machines, but they were not as modern as ours are now in the U. S., so
it still required much hand labor to thresh their grain.

I was born in Hoffnungstal in Kherson in 1892, and in 1893 I came to
the U. S. with my parents, Friederich and Christiana (nee Fiechtner)
Bauman. We came on a small ship, and it took 31 days to make the trip.
We landed by New York, then went by train to Bridgewater, S.D., as my
uncle Gottlieb Fiechtner Sr., came to Bridgewater, S.D., a few years
before. But that same year we, and also uncle Gottlieb Fiechtner Sr.,
came to Walworth county, as there was a little rough and stony land
left here for a homestead.

When we came here, Bowdle was at the end of the Milwaukee railroad. All
the farmers as far west as the Missouri river had to travel by team to
Bowdle or Eureka, to haul their grain and get their groceries. They
traveled across country, as there were no fences and no roads those
early days.

My father then took up a 160-acre homestead five miles east from where
Java is now located, and uncle Gottlieb took a 160-acre homestead four
miles east, where Gust Schaffer lives now. Gottlieb Fiechtner's wife's
brothers, Philip Spiry Sr., and his brothers, Fred and Karl came out a
few years before we did, and they were living about three miles north
from where Java is now; Otto Mueller lives there now.

We built a sod house called semljanka in Russia, and a sod barn, and we
got a well. Nearly all the pioneers were poor and had a hard start to
build up this country. Bangor, five miles south of where Selby is now
located, was the county seat.

The next year my father bought two oxen which weighed about 1800 pounds
each; and they worked without bridle or lines, just by saying the words,
"Gee, or haah, or hoh!"  We also bought one horse and one milk cow.
With the two oxen, the horse and the cow hitched on the breaking plow,
we broke up some sod and seeded some flax.

Later the same year, my mother was raking hay with one ox called Jim. I
was sitting on the frame of the rake, and mother was sitting on the
seat. Jim worked faithfully as long as the weather was cool, but by 10:
30, it got hot. There was a small lake about 300 feet away; then Jim
looked towards this water, and turned around and walked right into the
four-foot deep water with the rake and mother and me on the rake. Jim
enjoyed it very much in the water for about six hours; and he did not
care how hungry and thirsty mother and I were. We pleaded with him.
"Jim, please take us home." Then about four p.m., Jim looked back at us
and felt sorry for us, listened to us and was good enough to walk out
of the water, and take us home.

About two days later my father hauled this hay home with both oxen on
the hay rack on a hot day again. He had a very big load of hay and was
sitting on top of his high load; and he drove past this pond of water.
Then Jim and Charlie, the oxen, turned around and took him, with his
big load of hay into the deep water. Father had to wait almost six
hours until Jim and Charlie were good and ready, and pulled the load
home.

Sometime later, David Blinderman started a grocery store on the prairie
about five miles north of Java. John Hofmeyer started a grocery store
at the little trading post called Rosella, located northwest where
Arthur Helm lives now. Also a trading post and post office were started
about twelve to fourteen miles northwest of Java, near the Christ Rau
Sr., farm. This was called Sutley. All this was a great help to the
pioneer farmers who would travel by teams those days.

In the year 1899 the Milwaukee Railroad started an extension of their
road to the west of the Missouri river. By 1900 three new towns were
started: Java, Selby, and Evarts, west by the Missouri.

Java was growing very fast. Henry Thielen and Joe Bosch started the
first grocery store about one block east of Main street. David
Blinderman moved his grocery store to Java from the country to west
Main street. Bauer, Hoff and Stoller moved a building from Eureka,
enlarged it, and started a grocery, dry goods and hardware store where
the Christ Pfitzer Paint store is now. The post office was in the Bauer
store and run by Stoller's daughter. Mr. Jacob Stoller also moved his
home from Eureka, and built an addition to it. The home is now owned by
Amelia Wolf. The firm of Bauer, Hoff, and Stoller also built a large
grain elevator at an early date. John Hofmeyer from Rosella built a
very large two-story building on the corner on Main street, north of
the present barber shop, and started a grocery, dry goods, and hard-
ware. The second story of this building was used for a dance hall. Cook
and Arend Lakeman built a big building on the corner of east Main
street and sold groceries and hardware. Now Java had five grocery
stores in its first years, also two lumber yards. Fred Diegel built a
hotel on the corner of east Main street, north of the post office. Later
he sold it to George Zeller. He also built a large livery barn east of
his hotel, which was later operated by George L. Wolf. He did livery
work with his Model T in later years. A second hotel was built on east
Main and later sold to my uncle, Jacob Fiechtner- then to Bill Campbell
and later it was used for a high school girls' dormitory.

A medical doctor, Sigmond Rosenthal, moved from Eureka, and located on
east Main street. He practiced medicine for nearly twenty years in Java.

  Later R. M. Hennings started a drug store on west Main street, there
were two implement dealers, two blacksmiths, and later two banks: The
American State Bank, owned by Philip Spiry Sr., and sons, and The First
State Bank, owned by Mr. Hicks and Jacob Helm. An attorney, son of an
early pioneer farmer, Mr. Adams, located on east Main street. (Adams
Township is named after Mr. Adams).

In 1897 about twelve farm families organized to build a church on the
prairie, five miles east and one mile north of Java. These poor
pioneers, including my parents, donated all the work. They called it
the Hoffnungstal church, because half the members came from the village
of Hoffnungstal in Kherson, and the other half, from Hoffnungstal in
the state of Bessarabia in South Russia.

The Rev. Ottersberg from Hosmer, served us until 1900, coming to us by
team twenty-three miles. He could not come every Sunday; then the deacon
read the sermon from the book which was written by a minister in
Germany and contained a nice sermon for every Sunday. My father or John
Schuh Sr., read the sermon most of the time.

In 1900, the Karl Schnaible and Spiry small church was moved to Java,
and located where the Congregational is.

In that same year, our Hoffnungstal congregation, the small Java
church, and the Peace congregation (about eight miles north of Java)
joined together and called a minister, Rev. Fred Braun, a young single
man, who served us one and a half years. Rev. George Kirchdoerfer, a
young, single pastor from Germany, served us forty years. A year after he
came, he got married and they raised 4 children. For a time he served
six congregations: The St. Paul Lutheran in Java, our Hoffnungstal
church; the Buechler church, eight or nine miles east from Java; the
Bauder and Perman congregations, which had met in a school about twelve
miles southeast from Java; the Peace congregations, eight miles
northwest of Java and Lowry. He had to travel by team and could serve
only one church on one Sunday. The other churches had services with the
deacon reading the sermon from the sermon book.

Our Hoffnungstal church had no organ; in later years I took my violin
and led the singing. I taught the children and the older people many
new songs. Later our congregation bought an organ which I played for
the church for many years.

About 1909, the Java congregation moved the small church from its place
and used it for a school house. They built a larger church in its
place, which is now the Congregational church.

About 1930, we sold our Hoffnungstal church to the Sutley Congregation,
and we joined the St. Paul Lutheran church in Java. About 1931, this
church was raised and a full basement was built under the church. Later
we sold this church to the Congregational people. Then we built the
large church which we still use.

I was asked to write a little about my early history. My parents,
Friederich Bauman Sr., and my mother, Christiana (nee Fiechtner) were
born in Hoffnungstal, Kherson, in 1869 where they went to school and
were married in 1891. They came to Walworth county when I was one year
old. My father took up a 160-acre homestead and started to farm. We
endured many hardships, like many pioneers had to endure. They raised
six children, four boys and two girls, who are all still alive.

I was the oldest, born 1892, in Russia. I grew up, worked hard from a
young boy on, went to school, and was confirmed by Rev. Kirchdoerfer
March 24,1907, in the small St. Paul Java church.

My wife, Rosina Fiechtner, was born in Hoffnungstal, Kherson, Ukraine
in 1902, was baptized and went to school there. She was the daughter of
Matheis and Katherina (nee Brosze) Fiechtner. In 1912 when ten years
old, she came to the United States with her parents and family to Lehr,
N.D. There she attended school, was confirmed in the Lutheran faith,
and worked much of the time as a hired girl. In 1919 she came to
Walworth county with her family and settled on a farm ten miles south of
Java.

On October 3, 1920, we got married by Rev. Kirchdoerfer in our
Hoffnungstal church. We bought land five miles east of Java, built a
home, and started to farm. We were blessed with five children as
follows: Martin F., married and has one son, called Freddie, and lives
on the home farm; Clarence, married, with four children in Bismarck;
Harold, married, with three children and lives in Salt Lake City; Rose
Emma, married to Wilmer Stiegelmeier, have four children, and live on a
farm five miles southwest of Selby; Hilda, married to Otto Schafer,
with two girls, and lives in Minneapolis.

A short story: When I was still a young man in the cold winter, one of
our neighbor's husbands was very sick away from home. His wife, with
two small children, had cattle and horses to take care of. I felt sorry
for her, so I went to help with the work. There was only bread and
coffee, but no money.

  One morning I was working by the barn and the lady came and gave me
her husband's double barrel shot-gun with her last two shells. She
asked me to try to get a prairie chicken. I walked to the field; but
before I loaded my gun, I said a prayer and hoped to get at least one
chicken. But in a short time, I shot five big fat prairie chickens with
my two shells. The lady had tears in her eyes when I came home with so
much good meat. She quickly prepared one for dinner.

                PIONEER RECOLLECTIONS
                MR. AND MRS. WM. KUNZ

Thousands of people from the German country were lured into Russia by
the beautiful Czarina, Catherine II, herself a German princess, in
1763, in an effort to inspire the Russian peasant to higher ideals. In
1871, the special privileges granted them were withdrawn. This resulted
in a movement to the United States, Canada, and South America. The Black
Sea Germans started arriving in the Dakota territory while the
homestead land was available.

Two children stood beside the brown casket. "How strange papa looks!"
they thought. His lips were drawn, his face was thin and gaunt. He had
been ill for as long as the children could remember. When the last
spadeful of earth struck the box, five year old William carefully wiped
the tear from his little sister's eye and then from his own. He looked
up at his mother Christine. . . She was gazing far off into the
distance and seemed to hear only the sound of the wind. As if in a
trance, she held the children's hands in a firm grip, then led them
away.

"Why not come with us, Christine?" friends and relatives pleaded with
the sad, but non-bewildered and unfaltering Christine. Cautiously, she
made a study of the provisions of the Homestead Bill which had become a
law on May 20, 1862. Her prerequisites did comply: She was the head of
a family, she was a citizen of the United States, she had never borne
arms against the United States Government; and because of these simple
stipulations she was qualified to be granted by the government, 160
acres of land? 160 acres just for the asking? Was it really President
Abraham Lincoln who made all this possible?

Lured by the promise of free land excited this would-be-pioneer. On
the other hand, where was it they were going? How could she get there:
Her husband's illness had taken their earnings, the children were very
young. Would the journey be too much for them?

If ever there was a woman made for a life on the prairie frontier, it
was Christine.. despite this, she had feelings of doubt mingled with
curiosity: Was she brave and strong enough to endure the hardships, the
loneliness, the heartaches? Would the newly promised land be
bountiful and willing to share? Throwing back the shoulders of her
strong and able body, brushing away the unbidden tears, looking up
toward the Heavens, and with a prayer on her lips, she whispered, "Yes,
why not?"

Little William and Christina were healthy, happy children and became
intrigued with the idea that soon they would be going on a long
journey. Mother Christina sold her house for what little it did bring.
With the money she bought a young cow which would provide milk, then
made arrangements to pay a fare for herself and the children. The
children helped Christine decide what to take. A difficult task this.
The cookstove must go, necessary cooking utensils, essentials as flour,
salt, beans. . . a large quantity of baked bread, which had first been
dried out so it would not mold, the Precious Book, the shot gun and the
small powder keg. A large supply of cow chips and corn cobs had been
gathered, dried and put into gunny sacks to be used as fuel for cooking
for warmth. All was in readiness so that long before dawn on the 27th of
April, 1884, the sleepy, but excited William and Christina clutching
her rag doll, climbed aboard, snuggled down in the warm feather bed and
fell asleep.

The cavalcade of four white covered wagons, four teams of oxen, and
twenty adults and children moved slowly out of Yankton wending its way
on a journey of destiny. The wagons creaked and swayed and jolted along
across the rolling prairie. "Why do oxen's feet become sore?" asked the
little one as the caravan halted in a grassy meadow along the way. Had
Christine heard that covering hooves with cow hide would prevent this?
Time was needed to care for the tired animals, to grease the wheels, to
tighten the axle, and to repair the torn canvas.

        Mr. and Mrs. William Kunz--1901

Unexpectedly, as if the gray clouds above were overladen, they released
their burden of heavy cold drops of rain in sweeping torrents. For two
days the prairie schooners stood still, bogged down in mire and muck.
"Shall we turn back?" asked those whose spirits had been dampened by the
rain and storm. "No", replied the resolute Christine, "Let us go
straight ahead". She was one not to be defeated by the frontier without
giving it a chance.

On plodded the sturdy oxen, as the wheels creaked and the traces
squeaked against the weakening harnesses. The children's voices
screeched with delight whenever the wagons careened and lurched in the
washed out cow trails or jostled over large embedded stones.

"Turn due west", directed Christine, as the caravan approached the
village of Aberdeen. There was not even a cow trail to follow on the
tough course now, only tall buffalo grass bowing and bending in the
breeze in this awe inspiring magnitude of imposing grassland.

The weary, worn, haggard and grimy travelers of inextinguishable
courage and spirit, finally reached their destination. Christine knelt
in a prayer of Thanksgiving for the faith, and the unyielding
determination of these brave and courageous people. The stage was set!
Or was it? Once again the weather marked its time. Lightning and
rainstorms as these . . . they had never experienced. For refuge they
climbed into their crowded wagons once again. In spite of the rain, and
because of the great need of water, and because the men were forced to
devote their time to breaking sod for planting and building, Christine
decided that with the help of her sister, a well must be dug. She was
aware and cautiously guarded against the perils of "damp", the heavier
than air gas which sometimes gathers in the bottom of a well, and which
had been known to have taken the lives of pioneers. Luckily, after only
two days of hard labor, the sisters struck water. The well, curbed with
rock, still stands with its abundance of cool, cool water.

Christine's temporary shod shanty was built out of bricks, made of mud
and straw. It had two rooms. Built in the wall between the living room
and kitchen, was a hay-burning stove, such as had been used in Russia.
It was built out of flat stones and mud. . . 3 feet wide, 6 feet high
and 7 feet long. The walls of the oven were several inches thick and
thus heated the rooms. It was possible to fire the oven from the kitchen
through a 12x16 inch opening. Each evening and each morning the
children piled armfuls of hay and large gray dried buffalo chips into
the oven. This kept the rooms snug and warm.

Early that year much heavy snow fell and with it time and time again
bitter winds arose, sweeping the prairie with blinding blizzards. As a
result, Christine and the children faced the winter of 1884 without
provisions enough to ward off suffering during the months before them.
Their meager subsistence consisted of corn meal mush and milk. As if
hardships would never cease, a late frost in the spring killed the
grain crop. And the next autumn brought about scorched and seared
grasslands. On a fall day Christine watched from her window, the
smoke-filled sky hide the sun from view. Then the furious and violent
leaping fire moving across the prairies with the speed of lightning,
sweeping everything before it.

Christine assured the frightened children that because of the firebreak
around the yard, and because of God's ever watchful eye, they would be
quite safe. The fire swept close by, leaving nothing but a dark blanket
covering the earth for as far as they could see. White buffalo bones
were scattered everywhere in big piles. At the market in Ipswich, a
two-day journey, Christine received $10.00 for each ton of bones. The
total sum was adequate to meet her needs for the ensuing year.

The flax crop in the summer of 1886 was cut with a grass mower, gathered
and spread on the ground. By running two borrowed ponies over the
outspread flax, the seed was loosened from the chaff. With shovels the
chaff was raised into the air and let fall. The wind blew the straw
away as the seed fell to the ground. In three days time the crop of 67
bushels had been threshed. At 57 cents a bushel Christine received
$37.00 for her entire crop. The following spring her crop was "even"
better. She was thus able to lay away supplies for the winter of 1888
with its memorable blizzard. A valuable food had been added to their
stock now as antelope was roaming the country side, not too far from
their home.

Again and again, fear and horror gripped the heartstrings of the
pioneer. The Indians had been acting badly of late. They had killed the
Spenke family in Winona and the Sioux were on a rampage seeking revenge
for Sitting Bull's death, and now they were threatening to drive these
white settlers from their colonies in South Dakota. Hearts pounded,
pulses quickened and eyes became strained in an ever-sharp watch for
any movable object on the prairie. The families gathered in one of the
large homes. With pitch forks, oxhammers, and muzzle guns they waited.
On the fourth day a rider came galloping by, warning them to leave at
once for Eureka. On the track in Eureka, the Northern R. R. had coupled
up 11 cars with an engine attached, ready to pull out with women and
children in case of emergency. After the Indian scare had subsided, the
settlers wended their way back to the prairie cabins, but not without
fear of what might happen to them any day. Pranksters had had their
hey-day too, and William was one of them. Barely had the exhausted
families returned from their distressing Indian experience, when the
boys, having improvised explosives, by filling old well pipes with gun
powder, lit them and with hideous war "hoops", drove the panic-stricken
people back into the wagons and back to town. William had reason never
to forget the consequences of that exciting episode.

Many of the unjustly severe hardships on the prairie fell upon the wife
and mother of a family. It was the invincible Christine who doctored
the sick, the needy, the weary, the homesick and the lonely. It was she
who often closed the eyes and helped "lay to rest" the body of a loved
one, and in the absence of a pastor, spoke words of comfort to the
bereaved.

Christine's greatest attribute was her hospitality, her understanding of
neighbor, friend, as well as stranger. Hers was a heart of gold and her
generosity was over-whelming. William inherited many of these favorable
characteristics. At the age of 15, he had grown into a tall, lean,
brawny lad and eagerly took over entirely the responsibilities of caring
for his mother and sister.

With the passing of time, more and more families settled in the Sutley
area until the pioneer atmosphere began to wane. Among the newcomers
was a family of 8 sons, 3 daughters and one foster child,--ten year old
Katya. Katya,--from whence came this child?

She was a pretty little girl at the age of ten, brown eyes and dark
brown curly hair. Hopelessly forlorn, she was standing near the railing
on the second deck of the huge freighter chug-chugging its way toward
America. The Jewish and Russian emigrants were confined to the "hold"
beneath the lower deck where they were compelled to fight rats and
mice, combat illness, stench, death and disease. The second deck had
been reserved for the German people and here it was that,--as the salty
brine splashed up into her face, Katya wiped away her own salty tears.
Bitter memories hers, for so young a child: a last glimpse of her sweet
mother, as the coffin lid was closed and lowered into the ground; the
sobs of her father in despair, and then almost at the same time another
funeral procession making its way slowly down the road, to the same
church yard,--this time her gentle and kind father, and then there were
two mounds.

The sorrowful uncles at the gravesite singled out one or two of the 8
bewildered, grieving children, and led them out of the graveyard where
they dispersed, solemnly each one going a different way. A sudden
rocking of the ship brought Katya back to reality. As she steadied
herself she felt terribly homesick, lonely and terrified. With her tiny
hands she covered her face and wept with grief and fright.

On the 8th day of October of 1893, the ship embarked in New York
Harbor. The peasants were driven by guards into separate rooms where
their clothing was removed and put through a disinfectant process, to
free it of disease, germs, lice and bad odors. At the New York railroad
station were endless lines of passengers. On the dirty floor sat
emigrants with their small sacks of possessions, many of them too ill to
move on. Some having no place to go.

In Eureka, as the family stepped from the train onto the station
platform and the train began moving slowly away, there was a strange
quietness and a feeling of destitution in the air, only to be broken
moments later by cheerful greetings from relatives and friends.

Squirmy, giggly Katya clung to the sides of the wagon as it swayed and
bumped over the ruts through the wide open country side. With the
shadows of the evening the wagon stopped near the cabin which was to be
shared with relatives for the remainder of the year.

Katya's parents, as many hardy settlers before them, found at hand
material for shelter and fuel. The sod house provided shelter, the cow,
oxen and buffalo the food. Cow chips and twisted and dried prairie
grass served for fuel.

  The sod house plans followed the usual pattern,--16 ft. wide and 20
ft. Iong. The sod bricks were made by turning over furrows on the
ground where sod was thick. A spade was used to cut the sod into bricks
about 3 feet square. These bricks were laid side by side around the
foundation. The cracks were then filled with dirt and more layers were
placed on top. Every third course was laid crosswise of the others to
bind them together. This process was continued until the wall was high
enough to put a roof on it. A door and window frame were set in the
wall and sod was built around them. On the floor, over the rafters was
placed layer upon layer of prairie grass then covered with sod. In time
even grass grew on the roof.

Inside, the house was plastered with a mixture of mud, clay and ashes.
The temporary cabin was divided into rooms by curtains. The house was
crudely furnished. When the rains came, pots and pans were placed here
and there on the floor in order to catch the muddy drops of rain coming
through the grassy roof. The advantages of a sod house, however, were
numerous. It was cool in summer and warm in winter. There was no fear
of a storm blowing it over and there was no danger of destruction by
prairie fire. The barn was no more than a hay shed which served the
purpose for the calf. The oxen and cows were turned out on the prairie
to graze.

These daring and adventurous settlers must often have been reminded of
the good life they left behind. The beautiful gardens, orchards of
apples and nuts and grapes and pears; the spacious and luxuriously
furnished home with gypsy girl servants and the highly esteemed
mayorality position held by the father in Tuarnto, near the Black Sea.
As Katya grew older she looked forward for months to the time of the
4th of July celebration. For then, work was laid aside and the sun's
first rays found the family loaded into the wagon and hurrying off,
usually to Eureka. While relatives and friends met in groups, Katya and
her brothers strolled through the streets eating candy and drinking all
the lemonade that 6 cents would buy. Toward evening the tired family in
the wagon reluctantly took to the winding dirt road back to the cabin
on the prairie.

Sunday was a day of merriment for Katya too, for on his day she forgot
her hardship and weariness. While the elders assembled at a neighbor's
home after church, for a day of visiting, Katya was permitted to go
with her brothers, usually to a barn or a schoolhouse where there was a
wooden floor. At times it was just some shady spot under God's blue
heaven. Of all the frontier amusements, dancing held first place. Katya
was happy; not only was she and her girl friend given permission to
watch the dancers, but were granted the privilege of running back and
forth to the well, in order to replenish the water supply for the
dancers. Most often, the reedy notes of a mouth organ or a Jew's harp
or a fiddle furnished the music; if none of these were available, games
as Drop the Handkerchief, Skip to Ma Lou, and Charades, with the paying
of forfeits, were played.

Another form of amusement was the Corn Husking Bee. The corn was hauled
out of the field and stored in a granary before winter set in. The
young people gathered, sat on the floor of the granary and husked the
corn, throwing the ears in the middle of the floor. A young man finding
a red ear of corn was privileged to kiss the girl of his choice. Many a
smuggled red ear was brought out from under a coat at an advantageous
moment. Eating and dancing customarily concluded the evening.

Many pathetic pioneer stories relate how sickness and death on the
prairie affected the lives of the colonists. An accident at the age of
13 left Katya with a crippled left hand for the rest of her life.
Coming home from an unsuccessful rabbit hunt, due to a faulty gun, one
of the her step-brothers spied Katya in the yard, stopping to pull up
the pants of the 4 year old brother. Knowing the gun would not go off,
he jokingly threatened to shoot her. Laughlingly, she sloughed off the
threat. He aimed and fired. The shell shattered her arm near the
shoulder and entered the little boy's head.   Life of the pioneer
country doctor was a hard one too. Due to the deep snow and the
distance, three days passed before the doctor was able to thoroughly
cleanse Katya's wound. It was obvious that amputation was necessary.
Katya's father, however, refused to give his consent, realizing that
such crude instruments were known to have been used as an ordinary
saw or butcher knife and without anesthetics. The kind doctor with the
family watched by Katya's bedside during the crisis. Herbs, oil of
camphor, other home remedies and prayer were used, and Katya, being an
otherwise healthy and strong girl, with much determination, regained
her good health.

As 4th of July celebrations came and went, the activities became less
formal and more enjoyable. Speeches and toasts were replaced by ball
games, horse races, foot races and dancing. People welcomed such
opportunities to break the monotony of frontier loneliness.

With the passing of time, Katya became an unusually attractive young
woman, but because of her handicap was reluctant to intermingle
especially with a mixed crowd. This made her no less attractive to the
young men who were aware of pretty girls.

William had procured a buggy, now that he had reached the courting age.
This was regarded as a sign that he was interested in a certain young
lady. So it was that right after Katya's 16th birthday, she had her
first date with the vivacious, intelligent and witty William. She
eluded her brothers and in William's new buggy drove off with him to
celebrate the 4th of July. As a special token of love, William gave her
a brand new dollar. With it she purchased a long-time admired white
hat with a black ribbon hanging loosely down the back. How proud she
was and how pretty she looked.

Katya and William had a mutual liking and respect for each other. Her
parents thought he would make her a fine husband. His mother thought
Katya would make William a good wife. So, without the knowledge or
consent of Katya, she was promised in marriage to William . She was 19;
he was 24.

It was at a time, just before this, that, as many times before, the two
met. The dashing, charming, blonde Christopher and Katya. It was the
waltzing that started it. Katya, nimble as a flea, spun round and
round, thinking of only the two of them. While the music played, a
whole life time went by,--then when the music stopped for the last
time, the world fell in. The two had experienced for many months an
irresistible attraction for each other. They had danced in
silence,--not a word passed their lips,--only their eyes met, but the
understanding between them had a quality of enchantment. She could
speak no English. He could speak no German. This was their memorable
farewell dance.

The wedding day in November was a chilly one, Katya thought, though the
warm sun was shining through the bright blue clouds. She was not sure
that this was what she wanted. She had been deeply hurt, but being a
forgiving and understanding woman by nature, she nobly accepted her
destiny. Straight from the church they drove in her brother's buggy to
the wedding feast at her home. Friends gave the groom and bride a
parting salute by stealthily removing a shoe from her foot. The price
of the shoe at the wedding auction brought the pair a goodly sum of
money.

Katya, with a heavy heart had worked hard for weeks in preparation of
the momentous event. Now, after the guests had gone, she felt uneasy in
the sudden silence as she looked at her smiling groom. He bade her
farewell as he drove off alone to his home. Katya's obligation to her
parents was to remain in their home for several days in order to return
the borrowed tables, chairs and dishes, to clean and to set things
aright again.

Charming, kind and gentle Katya soon had her own little sod house
filled with a warm and friendly atmosphere. Wise mother Christine had
moved out in order to make way for the new mistress. The first winter
was one of loneliness and solitude. A meager diet of frozen potatoes
and salt pork did not help make it pleasant. However, with spring came
the warm sun, the gentle rains and the greened buffalo grass. And with
it new arrivals. The population of the settlement continued to grow, so
that Sutley became a little Community Center. There was a blacksmith
shop and the General Store, which kept everything from a pin to a
wagon, from soda to beer. The postoffice became a focal point too, for
everyone from miles around delighted in gathering around the red-hot-
belly stove, to chew and to relate events of the year with often
exaggerated tales.

Long before threshing time, in the second year, William and Katya
realized their two room house would soon be too small. Setting the sod
blocks for a new house was exceedingly hard labor. Too hard for Katya
in her condition, but if the house was to be ready by fall, Katya and
William had to keep on the job more steadily than they at first
realized.

As the sun was setting noticeably earlier one warm November evening,
Katya sat watching it from her favorite resting place on the porch. It
seemed to be playing hide-and-go-seek with the fluffy, down clouds,
drifting across the sky. Near by, Molly, the gentle cow was browsing
idly. William's favorite pony was grazing in the near-by meadow. He was
old now, but for years had been the swiftest riding pony in the
country. With William on his back neither pony nor rider ever stopped
for a fence or a gate. Grain in the field had been cut, gathered and
stacked neatly in huge piles. "Wheat like this will make us rich,"
joshed William, as he sat down near Katya and gently patted her hand.
The years of hard work had brought them closer together and Katya had
come to love this ambitious, fun-loving man very much. Living was good,
she thought. However, the time had come for William to build up a good
fire and to go quickly to fetch his mother Christine.

With sympathetic hearts, neighbors helped the sorrow-stricken William
dig a little grave under the large poplar tree near the little sod
house. Mother Christine wrapped the body of the still-born babe in a
sheet and the heart-broken Katya, holding it close to her heart in
farewell, laid the beloved baby ever so gently into the crudely made
box. As William put his arm about Katya's shoulder, drew her to him and
put his strong firm hand in hers,--it made everything seem all right
somehow.

With the passing of time, William accepted a job working for the Crown
Elevator Company in Java. In order to fulfill the contract as Mail
Carrier, Katya drove daily over the open prairie from Sutley to Eureka
in a small two-wheel sulky with their favorite pony leading the way.

For 32 years William operated the Java Equity Exchange. It was his very
life and he gave it all he had. Aside from this job he took an active
part especially in activities for the betterment of his family and
community. He and Katya strove to do all they could in advocating
education for all children. William was president of the Board of
Education when the present school was built. He was a local Fireman for
as long as he could take part, belonged to the Woodman's Lodge, served
as Mayor of Java, for two terms during the difficult 1930's and was an
avid worker for his church.

Upon leaving part of himself at the Equity Exchange Elevator, William
and Katya moved to Mobridge in 1945 in order to be nearer to Erna and
her husband, Dr. A. W. Spiry. There they celebrated their golden
anniversary in 1951. Six years later, on April 22, 1957, William bade
farewell to this world and on May 6,1972, Katya went to be with him.
They left behind the children they loved so affectionately:

        Edward of Watertown
        Wilfred of Bismarck
        Ervin and Howard of Minneapolis
        Erna and Nettie of Mobridge.

Eleven grandchildren and 18 great-grandchildren, one of whom, Chloe
Hauck, had gone before them to await their arrival in God's blue
Heaven.--Written by Nettie Hauck.

                PETE FRAZIER, SHREWD HORSETRADER

At the turn of the century, Pete Frazier was working as a cowhand in
the Evarts and Lebeau area. Later he moved to Mobridge, where he owned
a livery stable. Each spring, Pete would bring in a herd of wild horses
from west of the Missouri river. These horses would be broken to ride
or to work, then when he was certain that all the horses were well
trained he would tie them together and head east to Java, where he
spent several weeks trading and turning the horses into cash.

He was well known throughout the Java area for his knowledge of
horseflesh. He never deliberately cheated anyone, but if someone would
out-smart him in a trade, he would not be satisfied until he evened the
score.

His daughter, Freida, now Mrs. Ellsworth Schuh, often helped him get
started away from Mobridge. The horses all wore halters, which were
tied to the tail of the horse in front. They traveled in 2 rows.
Between 30 and 40 horses were moved in this manner. Pete took the lead
and one of his hired hands would follow. The horses were allowed to
rest, graze and drink only once on the 30 mile drive.

Pete always spoke well of the Java people. He said that the German
women were the world's best cooks, and no doubt his praise and
compliments earned him many an invitation to a meal.

He spoke German well enough to carry on a conversation. He also spoke
Sioux Indian fluently and was often called on when an interpreter was
needed. He died of a heart attack when he was 64 years old. Only a week
before his death, he had trailed a small herd of horses from southeast
of Java to Mobridge.

                JAVA WAS A BUSY PLACE

                By Arend Lakeman, Sr.
    (Condensed from Java Herald, Sept. 7, 1923)

A little over 40 years ago Dirk Lakeman and family came to America from
Holland and settled in Luke City, Nebr., where they resided about 4
years, then moving to Walworth county, on a farm just one mile west of
the present site of Java. They operated the farm-with another quarter
which lies just south of town until the fall of 1900, when the Milwaukee
railroad extended its line from Bowdle to Evarts. There was quite a bit
of discussion then as to where to locate the town of Java. Some wanted
to have the buildings from Bangor moved to what was known as Horseshoe,
and make it one town instead of two. The people of this vicinity and
Bangor did not hitch together very well, so Java was started on the
hill of its present site and Bangor was moved to what is now Selby.

In the fail of 1900 the Lakemans started to build a store. John Erbe
had just started a lumber yard and the material was purchased from him.
When the walls were up, a storm came and tore them down. It was in the
fall of the year around Christmas, when Bauer, Hoff and Stoller opened
their store. There had been kind of a race between Bauer and John
Hofmeyer, who was also building at that time, but Bauers beat him to
it. Lakemans store did not open until spring 1901. It was about that
time that Java began to boom. There were many times when they took in
from $250 to $300 per day, all cash. They sold everything from a needle
to a threshing machine. Two additions were built on the store, one for
farm implements and the other for furniture.

The rural community was growing also, as O. D. Buchecker had started a
land boom, bringing new settlers from lowa and Nebraska, as well as
from foreign countries. In 1903 the school house was built in the east
end of the town. At that time it was really out of town, with few
buildings near

In 1910 Arend Lakeman Sr. took his father's place in the store, and it
went under the name of Cook and Lakeman. He remained there about a
year, after which Cook conducted the store alone. Later it closed
altogether. It was a great life while it lasted, and for many years the
store employed 5 to 7 clerks, but as time went on, more and more stores
were started and business declined.

From 1925 to 1937 the Lakeman family operated the Java Cafe, and later
moved to Selby. The son, Arend Jr., and wife, Ruth, still reside at
Mobridge.

                THE ANDERSON FAMILY

The first ancestors of the Anderson family to make the long journey to
America from Norway, was Lars Anderson. He and his wife, Kari Kalstad,
and two eldest sons, Martin and Antone, after arriving in New York,
made their home at Coon Valley, near La Crosse, Wis. Several years
later, in 1885, with three additional sons: the twins, Charlie and
Louie; and the youngest of the family, Otto, came to homestead in
Walworth county in the Swan Creek area.

The necessary steps were taken to file on a quarter of land, as a
homestead, and an additional quarter for a tree claim. The little town
of Lowry is now situated on Lar's tree claim. Land on all sides
adjoining these claims was regarded as free range. In the years to
come, the family had a small income from taking herd cattle from the
farmers as far north as the Java area, and south to Lebanon. They were
expected to get the cattle in the spring and return them in the fall.
The price per head was 25 cents per season. In those days, as today,
some people felt they were charging unreasonable prices. Lars used to
tell, with great amusement, of one customer, who was quite a tobacco
chewer, who got out his pocketbook to settle up his herd bill and upon
finding out the enormous amount he owed, got so excited, he opened the
pocketbook up and mistakenly spit in it!

Kari died in 1914, and Lars, who was born in 1843, died in 1922.

Martin Anderson (1868-1942), the eldest son, married Eva Mauch, whose
parents came to America in 1880, and after homesteading in the Freeman,
S.D. area, came to live in the Java area. Eva was a sister to Mrs.
Jacob (Margaretha) Haux. To them were born: Edwin, Caroline (Mrs. Carl
Larson), Raymond and Norman. Mother Eva passed away in 1957.

Edwin, the eldest son, married Anna Holman, daughter of Engebret and
Marin (Noer) Holman, who immigrated to America from Norway, settling in
Bristol, S.D., where they entered the merchandising business.

Eddie and his family lived on farms in the southern end of Walworth
county until 1942, when they purchased the Garret Noble farm southwest
of Java. They lived here until 1948, when they moved to Java.

Being a baseball enthusiast for many years, Eddie's favorite sports
story is of an incident that occurred the first year he was a baseball
pitcher on the Java team: He was facing his former team mates from
Lowry, who were very familiar with his style, and treated them to a no
hit ballgame.

        Mr. and Mrs. Eddie Anderson

The six children of Eddie and Anna, who all graduated from Java High
school are: James, married Margaret Job, lives in Aberdeen, where he is
manager of the Brown county ASC office; Earl, married Maryl Wiedebusch,
lives on a farm he owns and operates, and is employed by the Safeguard
Industry at Aberdeen; Sylvia, married Edwin Karst, is employed as a
teacher's aide in the Selby school, and her husband, who is an Oliver
Implement dealer, owns and operates a Standard station and repair shop
in Lowry, where they live; Mary Anne, married Wilbur Krause, is
employed at the Selby Record newspaper office, and they live on a farm
they own and operate north of Java; Roger, married Karen Buechler, is
manager of the Walworth county ASC office in Selby, where they live;
Judith, married Gary Helstrom, lives in Crystal, Minnesota, and both
are employed as teachers.

                JACOB AMAN, SR.

Jacob Aman Sr., and his family left Russia in 1883 and took the long
journey to the land of America. The voyage to the New Country was rough
and hazardous, also the ship was unsanitary with many of the immigrants
being sick. Jacob's wife became sea sick and passed away shortly after
arriving in this country. They came to South Dakota by train and
settled in the Campbell county area. It was there that they built their
first home out of sod, which so many of the pioneers were doing.

They endured many hardships while trying to work and take care of the
family. This family included 5 boys: Rafael, Rochus, Michael, Anton and
John. There were also two half-brothers, Franz and Jacob, and one
half-sister, Mrs. Valentine Kary Sr.

        Jacob Aman, Sr. family, Michael, Mrs. Aman, Rafael, Anton,
        Rochus, Mr. Aman, John.

                MICHAEL J. AMAN

Michael J. Aman came to the new country of America from Russia with his
parents at the age of nine years. They settled in the Campbell county
area. He grew to manhood in the Artas, Mound City and Java areas, and
as a young man, he worked at various jobs.

He later married Christine Bollinger, who lived in the Artas area. In
1912, they moved with their little family to Saskatchewan Canada, where
they homesteaded, as did many other families from the States.

Mr. and Mrs. Michael J. Aman had four children: Edgar, married and has
2 daughters and living in Los Angeles, Calif., Alvin, married and has
two daughters and living in Culver City, Calif.; two girls, Mae, married
Walt Wolf and his one daughter and one son and living in Eureka, S.D.,
and Adeline, married Adolph Schuh and has one daughter and one son and
living in Java.

Mrs. Aman passed away in 1919 during the flu epidemic and then Mr. Aman
returned to South Dakota with his children. He later bought a home in
Java, where his children were educated in the Java school. He resided
in Java until his death in 1947.

        Mr. and Mrs. Michael J. Aman

                JACOB J. AMAN

Jacob J. Aman was born Jan. 11, 1860 in Londeau, South Russia, where he
grew to young manhood. In 1882, he joined the Russian Army and was in
the service for a period of 6 years. After being discharged from the
Army in 1888 he was united in marriage to Elizabeth Stiegelmeier.

The young couple came to America in 1889 and homesteaded seven miles
northeast of Java, where they built up a farm. Six children were born
into .this home, four sons and two daughters:

Jacob B., married Emilie Bender had one son, Albert and two daughters,
Irene and Allen. After his wife's death he later married Pauline Naasz.
He passed away in 1955.

John married Christina Wolf and had one son, Erven. John passed away in
1917.

Esther married Fred Mertz and is living in Aberdeen, S.D. They had two
daughters and one son.

Edwin married Lydia Zimmerman and they had six children, two daughters
and four sons. He was killed in a tractor mishap in 1942.

Albert R. married Alice Tews and had two sons and four daughters. He is
now living in the Bowdle Nursing Home.

Ida married Louis Gearhart and they had two sons. She is now living in
Aberdeen, S.D.

After deciding to retire from farming they built a home in Java on
North Main street in 1919. The home is now owned by the Arlo Fiedler
family.

Here they resided until their death. After a lingering illness, Jacob
J. Aman passed away at his home in 1928. Mrs. Aman continued to live in
their home until the fall of 1937 when she spent the winter in
California, with her son, Edwin and family. Planning to return to Java,
death interrupted her plans and she passed away in the spring of 1938.
She was buried in the Seventh Day Adventist cemetery northeast of Java.

A grandson, Albert A. Aman still resides on the family homestead.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob J. Aman

                MR. AND MRS. EMANUEL BAER

Emanuel Baer was born in South Russia December 28, 1880. He was the son
of John and Margaretha (Herman) Baer. The family immigrated to America
in 1893, coming first to Freeman, later coming to Walworth county,
where his father filed on land and became quite an extensive farmer,--
kept considerable livestock so there was always plenty of work for the
family. Later Emanuel also filed on land south of the first homestead,
which was a few miles east of Lowry, S.D. Emanuel received part of his
education in Russia and what schooling he could in the neighborhood
school. He herded sheep at an early age for the fabulous salary of
$8.00 a month. He clerked for H. A. Mason in his store in Bowdle, and
later worked in Bauer & Stoller store in Java; later still, for John
Hofmeyer in Java.

On July 25, 1905, he was united in marriage to Mary Schnaible of Java.
Mary was born February 15, 1887 in Freeman, S.D. to Karl and Elizabeth
(Oster) Schnaible. The family came to Walworth county in 1889 and lived
on a farm three miles north of Java. Her father raised large herds of
sheep and cattle and farmed extensively. Mary, along with other members
of her family had to help with many farm chores, working out in the
fields and walking behind a plow. She attended rural school. After
marriage they set up housekeeping in Java.

In 1906 Emanuel was elected Register of Deeds for Walworth county and
was re-elected in 1908 living in Bangor during these terms. While in
Bangor the courthouse was moved to Selby, so the family also moved to
Selby. At the expiration of his second term, the family moved to Java,
and he engaged in the real estate and insurance business for four years
when he was again returned to the office of Register of Deeds. After
another 4 years he sought and was elected to the office of Clerk of
Courts and he continued in this office up to his death in 1925. The
family moved to Selby in 1915 and lived there since.

Emanuel was a life long member of the Lutheran Church, serving as
trustee and treasurer; member of the Commercial Club; Golf club;
member of the early Java band and men's quartet.

To this union were born seven children,--two of whom passed away in
infancy. The children are Clara, Erna, Leander, Herbert and Emanuel
Dale.

Clara married Alfred Bastian. They have one daughter, Joyce, Mrs. Herb
McClellan and three grandchildren, all residing in Mobridge.

Erna married Edward Zabel and they have 3 children, Verne married to
Marcella Brockel and they have 4 children; Arlys, married to Harold
Brown and they have 2 sons; Sharon, married to Wayne Wolff and they
have 2 children, all living in and near Selby.

Leander married Martha Birkland at Poplar, Mont., and was manager of
the Poplar Mont., Elevator Co., when he passed away at the age of 46,
June, 1958. One daughter, Virginia Lee, Mrs. Al Hasselbach, lives in
Orchard Lake, Mich., with her husband and 3 children.

Herbert married Hilda Schanzenbach and they have 3 children, Dennis,
who passed away January, 1972 at the age of 29, leaving his wife,
Alvina and 2 children. Sandra Sue married Terry Stulken and resides in
Bowdle, S.D., they have 1 daughter; Judith Marie married Cecil
Ringgenberg and resides in Aberdeen. Herbie was affiliated with the
Selby Equity Union Exchange when he passed away in December, 1974.

        Mr. and Mrs. Emanuel Baer

Emanuel Dale (Buster) married Maxine Weber at Lincoln, Neb. They have 4
daughters. Karen, married Barry Ellenbecker, and they have one
daughter, and reside near Gettysburg. Tanya, Melanie and Rogene reside
at home in Spearfish, S.D., where Buster is in real estate and
insurance.

All three sons, Lee, Herb and Buster entered the service of their
country in World War 2.

While in the courthouse, Emanuel was the adviser and friend of all who
came to him and was helpful to the German speaking people in the early
20's.

Mrs. Emanuel Baer is a resident in the Samaritan Home in Selby. She is
87 years old and in fair health.

Our family has had its ups and downs and one thing we know is that only
God in Heaven has the answer to the various reasons for our life here
on earth.

                MR. AND MRS. JAKOB BAUDER, SENIOR

The quest for land motivated the immigration of the Bauder family from
Pfullingen, Germany to the Black Sea region. Catherine the Great, the
German princess from Anhalt-Zerbst issued a manifesto in 1763; however,
it was not until 1804, when her grandson Alexander I granted a second
manifesto, offering increased privileges that a mass migration took
place.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jakob Bauder, Sr.

The privileges, in short, were: 1. The free exercise of religion; 2.
Exemption from land taxes for 10-30 years, and 10 years in the cities;
3. Interest-free loans for the purchase of equipment; 4. Exemption from
military service "for unlimited time. 5. Self administration of the
community and the school, 6. Free apportionment by the Crown of 30-80
desseatine of land to each family.

These colonists were to be models in agriculture. Not everyone
qualified. The requirements were: A letter of good character; cash or
property amounting to 300 gulden; and they were to be married men.

Jakob Bauder, Senior, was born December 17, 1844 in the Colony of
Hoffnungstal, South Russia. He was the son of Johann Christoph Bauder,
a stocking weaver, who emigrated from Pfullingen, Germany in 1817 to
Russia.

Not much is known of Jakob's early childhood. The census registration,
District of Odessa for the year of 1858, shows that he, along with two
brothers and a sister were living with his grandfather, Josef Bauder.
It could be assumed the parents died at an early age. The real reasons
are lost to antiquity. The early years of settlement were very difficult
and many colonists perished from various epidemics.

Katharina Stahlicker and Jakob Bauder were married on November 5, 1867,
in the village of Hoffnungstal, meaning hope. Katharina was born
November 1, 1847 in Guldendorf, the daughter of Peter Stahlicker. They
moved to New Gluckstal, where their nine children were born.

In the colonies where they lived, the family was not permitted to
divide the land. Therefore, the father was often compelled to purchase
land from the Russians, at high prices, for his sons. So it wasn't
surprising that the colonists once more planned to emigrate, when the
news came from America, that the railroad companies needed them to build
the railroads and that there was land for homesteading ! The land was
always very important to them.

The decision to leave was made. The first step was to obtain a
passport. This was in three languages, Russian, French and German. Mrs.
Albert Wahl of Selby, S.D., has the original passport of the Bauder
family.

According to plan, eight families left the village in February, 1894.
They made an impressive group. They were Mr. and Mrs. Fredrich C.
Perman; his parents, Mr. and Mrs. Christoph Perman and two sons; and
the Adam Mertz, Jacob Mertz, John Feickert, Adam Schlecht, Gottlieb
Oster and the Jakob Bauder families.

After crossing the border into Prussia, they proceeded to Berlin, where
they had to undergo vaccination as well as having their baggage put
through a steaming process to sterilize it. After these preliminaries,
they went on to Bremen, Germany, where they boarded the big ship on
March 8, and began a new experience; that of crossing the ocean

After arriving in New York, the group boarded a train for Chicago, and
after a long trip during which they were sidetracked over Easter Sunday
in a Canadian City, they reached their destination and then came west to
Freeman, S.D. Here they spent a week visiting relatives and purchasing
horses, wagons and other necessary farm equipment which they shipped on
to Bowdle. On April 5th, the group of eight families, accompanied by
the Jacob Doerr family, who had been living at Freeman about two years,
while awaiting the arrival of the group, came to Bowdle. From here on
they scattered, some of them filing on homesteads in Walworth county,
some in McPherson county, and some near Monango, N.D.

The Bauders lived on the farm south of his son-in-law, Fred C. Perman
in Glenn Township, Walworth county. They were active in church work.
During those early years, church services were held in the country
school, about a mile west of their home. The church was a branch of the
American Lutheran church of Java. They retired to Lowry, and their son,
Jacob Jr., remained on the farm. Jacob Jr., later sold the farm and
moved to McLaughlin, S.D.

Jakob Sr., died in Lowry on April 21, 1919 and Katharina on February
23, 1927 in Bowdle, S.D.

Their children were Katherina, b. Aug. 17, 1868 m. Jacob Doerr 1891 di.
Oct. 10, 1940; Christianna b. Nov. 20, 1870 m. Adam Schlect Feb. 2,
1892, di. Jan. 12, 1952; Rosina b. Feb. 18, 1874 m. Fredrich C. Perman
Jan. 16, 1894 di. Jan. 1, 1959; Elizabeth b. July 17, 1876 m. Henry
Sonnenfeld Jan. 24, 1896 di. Sept. 13, 1963; Carolina b. Dec. 7, 1878
m. Christian Miller May 29,1897 di. Jan. 24, 1971; Dorothea b. Aug. 4,
1881 m. Andreas Lutz 1898, di. May 22, 1959; Mary b. April 12, 1884 m.
Christ Schilling January 1902 di. June 19, 1957; Christina b. Nov. 15,
1886 m. Wilhelm Schilling March 13, 1906 di. July 29, 1954; Jacob b.
Nov. 2, 1890 m. Caroline Mertz Jan. 11, 1916 di. July 11, 1937.

        John Bauer           Lydia Bauer

                JOHN AND LYDIA BAUER

John Bauer was born Jan. 17, 1888 on a farm in Campbell county, where
his parents had homesteaded. Later his dad bought a farm one mile
southeast of Eureka, where he grew up and attended grade school. In
1900 he was put in charge of the farm and his dad went to Java and
started the first store, in partnership with Hoff & Stoller. The store
was named Bauer, Hoff and Stoller. The post office was also housed in
this store for a few years. Later the farm was rented out and the
family moved to Java.

In 1908, John went west and filed claim on a homestead near White
Butte, but remained there only a year before he returned to Java. John,
his dad and brother Fred bought the Hoff & Stoller partnership and
named the store Bauer and Sons.

On April 20, 1912, John married Lillian Heerwald and to this couple two
children were born: Duane, born Jan. 23, 1914 married Dagney Nelson. The
Duane Bauer family now reside in Savanna, Ill. Irene, born Aug. 30,
1913, married Leonard Rickard and they are living in Provo, South
Dakota.

The John Bauer family were faithful members of the Reformed church.
John remained working in the store and also did some crop farming on
the Bauer farm. After the death of his wife, Lillian, he remarried. On
Dec. 9, 1919, John married Lydia Fiechtner, who was born Jan. 26, 1902
on a farm three miles east of Java, where she grew up and attended
grade school for four years. Lydia went to Mobridge to work and also
attended school there, but later quit school and went to work full
time, until she got married.

John and Lydia had four children. One son, LaVerne, born May 22, 1922,
served his country in World War II? as a Flight Officer and Pilot of a
B-29, and was killed in China in 1944. A daughter, Marjorie (Mrs.
Herbert Buechler), born Jan. 28, 1924, is teaching in the Bowdle school
and lives on a farm north of Bowdle. Della, (Mrs. Julian Ackerman),
born June 19, 1926, taught school at Wessington Springs, Webster,
Herreid and Oceanside, Calif. They now reside at Harlingen, Texas.
Delores, (Mrs. Harvey Teske), born March 4, 1928, was employed at
Daytons in Minneapolis, and taught school for one year before she was
married. She passed away in 1968 in Aberdeen. The Bauer family have 13
grandchildren and 7 great grandchildren.

John was owner and operator of Bauer and Sons until 1960 when he passed
away. Lydia remained living in Java, where she still resides. She was a
life long member of the St. Pauls Lutheran Church, became a member of
the Ladies Aid in 1945, now the A.L.C.W., and served four years as
local group chairman of India Missions and two years as group chair-
man. She was appointed District Group Chairman of North and South
Dakota, where she served for two years and also was a charter member of
the Church Altar Guild, which was organized in 1960 and is still a
member.

                GOTTLIEB BAMESBERGER

Gottlieb Bamesberger was one of the many pioneers of the Java territory
in its early beginning. Gottlieb was born July 16, 1880 in
Hoffnungstal, South Russia, and was the son of Christian and Katherina
(Orman) Bamesberger. He remained with his parents until he was 15 years
old and then took up a woodworking trade, which consisted of making
furniture and building things. This was Gottlieb's work for the next 5
years, until he became 20 years of age, and was military draft age for
the Russian army. This idea was rejected by Gottlieb, so he decided to
come to America and within a short time he and another friend, George
Reuer, got passes to come to America in April, 1900. They left
Bremerhafen Port and sailed by ship (F. Missler) and landed in Port New
York, then traveled by train to Bowdle, which was the end of the
Milwaukee railroad. He worked as a farmhand for one year.

The town of Java was being planned and in a short time many families
and laborers moved to the prospective new town. Gottlieb enhanced the
idea of doing carpenter work, so decided to come to Java and
immediately went to work. He was in the carpenter trade for about 9
years and helped design many houses, barns and granaries in the Java
area, many of which are still standing at this time.

        Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Bamesberger

In his early years in Java, he became one of the baseball players for
the Java baseball team and was one of their pitchers. This was one of
Gottlieb's favorite sports and always found time to play. In 1904 he
was united in marriage to Katherine Haux in the Java Lutheran church by
Rev. Kirchdoerfer.

They lived in Java until 1910 when they decided to go into farming, so
Gottlieb and his wife and two children, Christ and Lydia moved to the
Lowry area. They endured many hardships, but always remarked that
America was the "Land of Opportunity."

                MR. AND MRS. JACOB BENDER

Jacob Bender was born in the village of Worms, Odessa District, South
Russia, on August 5, 1863. Here he received his education and grew to
young manhood.

In 1886 he made the decision to leave his native land of Russia and
sought to make his new home in America. Coming to Yankton, Dakota
Territory, he settled there for about two years, living with relatives.
In 1888 he came north to homestead in Campbell county, filing on the
SE 1/4 of Section 6, Township 125, Range 74.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Bender

In order to maintain an income during these early homestead days, Mr.
Bender hired out breaking horses and made his mark as an accomplished
horseman. He made trips to Montana, selecting and purchasing wild
broncos, brought them back to Eureka, S.D., where he broke them and in
turn they were sold to area farmers.

On November 24, 1890, Jacob was married to Elizabeth Hauck, the
daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Karl Hauck. Mrs. Bender was born October
3,1865 in South Russia and came to the United States with her parents
in the spring of 1890. The young couple made their home on the El/2 of
Section 8, Township 125, Range 74, in Campbell county, which remained
their home until they retired from farming. On September 22, 1921, Mr.
and Mrs. Bender moved into a fine new residence in Java, where they
resided until their passing, Mrs. Bender on December 19, 1934 and Mr.
Bender on August 18, 1939.

Mr. and Mrs. Bender were the parents of 10 children: Elizabeth (Mrs.
Gottlieb Zeller), Emily (Mrs. Jacob B. Aman), Emma (Mrs. Edward F.
Biel), August, Lydia (Mrs. Fred Stoebner), Carl (died in infancy),
Albert, Henry, Martha (Mrs. Henry H. Wolff), and Edward (died at age 4).
Henry and Martha are the only surviving children and both are presently
residing in Java.

        Fred Bieber family, back, Arthur, Emilie, Ottilie, Magdalene,
        Herbert. Front, Gustave, Mr. and Mrs. Bieber, Albert.

                FRED BIEBER

Fred Bieber was born in Gluckstal, South Russia, Dec. 20, 1879, and
came to America in 1884 with his parents, Johann Jacob Bieber and his
wife Christiana (Ritter) Bieber. They settled near Eureka, S.D. In
1902, he married Rosina Hausauer who was born Dec. 22, 1882 at
Nuesselrodt, South Russia, a daughter of Karl Hausauer and Johanna
(Klein) Hausauer. She came to America with her parents in 1885, who
also had settled in the Eureka area. After their marriage they lived
two years with his parents on their homestead five miles northeast of
Eureka. During this time a son was born who died in infancy. Later a
daughter, Emilie (Mrs. Ferdinand Walz) was born who now lives in
Bowdle, S.D. They moved southeast of Java on the Parker farm which is
now owned by the Dale Bieber family. Later they moved to the James Kane
place. During this time, a son, Arthur Friedrich was born who later
died from a relapse of measles at the age of nearly three years. After
two years they bought the Bieber homestead and moved back to Eureka.
This home is still in existence and is occupied by a nephew. Edward
Hausauer.

The children born here were Gustave W. (who married Marie Schreter) and
now lives in Mobridge, S.D. Magdalene (Mrs. Henry E. Schreter) who
lives in Java and Ottilie (Mrs. Oscar Schnaible who also lives in
Java.)

He sold his farm in Eureka and in the spring of 1911 he bought a farm
seven miles south of Java and move on it. Here the three younger
children were born. Arthur F. married Letha Haskeil, living on and
owning the home place; Herbert F., married Henrietta Sayler (deceased)
and remarried to Mrs. Pauline Maule and living eight miles south of
Java; Albert J., married Marie Link and living at Guttenberg, Iowa.

During the thirties there were hard times, low prices, drought, dust
storms, grasshoppers, not enough feed for the livestock and not enough
