money to pay taxes. The rains came and things seemed to be brighter.

Mr. Bieber was also actively associated with church and community
affairs. He was one of the original stockholders of the Java Equity
Exchange, and served as a director of that organization for many years.
He was also a schoolboard member of Ryan School District.

They retired and moved to Java in Oct., 1939. After they moved to Java,
they lived a more leisurely life, enjoying their family and friends and
also spending some time fishing and gardening.

On April 28, 1949, Mr. Bieber passed away. In 1972 Mrs. Bieber
celebrated her 90th birthday with her children, grandchildren, great
grandchildren and great, great grandchild and friends. She passed away
Oct. 12, 1974.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob C. Bieber

                JACOB C. BlEBER

Jacob C. and Eva (Perman) Bieber were born, raised and attended schools
on farms about ten miles south and southeast of Java. Jacob was born in
1896 and Eva Perman in 1900. They were married Dec. 31, 1918 and
settled on a farm ten miles south and 1/4 mile east of Java, where they
farmed and raised cattle.

Their ten children were all born while they lived on the farm and all
were members of St. Paul Lutheran church in Java.

The first years of their married life were rough going as they had very
few of the conveniences that we have today. Cow chips, corn cobs and
old wood were used for fuel as coal was expensive and few families
could afford it.

Then came the "Dirty Thirties" starting about 1933 through 1936. The
Biebers were fortunate for not having any of their cattle shot, on
orders of the government, as many had, because of lack of feed. They
bought and shipped in hay from out of the state and later rented
pasture in the Conde area and southeast of Gettysburg.

They, with the help of the family, milked twenty-five to thirty cows
which supplied the food and the clothing for the family. There were no
gardens because of the drought. The dust would blow so hard the sky
would be dark obstructing the sunlight. Lights would have to be burned
during the day. The harvest wasn't much either, as there were about as
many thistles as stalks of grain in a bundle, and grasshoppers were
thick, too, taking what little grain there was left. In 1936 many
horses and some humans contacted encephalitis (sleeping sickness) in
South Dakota. In the summer of 1937 an outbreak of Anthrax took eleven
of the best milk cows and other cattle including some of the neighbors.

A happier highlight of the "Thirties" was a trip to the Worlds Fair in
Chicago, late in 1933.

In 1945 the Jacob C. Biebers retired and moved to Gettysburg where they
bought a home.

Their ten children were: Martha married Jacob Heier and live in Selby,
S.D.; Rueben (deceased) married Irene Schneider; Ellora, married Jack
Patterson and live in Boulder, Colo.; Alma, married Alvin Heier and
live in Onida S.D.; Meta, married Vern Overgaard and live in Edina
Minn.; Duane lives in Omaha, Neb., Venoye married Duane Hughes and live
on the homestead; Jerome, lives in Onida, S.D., Henrietta, married Wm.
Wright and lives in Frazer, Mich.; and Kenneth lives in Onida, S.D.
The Biebers have forty grandchildren and 17 great grandchildren.

In 1950 Mr. Bieber started raising Registered Herefords and is to date
in partnership with his sons.

                CHRISTOPH BIEL

Christoph Biel was born Jan. 7, 1877 at Golgalofore, South Russia. In
1894 he came to America with his parents and settled in the Sutley
Community, north of Java.

Karolina Keller came to America in 1880 with her parents and they
settled in the Campbell county area. On Dec. 11, 1900 she was united in
marriage to Christoph Biel. They resided on a farm in the Sutley
Community until 1929 when they bought a home in Java, to live a retired
life.

To this couple were born four children, three sons and one daughter who
were:

Bertha (deceased) married Jacob Kary.

Theodore C. Biel married Martha Zimmerman, now living in Mobridge, S.D.

Fred C. Biel married Dorothy Huber and now living in Java .

Leo Biel (deceased) married Ella Wolff and she now resides in Mobridge,
S.D.

On Nov. 11, 1938, Christ Biel suffered a stroke which was later
followed by a serious stroke in 1947, and never regained his health
completely and passed away in December, 1948. Mrs. Biel passed away in
January, 1952 after a lingering illness .

A grandson, Earl Biel, is living on the homestead at the present time.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christoph Biel

        Fredrick Biel family picture

                FREDRICK BIEL

Fredrick Biel was born at Odessa, South Russia on Feb. 1, 1869. He grew
up to young manhood and was united in marriage to Barbara Oltmar, who
was born Jan. 21, 1870 and also lived in Odessa, South Russia.

The Fredrick Biel family left their home in Anendahl, Russia and
undertook the long journey to come to the United States. One daughter
Louisa and Fredrick's parents accompanied them on their trip to the
United States. They landed in New York Port in April, 1894 then
traveled by train to Roscoe, S.D., and later went to Eureka, where
they joined other friends and relatives.

The Biel family homesteaded 7 miles north of Java, which is now the
Floyd Biel family home in Campbell county. They farmed numerous years
and also raised a family of 2 sons and 3 daughters who were:

        Louisa (Mrs. Christ Rau)
        Edward F., now residing in Mobridge
        Emma (Mrs. John Kary)
        Theodore F.
        Lydia (Mrs. Carl Rieb)

Times progressed and Fredrick bought another farm which is known as the
Ted F. Biel farm. He farmed until 1919 when he bought a home in Java,
and led a retired life.

                EDWARD F. BIEL

Edward F. Biel was born Oct. 6, 1896 and attended the Sutley school. He
helped his parents on the farm and later was drafted into service in
1917 during World War I. Edward served three months in battlefield at
France, and served in the Army of Occupation in Germany for 6 months. He
returned home and was discharged June 7, 1919. In October, 1919
Edward married Emma Bender and they took over his father's homestead.

Edward and his wife farmed for many years and raised a family of four
children and educated them in the Java Public school. Shortly after
Mrs. Biel's death, Edward purchased a home in Mobridge, where he still
resides.

The Biel children were:

Elmer married Clara Pfaff and live in Howard, S.D. They had one son,
Lynn married; and one daughter, Jill, married.

Deloris (deceased) married Albert Schreter and had one daughter.

Floyd married Lorraine Bieber and are living on his father's farm. They
had six children: Connie (Mrs. Dan Bauer); Steve, Marsha, Danny,
Sherilyn and Lori.

Rev. Kenneth married Caroline Levie, and has 2 sons and 2 daughters.
They now reside at Rock Springs, Wyo.

        Ed F. Biel family, Floyd, Emma, Elmer, Ed F., Deloris and
        Kenneth Biel.

                GOTTLIEB BOEHLER

Gottlieb Boehler, a native of Rohrback, South Russia was born Dec. 4,
1872 to Elizabeth (nee Bachman) and Phillip Boehler. The family
ancestors came from Germany. When political and social conditions in
Russia made it an unpleasant place to live for the freedom-loving
Germans, many decided to seek new homes in America. He came to America
at the age of 16, making the trip with a large party of other emigrants
to stay with his oldest sister, Sophia, Mrs. John Ochsner at Sutton,
Neb. Several years later he came to the Dakota Territory, where he
filed claim on a homestead for himself, consisting of 160 acres, 2-1/2
miles west of the now City of Java. He was married to Eva Wolf, who had
emigrated with her father, Fred Wolf Sr., who was a schoolmaster, and
her mother, Elizabeth (nee Milea Wolf) died at Marienberg, South
Russia. Mr. Wolf had filed claim on a homestead in this same area.
Eureka, S.D., then was the nearest railroad town that had their first
train service, July 27, 1887.

The young couple were happy and contented; however, as time went on
gradually paid off their debts and saved money to buy more land. With
perseverance and hardship coupled with plain hard work a comfortable
sod house was established and served its purpose well. In 1905 a wooden
house was built, which at this time is still in use at the homestead
where the sons, David, Emil and Rueben continue to till the bountiful
soil.

        Gottlieb Boehler family, back, Emma; Gottlieb Jr., Gottlieb,
        Louis, Elizabeth, Mrs. (Eva) Boehler, Lieta, Linda.

Their family of 12 children consisted of 7 sons and 5 daughters: Lydia
died in childhood ; Emma, Mrs. John Schuh Jr., Nov. 9, 1953; Sons,
Gottlieb, Oct. 31, 1962; Louis, April 24, 1969; Jacob, Jan. 9, 1965;
Arthur, Aug. 3, 1973. The living are: Linda, Mrs. Fred Pregitzer, of
Java; Elizabeth Turner, of Indiana; David, Emil, Rueben of Java; Lieta,
Mrs. R. H. Calkins, Grand Junction, Colo. Our beloved father, Gottlieb
Sr., died April 25, 1949, and mother, Eva, Dec. 7, 1955.

        Mr. and Mrs. Michael Buechler

                MR. AND MRS. MICHAEL BUECHLER

Mr. and Mrs. Michael Buechler, nee Magdaline Herman, were married in
Russia in 1878, and came to America in 1882. They came to the Java area
and homesteaded on a farm about 10 miles east of Java, which is now the
John F. Schilling farm. They went through long hardships while trying
to raise a family of five children. In 1921 they bought a home in
Bowdle, and retired a leisurely life. The children were:

Katherine, married Jacob Himmerich and still lives in Java.

Maggie married Jacob Gross and is living in Bowdle, at age 91, with her
daughter, Mrs. Alvin Reuer.

Jacob, John and Henry, all deceased.

In 1938, the Michal Buechlers celebrated their 60th wedding anniversary
in Bowdle, with their children, grandchlldren and great-grandchildren.

                HENRY BUCKENBERGER

Henry Buckenberger was born Aug. 9, 1878 in Menno, S.D.: married Marie
Merkel in Feb. 25, 1900. She was born in Marienberg, South Russia on
Dec. 13, 1873. She came to America with her parents in 1899, residing
in Campbell county.

They lived on a farm in Campbell county after their marriage until
1909, when they moved to Java. They had 9 children, 2 of whom died in
infancy.

Henry first operated a meat market. Later, he started a harness shop on
the east side of Main street, beside the present Postoffice. He sold
this to Helm and moved to what is now the Lumber Yard. He operated the
Hardware Store and Harness shop with his oldest son in 1922. This was
the present Red Owl store. He sold this to Baer of Bowdle, and he and
his son went across the river for one year to break land. Coming back,
he started a shoe shop.

In 1936 both his wife and son Albert died of food poisoning. He was a
Rawleigh salesman at this time. Later he moved to Kennewick, Wash., for
several years. Coming back, he started his shoe shop again in the Lang
building. Going to Washington again, coming back and set up another
shop in the Shock building. In 1945 he sold out completely and went to
Washington to live with his daughter.

Several years later, he came back and lived with several children
before going to the rest home in Eureka. He was there one week when he
entered the Eureka hospital, where he passed away of pneumonia on Nov.
19, 1950.

The children were:

John A., born April 15, 1901 at Sutley, died Feb. 14, 1965 was married
to Dorothy Wolff, Sept. 29 1921. She passed away in June on 1934. He
later married Ella Wolff on Feb. 14, 1936. For a time he worked in the
garage. He was in the hardware store with his father, farmed in
Campbell county and worked for the Milwaukee railroad. He lived in
Bowdle for a time, then moved to Java. They have 3 children, Calvin,
Kennewick, Wash., who is married and has 5 children, Mike, Patty,
Terry, Bobby and Veronica. They are all married. Violet, married to
Oliver Goetz, Java. They have 2 children, Carol (Mrs. Al Kringstad)
who has one son, Scott; and Jeanne, both L.P.N's living in Des Moines,
Iowa. LaMoyle, married to Calvin Aman, Java. They have 6 children,
Kathy (Mrs. Gary VanderVorst), Hudson, Wisc., Craig, Nancy, David,
Cheryl, and Patricia. One daughter Teresa passed away Nov. 9, 1969,
when she was 7 years old.

Arthur R., born May 19, 1902 at Sutley. Died 1972 at Kennewick, Wash.;
married Mary Arbach in 1938. He went to barber school and worked in
Milwaukee. Later he moved to Kennewick, Wash., and worked for the Welch
Grape Co. They have one son Ronald, who is living in Seattle. He is
married and has 2 children.

Edward F., born March 25, 1906 in Sutley; worked for the Milwaukee
Railroad at Java. Later he moved to Kennewick, Wash., and worked for
the S.P. & S. railroad. He then worked for the Big Y Cannery, and then
worked for the Welch Grape Co., until his retirement. He married
Pauline Teske. Later he was married to Viola Rieger. They have 2
children.

Martin, born Dec. 7, 1907 in Sutley, married Frieda Naasz Nov. 7, 1933.
He farmed with brother John for several years in Campbell county. He
started working for the Milwaukee railroad while living in Java. He was
transferred to Ipswich then to Bowdle. He retired from the railroad in
1953 and is presently working for Kurle and Sons Implement, starting in
1956. They have one son, LeRoy living in Rockford, Ill. He is married
to Jan Dehlin. They have 3 children, Marty, Roy and Kelly.

        The Henry Buckenberger family and others in early Java Main
        street scene.

Albert, born Feb. 25,1909, in Java and died June 18,1936 of food
poisoning. He graduated from Java high school and then worked for the
Milwaukee railroad until the time of his death.

Alvina, born Sept. 26, 1912 in Java, graduated from Java high school
and then went to Kennewick, Wash., with her brother Ed. She married
Alvin Martell of Kennewick, where they still reside. They have one son,
Jim, who is married and has 2 children.

Ben A., born March 22, 1914 at Java. He graduated from Java high
school. He bought the garage from Louie Helm. He sold out and went into
partnership with Herbert Fiechtner, operating a recreation parlor in
the Dave Blinderman store. He later sold his share to Herbert
Fiechtner. In 1936 he married Hazel Nies. They moved to Kennewick,
Wash. He went to Walla Walla and worked for Montgomery Ward, then was
transferred to Eureka, Calif. Later he started his own clothing store
in Fresno. He has now branched out to more stores. He is in partnership
with his brother-in-law, Dick Nies in a trailer court at Pebble Beach,
Calif. They have a home in Fresno and Carmell. They have 3 children,
Bob, married and has 2 children; Larry, married, 2 children and Sherry,
married, one child. They all live in California.

        The Campbell family in 1914

                THE CAMPBELL FAMILY

In 1904 or early 1905, Wilbur E. Campbell, his wife Mae, two daughters,
Verna and Eunice, in the company of his brother, Albert G. Campbell and
family came to the Bowdle area from Denison, lowa, to be near an older
brother, Walter J. Campbell and family, who operated a general store in
Bowdle, and later in Selby.

Albert G. became a Methodist minister in the Black Hills region. Wilbur
E. farmed in the Bowdle, Java, Selby, area until 1913 when the family
moved to Java, purchasing the Java Hotel business from the Zeller
Sisters. Later, Mae Campbell purchased the Rosenthal building and
continued the hotel in that location.

Wilbur's activities included auto mechanics with Schlepp Garage,
operation of the Java Light Plant, the south rural mail route and
operating the separator for John Wolff's threshing outfit each fall for
about 20 years.

The Campbells were divorced in 1928. Wilbur remarried, moved to
Memphis, Neb., where he died in April, 1933.

Mae married Dwight Hall in 1943, lived in Portland until his death. She
returned to Billings and died there in June, 1957.

Verna married Arthur J. Stuart in 1921. They lived in Billings, Mont.,
until her death in December, 1947.

Eunice married Orville L. Griggs in December, 1925. They moved to
Billings in 1930. Orville retired as Senior Marketing Assistant of
Humble Oil Co., Jan. 1, 1968. He had been with the company for almost
thirty years. They continue their residence in Billings, where
Orville conducts a commercial photography business from his home. The
family consisting of a daughter, son and daughter-in-law, and six
grandsons, live in Billings.

        Carl F. Clement       Mrs. Carl F. Clement

                CLEMENT FAMILY

The Clement family were among the early pioneers of Java, coming in
1903 when they established the Java Herald, a two page weekly newspaper
printed one half in German and the other half in English. Equipment was
from the old Rising Sun at the then county seat of Bangor.

Carl F. Clement, with his wife, Kate, and three children, Carl W.,
Freya and Fritz, had spent the winter months at Canning, S.D., where
Mr. Clement taught a country school. That summer Mr. Clement worked on
a newspaper in Eureka, but gave a Fourth of July speech in German at a
celebration in the new town of Java, and at that time, he was asked if
he would start a newspaper there. Money was raised and the first issue
of the Java Herald made its appearance in September of 1903.

The Clement family came to South Dakota from Ohio where Mr. Clement had
operated several newspapers and when he wasn't operating a newspaper,
he was teaching school. He was superintendent of the Napoleon city
schools and also at several other cities in Ohio. He also graduated
from a law school and practiced law in addition to operating a
newspaper, both in Ohio and in South Dakota. He was Java's first city
attorney and mayor, and later became states attorney for Walworth
county for a number of terms.

Mr. Clement was born May 14,1859 at Lockport, N.Y., and received his
education at Wartburg Seminary, Dubuque, Iowa, where he studied for the
Lutheran ministry. He graduated in 1880 but his love for the newspaper
business led him to give up the ministry. He operated a music store for
several years in Nebraska before returning to Ohio and then came to
South Dakota in 19102 where his family soon joined him. Mr. Clement
passed away March 23, 1922.

        Fritz Clement   Mrs. Fritz Clement

Mrs. Clement was born July 31, 1869 in Sidney, Ohio and she married Mr.
Clement, Feb. 2, 1895. In addition to the three children mentioned
above, another daughter, Marguerite (Gretchen) was born in 1906. She is
now Mrs. M. C. Munson, living in Madison, Wis. Two daughters, by a
previous marriage of Mr. Clement were Mrs. H. C. Mains (Ella) and Mrs.
C. O. Reed (Laura). Both lived in Java for a number of years, where
Laura taught elementary school. Both are now deceased. Freya, who
passed away in 1923, also was an elementary teacher in the Java
schools. Mrs. Clement passed away in 1958 at Selby.

Carl W. Clement passed away in February, 1972 at Millbrae, Calif.,
where he lived with his wife, Mildred (Hadley) and daughter, Leilani,
for many years after spending about 10 years in Hawaii, where he was an
electronics engineer with the U. S. Navy.

Only members of the family still alive are Fritz, who operates the Java
Herald and Selby Record, and has lived in Selby since he acquired the
Record in 1945 with his wife, Ida (Krause) and two children, Bruce and
Germaine (Bunny) who is now married and lives with her husband, Wendel
Mead and two daughters, Mary and Linda in Bowie, Maryland; and Gretchen
(Mrs. M. C. Munson) whose husband was a former Java school
superintendent. Gretchen was a member of Java's famous girls basketball
team.

The Clements were active in civic affairs while living in Java. Both
the father and son, Fritz, took part in local activities and Fritz
was a member of the city council for fourteen years. Carl F. Clement
was a lover of music and organized many musical groups during the early
days of Java when most of the entertainment for the area people came
from local talent groups.

        James Corson, Alice Corson, Grace Corson, Merl Corson and Kezia
        Corson.

                MR. AND MRS. JAMES CORSON

James C. Corson was born in Shelby county, Ohio, on Sept. 23,1856 and
Kezia Young was also born in the same county on Aug. 17,1862. They were
married at Montra, Ohio on April 23, 1881. The oldest child, Martha
Alice, was born in Shelby county, Ohio, on Jan. 11, 1883 and moved with
her parents to South Dakota in 1886.

The Corson family homesteaded on some land 2 miles east of Java, and
the first sod house was built 14 years before the town of Java was
started. They later moved into Java and their home is now the Mrs.
Lewis Hicks residence.

Thurman Corson built the first grain elevator on the Milwaukee
railroad. Corson county was named after J. C. Corson, who also worked
for the railroad, selling lots in new towns as the railroad extended
westward. Mr. Corson was also the first landowner in the new town of
Java. He and one of his brothers owned the first threshing machine in
Walworth county, an old Buffalo Pitts steam engine and a Red River
separator. Many a long winter was spent moving from one farm to another
threshing wheat that had been in stacks for months waiting for this
machine to arrive.

The rest of the children were born to the Corson family while they were
living in the Java area. Alice, Grace, and Merl were all educated in
the Java school, and Gerald also spent four terms in the Java school,
commuting from Texas to South Dakota. The long cold winters caused an
illness that forced them to move to a warmer climate and in 1910 a move
was made to Texas.

In the meantime, Alice was born in Ohio, was married to Gerrit Noble of
Java, and one son, Leonard was born Nov. 27, 1904. Gerrit passed away
in 1938 and Alice in 1974. Leonard is now retired, lost his wife in
1973 and now spends most of his time with a daughter in Hot Springs, S.
D., or at a fishing camp in upper Minnesota.

Grace, who was born in the Java area on May 28,1888 was married to
Arthur Johnson, who was an engineer for the Milwaukee railroad. He was
killed in a train wreck in 1910. They also had one son, Howard, born in
1909 and married Alma Merkel in 1928. They moved to Texas where Howard
passed away in 1964. They also had one son, Jack, who is married and
has three children. After Arthur's death, Grace moved to Texas, where
she remarried and when she died in 1972, she left a family of 2 boys
and 2 girls and a number of grandchildren.

Merl, who was born in Java, on Feb. 23,1887 was married to Leafy Ruyon
in 1907. Three years later they moved to Texas with his folks and
raised a large family of nine children, of which four were burned to
death in a fire that destroyed his home in 1925. Leafy passed away in
1961 and Merl passed away in 1964, leaving five children and a large
number of grandchildren, some of who are boys that will carry on the
Corson name in Texas.

Gerald, born in Java, S.D., on March 8, 1903, was married to the
former Alma Merkel Johnson and they reside in Houston, Texas.

James C. Corson died in Houston, Texas on April 12, 1938 at the age of
82 years and his wife, Kezia died in Houston, Texas on April 21, 1949
at the age of 87 years.

        Mr. and Mrs. Adam Eisenbeisz

                ADAM EISENBEISZ

Adam Eisenbeisz was born June 2,1882 in South Russia and was a son of
Christian and Rosina Eisenbeisz. At the age of 15 years he came to
America with his parents, the family settling on a farm northeast of
Java, where they resided for 11 years.

March 16, 1905 he married Margaretha Pfitzer at the Lutheran church in
Java. In 1908 they moved to Akaska, S.D., where he was engaged in
farming for eleven years. In 1919, they moved back to Java. In the
spring of 1920, Adam, Mathias and Julius Pfitzer purchased a business
from Carl Hepper, known for many years as Pfitzer and Eisenbeisz. In
1928, Adam purchased full interest and operated a recreation parlor
with his son, which was under the firm name of Adam Eisenbeisz and Son.
Failing in health persuaded Adam to retire in 1947.

This couple had three daughters, who were Katherine married Fred Goetz,
and residing in Java; Martha, residing at the old home place in Java;
Margaret (deceased) married Joe Mueller, who had one daughter, now
residing in Aberdeen. One son, Arthur, married Rose Stoecker, now
resides in Java, and is in insurance business. They have 2 sons: Virgil
married Joann Wolff, and Gene, married Ann Engelhardt, and they all
reside in Aberdeen, S.D.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Fiechtner

                MR. AND MRS. JOHN FIECHTNER

John Fiechtner was born August 23, 1891 at Bridgewater, South, Dakota,
the son of Gottlieb and Karolina (Spiry) Fiechtner, who immigrated to
South Dakota in 1886 from South Russia (originally of German descent).
His family remained in the Bridgewater vicinity until 1893, and came to
Walworth county and homesteaded about three miles east of Java
townsite. John remained on the homestead and took over the
responsibility of operating it after the death of his father in 1908.
His mother sold the farm in 1918 and moved to Java. His grandfather,
Gottlieb Fiechtner also lived on the homestead in a little house by
himself.

Emelia Naasz was born September 24, 1894 on the Naasz homestead in
Walworth county, five miles east of the Java townsite, the daughter of
George and Magdalena (Graf) Naasz. Her parents immigrated from South
Russia (originally of German descent), and arrived on their homestead
May 5, 1893. They built a two room sod house, and this remained their
home until they built a three room house with a summer kitchen that was
built with stone, straw and mud. An additional room was built on later
for her grandmother Naasz, and she lived there for awhile. Emelia
moved to Java with her parents at age 13 in 1907.

John and Emelia were united in marriage on November 18, 1913. They
moved on the Naasz homestead, having purchased it from her parents.
Their three children: Herbert, Irene, and Gertrude, were born there.
The years of farming were lean, and they could not keep up the
payments. They left the farm and lived briefly in Mobridge and then
Java. After that, they moved onto the Kapfenstein farm and from there
to a farm four miles southeast of Java. Living there for five years,
the children attended the Spring Valley school. Then in 1930 when the
depression hit, they moved to Java, working wherever jobs were
available. Their children graduated from Java High School, and then
married. After a few more years of farming, they moved to Mobridge and
purchased a house. John worked near Provo on a Defense project. Then he
worked for the Milwaukee Railroad in Mobridge for almost ten years
before he died in June, 1948. Emelia stayed in Mobridge until 1951 when
she sold her house and moved to Lodi, California, where she still
resides.

One incident that remained clearly in their minds was the Flu Epidemic
of 1918. They went from one place to another to look in on the sick and
give assistance. Flu hit the Adolph Naasz home south of Java,--people
living there at the time were as follows: Mr. and Mrs. Naasz and their
children, Ted, Art, Adolph Jr., Alvina, Emil, Esther, Herman and Irene;
Mr. and Mrs. Jake Fiechtner (Lydia Sackman) and their children, Hertha
and Alvina; and Grandma Sackman (Mrs. John Sackman) and her children,
Tillie, Andreas, Reinhold and Herman. All of them were sick in bed
except Adolph Naasz. This house was sorrowed with the death of four of
the occupants, Mrs. Jake Fiechtner, Alvina and Esther Naasz, and
Grandma Sackman. The Karolina Fiechtner family had the flu also. The
people that resided there were as follows: Rose, Minnie, Bill, Lydia,
Mary and Emelia. Rose and Minnie came from Kansas for a visit when the
flu hit. Jake Fiechtner and two children moved back here too, after the
death of his wife. And then at John and Emelia's home which they shared
with Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Fiechtner on the Naasz homestead. The
following were sick with the flu: their children, Herbert and Irene;
Gottlieb and Christina and their two sons, Emil and Albert. Emelia
didn't get sick with the flu until about a month later, after everyone
was well; but her sickness wasn't too serious.

                The Fiechtner Children

Herbert married Ida Klein. They farmed east of Java until they retired
in 1973 and are now living in Bowdle. They have six children, Phonia,
Terry, John, Connie, Joel and Barbara. Phonia married Harvey Dietrich,
who is a principal at Eureka High. He has a Master's Degree in
Education Administration and teaching. She worked in the A.S.C. offices
in Brown and Jones counties, and taught a school in Walworth
county for one year. They have two children David and Susan. Terry
married Arliss Schilling. He graduated from the School of Mines and
Technology with a degree in Chemical Engineering. He worked for DuPont
in Davenport, Iowa, and is now with N.A.S.A. He was with the Apollo
program and is now working on the Space Shuttle program. Arliss is a
registered nurse graduating from Minneapolis General Hospital. She is
the nursing instructor at the Deruvard County Hospital in Florida.
Their children are Curtis, Lisa and Amy. John married Julie Rath of
Aberdeen. He has a B. S. Degree in Education, graduating from Northern
State College. He worked on his Master's Degree at Grand Forks and
Fargo. He was Superintendent of Schools at McHenry, North Dakota, and
is now employed at Farmland Industries in Monte Vista, Colorado. Julie
attended the School of Commerce in Aberdeen. Their children are Greg
and Pam. Connie married Rodney Blumhardt. He graduated from Northern
State College with a B. S. Degree in Education, and is now employed
with the Farmers Union Insurance Co., in Denver, Colorado. Connie
attended the School of Commerce in Aberdeen, and was employed by the
Aberdeen School System as a secretary for several years. Their children
are Mark, Kristi, and Sara. Joel married Kathy Mittleider. He attended
Northern State College for three years. They now live on the family
farm, and are engaged in dairy farming. Kathy is a registered nurse,
attended Presentation College, and is now employed at the Bowdle
Nursing Home. They have one child, Jamie Jo. Barbara married Norby
Reuer. He graduated from Northern State College majoring in biology. He
is teaching and coaching in White River, South Dakota. Barbara took a
secretarial course at Northern State College and was employed at the
college. She is now a teacher's aide in White River, and is working on
her Bachelor's Degree in Education.

Irene married E. Levi Krause. They are now retired, living in Mobridge.
They owned a jewelry store in Bowdle and then he was Social Welfare
Director of Edmunds county while living in Ipswich. Then he joined the
Navy during World War II. He was in the Navy Ship Repair Unit as a
Special Artificer Instruments. While residing in Mobridge, they owned a
watch repair business, and owned and operated Krause's Drive-In. He
attended Bradley Polytechnic Institute in 1927, and earned his watch
repair degree. They have four children, Russell, Judy, Kenneth and
Lorna. Russell married Margaret Wik of Aberdeen, S.D. He is a graduate
of School of Mines and Technology in Rapid City, majoring in Mechanical
Engineering. He worked for Montana-Dakota Utilities in Mobridge, S.D.,
and in Mandan, N.D., as their controls engineer. While in Mandan, he
received his Professional Engineering Degree. Then he accepted a job in
Jackson, Michigan as a consulting engineer, designing power plants. He
is presently the Project Manager for Charles T. Main Co., a consulting
engineer company, in Boston, Mass. Margaret did secretarial work in
Rapid City. They have four children, Linda, John, Greg, and Kathy. Judy
married James Halamka of Des Moines, Iowa. She graduated from Augustana
College at Sioux Falls, majoring in biology, and received her Master's
Degree in Biology from Drake University at Des Moines, Iowa. She taught
at Luther College in Waverly, Iowa. Jim also graduated with a Master's
Degree in Biology from Drake University, and is presently teaching in
Junior Community College in Port Huron, Michigan. Judy does some
part-time teaching at the same college. They have three sons, Steven,
Michael, and Eric. Kenneth married Beverly Zeller of Miller, S.D. He
graduated from Augustana College in Sioux Falls, S.D., with a degree
in physics. He is presently employed with the State Highway Department
in Soils and Research as a geophysicist. He is also in Computerized
Process Controls and Instrumentation. He received his Professional
Engineering Degree in August, 1974. Beverly is a graduate of the
University of South Dakota at Vermillion, S.D. She is a computer
programmer analyst for the State of South Dakota. They are residing in
Pierre. Lorna married Ed Watkins of St. Lawrence, S.D. She graduated
from South Dakota State University at Brookings, with a B.S. Degree in
Mathematics. She taught school in Selby. Ed also graduated from S.D.S.U.
with a degree in mechanized agriculture. He worked for the Co-operative
in Selby as a fertilizer specialist, and then at the First National
Bank in Selby as an agricultural representative. They now live on a
farm near St. Lawrence, where they are engaged in farming and ranching
with his brother and father. Lorna does part-time teaching in Miller.

Gertrude married Edgar Pietzke of Bowdle, S.D. They have resided in
California since 1941, mostly around the Stockton and Lodi area. They
owned a grape farm. Edgar is now in the trucking business, and they own
an apartment house in Stockton. Gertrude clerks in a clothing store, and
works part-time in a cannery. Their children are Brian, Marilyn and
Jerry. Brian attended college and was in the Military Service for two
years. He now owns an accounting firm in Stockton, Calif. Marilyn
attended college. She is a bank auditor working for the Wells Fargo
Bank in San Jose Calif. Jerry is attending college in Fresno, majoring
in science, and helps his father with trucking.

        Gottlieb Fiechtner I

                GOTTLIEB FIECHTNER I

Gottlieb Fiechtner I, was born March 7, 1830 and grew to young manhood
in the village of Hoffnungstal in the Kherson territory. Gottlieb
married Karolina Wahl, who was born April 10, 1830 and lived in the
southern Russian area for many years. They came to America when they
were in their early fifties and most of their children were grown up
and married.

They homesteaded land east of Java, near his son, Gottlieb II, and his
family, and farmed for a few years. His wife died a short time after
they came to America, then Gottlieb lived in a small home on his son's
farm. A number of years later, he came to live with his granddaughter,
Louisa (Mrs. Christ Sauer) and family. Several years later, he passed
away.

This couple were blessed with seven children, which were: Mathias a
doctor in Russia, and was married, but later passed away in Russia.

John married and later homesteaded in Nebraska.

Gottlieb II, married Karolina Spiry and homesteaded near Java.

Karolina married Christ Sauer Sr., who passed away, and she later came
to America with her two children, John and Christian Jr. She settled in
California, and later married Jacob Kapfenstein.

Katherina married Gottfried Zweigart and homesteaded in Bridgewater.

Christiana married Fred Bauman Sr., and homesteaded in Bridgewater, and
later five miles east of Java.

                GOTTLIEB FIECHTNER II

Gottlieb Fiechtner II, was born May 28, 1855 and grew up in the Kherson
Territory. He grew to young manhood and married Karolina Spiry in the
year, 1882. Karolina was the daughter of Fred Spiry Sr., who was born
March 22, 1863 and lived in the Ukraine area of Russia.

After several years of hard labor trying to make a living for his
family, Gottlieb and his wife and three children - Louise, Kary and
Gottlieb III, undertook the long journey to come to the promised land
of America. In 1886, they departed from the Odessa port and after
several weeks they landed in the port of Baltimore, Md. They remained
in Baltimore for some time, as the three children became very sick and
had to be hospitalized. Gottlieb's wife had to work in the hospital to
cover the necessary expenses.

        Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Fiechtner II

After leaving Baltimore, they journeyed by rail to the Scotland and
Bridgewater area to join other relatives. They were here a short time
when Gottlieb contacted his parents in Hoffnungstal to persuade them to
come to America as there was more land available to be homesteaded.
When the Fiechtner family had saved enough money they too journeyed to
America and landed at Port Detroit in 1893. They traveled by train to
the Bridgewater area to join his son, Gottlieb II and his family.

Several months later, Gottlieb and Karolina and their children: Louise,
Kary and Gottlieb III, moved to the Java area to be near Karolina's
parents. Gottlieb's parents and other relatives also made the move to
settle near the Java area. Gottlieb and his wife homesteaded four miles
east of Java, now the Gust Schaffer farm. It was there they built
another small house for Gottlieb's parents. Nine more children were
born to Gottlieb and Karolina, which were Jake, John, William, Katie,
Minnie, and Rose, all deceased; Mary is now living in California; Lydia
and Amelia now living in Java. Louise, the oldest child is still living
in Java, and Gottlieb and Kary passed away a number of years ago.

The children all grew up around the Java area and later married and
homesteaded on farms nearby. Before Gottlieb's death in 1908, he served
as Walworth county assessor for a number of years. His wife, Karolina
remained on the farm and later she bought a home in Java. In 1925 she
lived with her youngest daughter, Amelia until her death in 1952.

                GOTTLIEB FIECHTNER III

Gottlieb Fiechtner III was born in Hoffnungstal, South Russia on Sept.
24, 1884. He was a little boy when his parents undertook the trip to
come to America. It was a long but somewhat exciting to the three
Fiechtner children. They homesteaded in the Scotland and Bridgewater,
S.D., area in 1887. They settled amid friends and relatives that had
previously lived amongst them in South Russia.

In 1893 when Gottlieb was six years old, his parents moved to the Java
area, where his father and mother filed claim on some land east of
Java. Things looked promising for the Java area, due to the extension
of the railroad which was being built westward.

Gottlieb spent little time for school, as he had to help out with the
farming, which was done mostly by oxen and mules. He grew to young
manhood and later married Christine Naasz, who too, came to this
country with her parents, George Naasz II at the age of two years. They
were married April 4, 1907, in a small country church, near the
Fiechtner and Naasz homesteads. They lived on his father's homestead
for many years, which is now owned and farmed by Gust Schaffer. Four
children were born to the Fiechtners while they were in the Java area.
Gottlieb also owned and operated a livery stable in 1919, but after
three years, he sold it, and farmed until 1928 when the Fiechtner
family moved to Montana. They farmed in the Crow Rock area, which was 42
miles north of Miles City. No rainfall and hail and his wife's illness
forced Gottlieb to move back to South Dakota. They moved to Java, where
Gottlieb found employment with the railroad for many years and later
was employed at the City Power Plant until he retired. The family were
members of St. Paul Lutheran church.

The Fiechtner children were:

Emil (deceased) married Hilda Hirsch and had one daughter, Janet, who
is married and has one daughter and one son. They all live in Miles
City, Mont.

Albert, married Martha Hirsch, and had two sons, Jack and Lyle. Jack
married and has two sons and two daughters. Lyle married and has two
daughters. They all live in Miles City, Mont.

Gary married Phyllis Simpson and has two sons, Dennis and Bret. Dennis
married and Bret, at home, attending high school in Modesto, Calif.,
where they now live.

Erna, married Elmer Schlepp, and has one daughter, Marlys, and one son,
Douglas. Marlys married Neal Pfitzer and has one son. Douglas married
Lynette Hettich.

        Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Fiechtner III

                MR. AND MRS. GOTTFRIED FIEDLER

As a descendant of Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried Fiedler, I am proud of the
staunch hearts and spirit of our pioneering forefathers, whose ancestry
originated at Rosenberg in the Danzig area of East Prussia in Germany,
were among the early pioneers of Yankton county, Dakota Territory, the
state of South Dakota and Walworth county.

Gottfried Fiedler born, Jan. 22, 1857, the son of John Jacob Fiedler
and Anna Helena Martin-Fiedler and Karolina Reimer-Fiedler born Sept.
27, 1860 the daughter of John Reimer and Florentina Hildebrandt-Reimer
were both born in the village of Rosenberg in a colony in South Russia,
where their parents had migrated to in the early part of the 19th
century. Here they were to enjoy complete liberty in church and school,
local ordinances, military exemption and grants of Crown land, in
exchange for the tilling and planting of it and large groves of timber.
They built fine homes with more trees and orchards requiring much care,
but toward the close of each day they paused at the sound of the
Angelus from their parish church two miles away, that they walked to on
Sundays, enjoying the wild tulips, iris, violets, carnations, poppies
etc., along the way. Our paternal grandfather owned a Dutch windmill
and provided the villagers with a variety of flour, meal and grits,
built plain and fancy furniture, had handicrafts like basketweaving
that the children learned to do alongside their elders. Our maternal
grandfather was a horticulturist, a shoemaker, a postillion,
distributing the mail to many surrounding villages and a stage driver.
This led to the hauling of foods to the front lines during the Crimean
war, to within sound of cannonfire. After the war was over, both sets
of our grandparents migrated to the Crimea to establish new homes
there. That mild climate was perfect to grow a wide variety of fruits,
as well as other foods. Here our parents received most of their formal
and religious education, under their own schoolmasters. The higher
education in the large village of Zurichthal, nestled in the Yalta
Mountains, where they were also confirmed during the Easter season. Of
the subjects taught in their regular schools, reading, history and
geography were some of their favorites, learning much of the outside
world, especially America, that they heard much of from their parents.

After 10 or 12 years, when privileges were being renounced our
grandparents knew definitely that America was where they would go to
once more seek full freedom. It was not easy to leave their comfortable
homes, but after a year's preparation their whole village was ready to
leave by May, 1876. A cross-country train trip took them to Berlin and
Hamburg, where they embarked on one of the finest ocean liners of that
day, the Kaiser Wilhelm. After several weeks at sea, they reached their
port of entry, Ellis Island. A new experience there was to go to New
York City, via a submarine tunnel and about the city on an "elevated".
Another cross-country train trip brought them to their destination of
Yankton, D.T., which was the capitol. Getting their families settled in
town, our grandfathers set out to buy homes and found suitable places
not too far out. Having arrived in June, 1876, they found much
excitement due to the Black Hills gold rush but too, the countryside
was at it's best. Grandfather Fiedler was fortunate in obtaining a well
improved place with fine trees, just 1 mile north of Mission Hill. And
grandfather Reimer found an equally good place another mile east of
there, overlooking a wide fertile valley, but had only one tree in
their yard. That looked very desolate, having left so much beauty
behind, so improving was begun with the help of all of the family.

Our parents were married Nov. 18, 1878 and for a few years lived in a
small log cabin in grandparents Fiedler's yard. They were active in
community affairs and sang in the choir of their church, where
grandfather was the reader. As land became available nearby, they too
purchased a place of their own, a few miles farther out, with no
improvements on it. Lumber was hauled from Vermillion to build their
first frame house, granary etc. In the spring, trees were planted for
shade and fruit and the shelter belt contained 400 black walnut
seedlings of which most may still be alive after 90 years, as they were
not long ago. Ground was prepared for seeding, taking all of father's
time, leaving mother with the care of house, yard and garden, besides
small children to look after. As the season advanced and the work
increased, father purchased a reaper for himself and a sewing machine
for mother, each serving as an extra pair of hands to help them.

They prospered, built a new more modern roomier house and enjoyed this
home for 20 years before they felt the need of more land to provide for
their family. During this time, they experienced the big blizzard of
October 15, 1880, causing the destructive flood in the spring of 1881,
as well as the infamous blizzard of January 12, 1888, that paralyzed
the whole territory.

They moved to Freeman, S.D., which was their postoffice, and they
attended church and school in town. Having lost two infants, who were
buried at Mission Hill, another son was born to them here, Jan. 1,
1901. The very level land had attracted them, but after 3 years of
above normal rainfall and no drainage, saw another move was necessary.

They came to Java, in March of 1903 and located about 7 miles northwest
of town. The oldest son, who had a small business in town had married
by the end of the first year, as had their second daughter and both
lived in town, the oldest having just been married before they moved,
had remained at Freeman. In spite of this, the 3 room house they found
to be quite inadequate and though a 4 room addition was built on, did
not quite measure up to the better homes they had before. In 1904 their
youngest daughter was born and the last half of the family received
their public and religious education here. The school house was located
on their land 1/4 mile from the house and the little country church,
two miles, with the parish church in Java, where we were all confirmed.

Our parents provided a good home for us here, for 20 more years, before
they retired in 1924 and moved to Mobridge to build their last home.
They celebrated their Golden Wedding here Nov., 18, 1928, but could
share only 8 years here together before father became ill and passed
away May 1, 1932. Mother continued to live here for 15 more years until
in 1947 three more of her children moved to Canistota, S.D., she went
with them for company-sake, as most of the family had left this area
during the depression years. Mother lived there only 8 months when she
passed away suddenly on June 15, 1948. That left only the youngest
daughter in this part of the state and living in the parental home.
Besides the parents and the two infants, those that have passed on,
too, are: Son, Gottfried in 1918; daughters, Helena in 1930 and
Florentina in 1951; and sons Albert in 1951, John in 1960 and Henry in
1971.

Those still living are: Wilhelmina, Woodland, Calif., Caroline, Fargo,
N.D., Marie, Ludlow, S.D., Elizabeth, Fargo, N.D., Anna, Yakima, Wn.,
and Ruth, Mobridge, S.D.

        Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried Fiedler

                MR. AND MRS. MARTIN FIEDLER

Martin Fiedler was born in South Russia on Jan. 28, 1874. At the age of
two years he came to the United States with his parents, Mr. and Mrs.
Jacob Fiedler Sr., and the family settled in Yankton county, near
Mission Hill, S.D.

Mrs. Fiedler (nee Marie Pleinis) was born in South Russia, and at the
age of three years came to America with her parents, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob
Pleinis Sr. They homesteaded five miles north of Java. Marie worked very
hard and at the age of seventeen she was the head cook for a large
threshing crew that did a lot of threshing in the Bangor area of
Walworth county. Sometimes her little sister Emma (Haux) helped her
wash the dishes. She baked all of the bread, rolls, kuchen and pies for
the crew and prepared three big meals and two lunches a day in the
"cookcar" as it was called. Marie also spent several years working in
the Java Hotel in the early 1900's. She became the bride of Martin
Fiedler on Feb. 7, 1905 and they made their home on a farm near Mission
Hill, S.D., for twelve years.

In 1917, Mr. Fiedler purchased the Jacob Pleinis Sr., farm, located
five miles north of Java. The move took place in March of that year.
The family consisted of Mr. and Mrs. Fiedler and their five children,
Herman, Carl, Clara, Art and Harold. The move was a big ordeal, as it
was done by rail transportation. Each of the older children was
responsible for taking care of some of the personal belongings, while
taking this long train ride. Clara remembers carrying a little pail of
water containing the goldfish which she proudly showed to every
passenger that came on board.

It took a great deal of work to get settled. Two more children,
Gerhardt and Eileen were born within the next six years. Many
improvements were made on the farm. The biggest one was the
installation of a Delco light plant in 1927.

The years on the farm brought many good and happy times, but the "dirty
thirties" caused many heartaches, also.

In 1939, Mr. and Mrs. Fiedler retired and moved to Java. Their son,
Art, purchased the home farm, and the land surrounding it was farmed by
Art, Herman and Harold.

Mr. Fiedler passed away in April, 1945, and Mrs. Fiedler passed away in
August, 1970.

Mr. and Mrs. Fiedler were the parents of seven children. They are:
Herman, married to Emma Stangl, Carl to Adriane Kinney, Clara to Jake
Nehlich, Arthur to Irene Bieber, Harold to Clara Rosin, Gerhardt to
Viola Schadler, and Eileen to Reuben Schmierer.

        Mr. and Mrs. Martin Fiedler

        Mr. and Mrs. Philipp Flemmer

                PHILIPP FLEMMER

Philipp Flemmer was born on Oct. 1, 1868 in Gluckstal, South Russia. He
joined the migration to America in 1889 with the Martin Grosz family,
and filed on a claim eight miles north of Java, in the Hiddenwood area.
On Dec. 26, 1897, he married Katharina Schrum at Ashley, N.D.

Katharina Schrum Flemmer was born on May 26, 1880 in Beresina,
Bessarabia. She accompanied her parents to America in 1888.

Having acquired a team of horses, Philipp Flemmer started the first
drayline in Java. At times, he drove to Ipswich, then the end of the
railroad, and brought settlers into the Java area. One of these
pioneers was Rev. Braun, the first Pastor of the Lutheran church in
Java.

In 1905, the Philipp Flemmer family moved to Eureka, S.D., where he
worked for the Serr and Quast drayline concern.

Six children were born to the Flemmers: Christian of Eureka, S.D.;
Emma Becker of Stockton, Calif.; Adeline Stroup of Cottage Grove, Ore.;
Mary Hepper of Mobridge, S.D., Mrs. L. M. Graves (Ann) of Redfield, S.
D.; and Mrs. E. R. Kary (Gertrude) of Eureka, S.D.

                MARTIN GEHRKI FAMILY

Martin and Tina (Stuart) Gehrki were prominent residents of Java from
the early 1900's to 1961. They resided these many years in the home now
known as the Mr. and Mrs. Dave Schlepp residence on South Main in Java.

Mr. Gehrki was born in Germany and his wife, Tina, in Detroit, Mich.
They were married in Java. Mr. Gehrki passed away in 1961, and Mrs.
Gehrki, age 92, is residing in Aberdeen at this writing.

During their residence in Java, they were most active in community
affairs. For many years, Mr. Gehrki managed the McCabe elevator in
Java. He served as mayor of Java, was a member of the school board, and
for many years served as city auditor. Mrs. Gehrki was active in
Presbyterian church work, served as secretary of the Eastern Star and
at one time was dean of the girls' dormitory of the Java High school.

They have two daughters, Elizabeth and Agnes, both of whom graduated
from the Java High school. Elizabeth (Mrs. Erwin Kuhnert) resides in
Aberdeen, and Agnes (Mrs. Frank Manders), formerly of International
Falls, Minn., now resides in southern Texas.

        Martin Gehrki        Mrs. Martin Gehrki

                JOHANN JACOB GEIGLE

One of the early arrivals in the Java vicinity in 1885, was Johann
"Jacob" Geigle. His grandfather Balthasor Geigle left Cal-Wurttenberg,
Germany in 1804 and settled in the German Colony of Bergsdorf, which is
located in Grigoriopol district of Ukraine in South Russia. His father,
George Philip was born in Bergsdorf and in 1838 moved to the newly
formed colony of Helenstal. Here in Helenstal Johann Jacob Geigle was
born May 11, 1848 to George Philip Geigle and Carolina Geigle nee
Heinzman. In 1872 he was united in marriage to Katherine Sieler.

Many of their friends and relatives had been leaving Russia to travel
the great distance over land and sea to establish their new home in
America. Reports were that fertile land could be acquired upon
homesteading. Thus it was that Johann, better known as Jacob, and
Katherine, along with grandma Sieler, her brother, Casper Sieler and
the Vollmers, and Fullmers left Russia, Sept. 30, 1884 for Scotland
then the Dakota Territory. Here they spent the winter with Schaeffers
and next spring left to settle on a farm 11 miles north of Java in
Campbell county, known as Sutley. The Geigles immediately began to break
sod for planting a crop. As soon as time permitted the construction of
sod house began. This house was habitable until 1948 when it was torn
down to make way for a modern structure.

The closest trading town was Ipswich, until the railroad came to Eureka
in 1887 and Java became their trading town after 1900.

Twelve children were born to this union: four died in their infancy,
and the others were, Adam, Katherine, Christina, Pauline, Carolina,
Jacob, Rosina and Alvina. Alvina, Mrs. Simon Schaefer of Tripp, S.D.,
is the only living member of the family.

In 1915, Mr. and Mrs. Geigle retired from farming and moved to Java,
and resided on Main street until their death. Mrs. Geigle passed away
in March, 1923, and he in June, 1933. They were faithful members of
Reformed church and both are buried in Reformed cemetery in Java.

        Mr. and Mrs. Johann Jacob Geigle

        Jacob Geigle        Mrs. Jacob Geigle

                JACOB GEIGLE

Jacob Geigle was born October, 1885 to Johann Jacob Geigle and
Katherine Geigle, nee Sieler in Sutley, 11 miles north of Java. He
spent his life at the parental home until they retired in 1915. He took
over the family farm and was active in farming until ill health forced
his retirement in 1945. The farm was sold in 1947 to Erwin Rau. In
November, 1916 he married Carolina Lechner of Eureka.

The Geigle's were charter members of Sutley Congregational church and
Mrs. Geigle was charter member of Sutley Ladies Aid. Mr. Geigle served
on Ackerman District No. 46, school board for many years.

Eleven years after his retirement death came on May, 1956 and remains
were interred in the Java Congregational cemetery. Mrs. Geigle
remarried in 1961 to Gottlieb Zeller and in 1965 he passed away.
Presently Mrs. Zeller is residing in Lake View Apartments in Eureka, S.
D.

The Geigles have one son, Herbert, who is residing in San Juan, Puerto
Rico.

                GIMBEL FAMILY

When one thinks of writing family history dating back almost 100 years,
many things that should be included, space just will not allow.

The Gimbel family, of which this writer is the great grandson, dates
back to the Heinrich and Karoline Gimbel family, who were born and
raised in Russia. Their large town was Rostor, northeast of the Black
Sea. This was largely a farming community with many small villages, out
of which the people farmed and raised livestock. Heinrich Gimbel did
carpentry work, building mostly with stones,--a trade that was followed
in this country. The first home, combination house and barn, was built
of stones, with very little lumber used.

Heinrich Gimbel was born in Russia in 1829, of Russian parents. He and
his wife, Karoline grew up when Alexander II was ruler of Russia. Under
that ruling Russia was continually at war; first toward the west and
later with the Chinese to the east. Heinrich and Karoline's eldest son,
Jacob spent 4 years in the Russian military service. It was this type
of constant wars and poverty that prompted Heinrich and Karoline to
migrate to the U.S. in 1879. They left their eldest married daughter
and their 2 sons: Jacob, then 28 years old, and Simon, who was 10 with
his brother, in the homeland, to come later if it was better in the
United States.

        Left to right on harvesting picture, Simon Gimbel Sr. on
        header; hired hand, Ernest Gimbel and Elizabeth Gimbel (Mrs.
        Hans Carlson).

They first worked in the east till early spring when they migrated to
Yankton, which was then the capitol of Dakota territory; from there
they came to Richmond, S.D. territory, which was later called
Columbia, S.D. It was there they met people by the name of Shandoers,
and they all came as far west as where the old original homestead
began, one and a half miles south of the old farmstead now occupied by
the great grandson, O. E. Carlson. Shandoers lived just 1/4 mile east
of the farmstead. It is here where Heinrich and Karoline continued to
live until 1892, when their 2 sons, Jacob and his family, and Simon
Gimbel came to this country. Jacob filed on land in the Long Lake area,
and lived there until the 1900's when they went to Canada.

In 1893 Heinrich Gimbel purchased a claim shack and 40 acres of land
from an English man (Andrew Lootz), a young bachelor who was the
original owner of the old farmstead. It was here Simon started farming.
He married Rosina Schanzenbach in Eureka in 1894. The Schanzenbachs were
prominent citizens of the Akaska and Selby area.

It was in the spring of 1895 when Granddad was going to take his wife
to Akaska to her parents where their first child was to have been born;
but alas, time ran short at the Hoefs farm about 7 miles west of the
homeplace. It was there that this writer's mother, Elizabeth was born
on March 4, 1895. The Hoefs were parents of May Hoefs, who had a
clothing store for many years in Selby.

Heinrich Gimbel passed away at the age of 73 in September of 1902. At
his dying request, he was buried close to his home and later two of
Simon's children, Henry, age 1, Jan. 2, 1907 and Maggie, age 7, on Dec.
21, 1907, were also buried beside their grandfather. It is the hope
that this writer may also someday be buried there.

In 1908, Karoline Gimbel moved to Java, and later when Java began a
building boom, Simon also moved to Java, where he did carpentry work.

To Simon Gimbel and his wife, seven children were born: Elizabeth,
Ernest, Maggie, Bertha, Henry, Theodore, and Simon. At this writing 3
children, Dr. Simon, Ernest and Bertha (Mrs. Glen Colson) are still
living.

This writer's father, Hans O. Carlson, who came to this country at 19
years of age from Goteborg, Sweden, worked in Montevideo, Minn., for
some years before coming to Java where he worked on the Milwaukee road
until his marriage to Elizabeth Gimbel. They then lived on the old
original homestead. One son, O. E. Carlson, was born to this marriage.
Hans Carlson passed away in 1947 and this writer has lived on the old
homestead since.

O. E. Carlson was married to Dorothy Thomas in 1948, and to this
marriage 4 children were born: Diane (Mrs. Mick Mercigan);. Dinah (Mrs.
Frank Konda); Perry and Dinal. So this takes this writer up to 1974;
but in looking at old pictures etc., the many names that have changed
in our area: Alfred Hingcliff, used to be Heinrich Gimbel's neighbor;
O. D. Buchecker, the man who wore a buffalo headdress; the Nobles, who
had 50' wooden silos; Herman Wosnuk; Wm. Payne; the Hagadus (there is
still part of their stonehouse left); all these helped to build this
community and all are gone,--only memories are left.

This writer also ran across the many different religions to which
members of the family belonged: Simon Gimbel belonged to the
Congregational faith; his mother, Karoline, joined the Adventist faith
and was a member until her death on Oct. 29, 1915; my father belonged
to the Zion Lutheran faith; this writer joining the Catholic faith in
1948. So, another page of history for our Java and area. --O. E.
Carlson .

        Mr. and Mrs. John Goetz, Sr.

                JOHN GOETZ SR.

John Goetz Sr., was born in Gluckstal, South Russia on Aug. 15, 1870
and stayed with his parents until he grew to young manhood. On Jan. 31,
1895 he was united in marriage with Christine Unruh and to this couple
were born eleven children, of which nine survived.

In 1905, the John Goetz family undertook the long voyage to come to the
United States. They settled at Hillsview and later homesteaded near
Akaska. Some years later, the family moved to a farm near Java, where
they resided until 1925 when they bought a home in the city of Java.
The family were very active church members of the Lutheran church. In
1927, his wife passed away. He ran the drayline for a few years. On
July 2, 1929 he married Mrs. Justine Wolf also of Java .

The children were as follows:

Jacob Goetz married Katherine Henne (deceased), and Jacob now lives in
Eureka, S.D. They had 3 children, one son and 2 daughters, all married.

John Goetz married Ida Naasz; he passed away in 1971. They had one
daughter, Geraldine, who married Leonard Schmidtgall and they have 3
daughters and 1 son.

Fred Goetz married Kathryn Eisenbeisz and live in Java. Had one child
(deceased).

George Goetz was killed in a car accident in 1949. He married Emma
Schaffer and they had 2 sons and 2 daughters, all married. Emma is also
deceased.

Lt. Col. Carl Goetz, married Neva. They had 3 daughters all married. He
retired from Service and are now living in Tacoma, Wash.

Barbara Goetz married Henry Lutz and they had one daughter. Barbara
passed away in 1948 and daughter Elaine drowned in 1940.

Rose Goetz married Gerhardt Schaffer and had one son, They now live in
Watford City, N.D., and are operating a grocery store.

Frieda Goetz married Robert Haux and are living in Salmon, Idaho, where
they are both teaching.

Kathryn Goetz married Ralph Carroll, now deceased. She lives in Fort
Worth Texas, during the winter, and spends her summer months on a
resort in South Fork. Colo.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Goetz, Jr.

                JOHN GOETZ JR.

John Goetz Jr., was born in Gluckstal, South Russia, on April 13, 1901.
He came to the United States with his parents when he was a little boy
at the age of 4 years. He lived on the farm near Java, until he grew to
manhood. He was married to Ida Naasz in June, 1928 and moved on a farm
east of Java. They farmed for many years until he bought a home in Java
in 1940. He was employed by Biels' Red Owl of Java, for many years and
then later was a meat cutter for Hank's Red Owl in Selby, until his
retirement.

John passed away in November, 1971 and Ida is now living in the Selby
Nursing Home.

They had one daughter, Geraldine, who married Leonard Schmidtgall, and
live on a farm 5 miles east of Java. They have 3 daughters. Georgia,
works as a secretary at N.S.C., in Aberdeen; Joy, age 6, will start
kindergarten in the Java school; Sherri, age 4, spends most of her time
at home; one son, Jay, attends Java High school, where he will be a
member of the Senior class.
 
                GOTTLIEB GOETZ

Gottlieb Goetz was born in Hoffnungstal, South Russia, on July 2,1864
and when he reached young manhood he married Christina Oster, who also
was born in the Hoffnungstal area on Nov. 30, 1864.

They came to America with their two oldest children, Gottlieb and
Charlie. In 1889, they undertook the long journey and arrived at
Eureka, S.D., but remained there temporarily until they were able to
file a claim of land 8 miles northeast of Java, where they homesteaded.
They raised their family of 8 children. who were: Gottlieb, Charlie,
John, Christ, and Sam, (all deceased) and 3 children living who are:
Magdelina, married Sam Schlepp and now living in Mobridge, S.D.; Fred
now living in Roseburg, Ore.; Edward married Margaret Bratzel and now
living in the Selby Nursing Home, Selby, S.D.

        The Gottlieb Goetz family

Gottlieb passed away July 11, 1911 at the age of 47. Mrs. Goetz
continued living on the homestead until 1919, when she married
Christian Pfitzer and moved to Java. One son, Charlie took over the
farming on the homestead, which is now being farmed by a grandson,
Oscar Goetz. Mrs. Goetz passed away March 3,1943 at the age of 79 years.

                CHARLIE GOETZ

Charlie Goetz was born Feb. 25, 1889 in Bessarabia, South Russia, son
of Gottlieb Goetz and his wife, Christina (nee Oster). He came to New
York Harbor by boat with his parents and older brother, Gottlieb. They
arrived at Eureka by train, but remained there only a short time. A
land agent helped them seek land to their satisfaction as they wanted
land with rocks which was used to build a house. This was located 8
miles northeast of Java. He worked and went to school in Adams Township
in Walworth county and was confirmed March 27, 1904 by Rev. Geo.
Kirchdoerfer in the Lutheran church in Java.

On April 10, 1913 he married Magdelena Horst, who was born Jan. 17,1890
at Bessarabia, South Russia, a daughter of Gottlieb Gohring and
Margaretha (Hindemeth) Gohring and was later adopted by Leonard and
Christina Horst. They came to America in 1898 and the Horst family
homesteaded 7 miles northeast of Java. She was confirmed by Rev. Geo.
Kirchdoerfer and grew to womanhood around the Java area before marrying
Charlie. After their marriage Charlie and his bride moved to Crow Rock,
Mont., and lived there for 5 years before returning to South Dakota.
They lived on his father's farm until 1942 when they bought a farm 10
miles northeast of Java, now owned and operated by a son, Oscar Goetz.
In 1947 they bought a home and moved to Java to lead a retired life.
Mr. Goetz passed away November, 1957 at the age of 68 years, and Mrs.
Goetz continued to live in Java, until her death in October, 1965 at
the age of 75 years.

The Charlie Goetz family had 9 children and 16 grandchildren.

They were: Maggie married Albert Kary had one daughter, Vera, and are
living in Java.

        Mr. and Mrs. Charlie Goetz and family

Oscar married Stella Jurief and had 4 sons and are living on the home
farm 10 miles northeast of Java.

Edward, known as "Whitie" passed away, Jan. 21, 1969.

Ferdinand married, and has 2 daughters and are residing in Dallas,
Texas.

Harold Goetz married Irene Hirsch, and had 2 sons and are living on a
farm, 1 mile east of Java.

Howard is employed in Bowdle, S.D.

Irene married Theo Oster and had 2 daughters and are living in
Aberdeen, S.D.

Erna married Ted Schmierer and had 2 sons and is residing in Aberdeen,
S.D.

Calvin married Sylva Walker and had 3 sons and are living in Miles
City, Mont.

All the Goetz children attended school in Walworth county.

                JACOB AND MARGARETHA HAUX

Jacob Haux Sr., was born Oct. 4, 1874 at Koscharke, South Russia, where
he grew to young manhood and later was confirmed by Pastor Bernbaum on
April 18, 1893.

At the age of 21 years, Jacob came to America with his parents, George
and Katherina (Merkel) Haux Sr. The Haux family arrived in the Dakota
Territory and settled on a farm 1-1/2 miles northwest of Java, which
they later claimed as their homestead. Jacob helped his father with the
farming for several years.

On Oct. 30, 1906, Jacob married Margaretha Mauch who was born Oct. 28,
1884 on the ship enroute to America. Margaretha and her parents
homesteaded in the Freeman, S.D. area for a few years before coming to
the Java vicinity. After Jacob and Margaretha's marriage, the young
couple made their home with Jacob's parents and later took over the
homestead after they passed away. Farming was their livelihood and many
hardships were endured by this couple while raising their eight
children. The Haux family were devout church members of the St. Paul
Lutheran church of Java. Many services were rendered whenever possible,
and generosity endeavored the family to make a donation of land to the
Lutheran church for the purpose of a cemetery plot, which is still in
existence.

Jacob farmed with the help of his sons, until his death in 1948. A year
later his wife built a home and moved to Java where she lived until
1972 when she sold her home to Otto Mueller and moved to the Good
Samaritan Center at Selby, S.D.

Five sons and three daughters were born to Jacob and Margaretha who
were:

John born Aug. 16, 1907; married Emily Wolff, and reside on a farm
5-1/2 miles northwest of Java. They have one son, Larry, who is still
living with them.

Jacob, born April 27, 1909; married Johanna Weber and have one son,
Jerry. Jacob passed away in 1946 and his wife Johanna lives at
Bakersfield, Calif.

Lydia, (Mrs. Berthold Schnaible) born Jan. 7, 1911; reside at Java
where they are living a retired life. They had 3 sons and one daughter.
Roger married Janice Johnson and have two boys. Ray Lee married Susan
Westling and have one son. Bob married Judy Bieber and have 2 girls.
Ruby married Chuck Birkholt and live at Selby, S.D., where they are
both employed.

David, born Dec. 30, 1912; married Frieda Christman and reside on the
homestead. They have one son and a daughter. Dennis married Judy
Christenson and have one girl and two boys, who reside at Wayzata,
Minn. Maxine married Earl Frank, who is in the Service and stationed at
Rapid City. They have one son, Douglas.

Otto, born April 10, 1915; married Alvina Wolff and reside at Aberdeen,
S.D., where they are both employed.

Albert, born April 1, 1917; married Jean Miller, Albert passed away in
1968 and his wife resides at Reno, Nevada.

Minnie, (Mrs. Erhard Beitelspacher) born Nov. 6, 1919; reside on a farm
13 miles southeast of Java. They have 2 daughters and one son. Lonita
married Carmen Kilber and live at Minneapolis, Minn. Cynthia married
Lyle Schock and live at Rapid City; LeRoy married Carla Kilber and live
near his parents.

Irene, born Dec. 6, 1921; graduated from the Java school and passed
away in 1940.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Haux, Sr.

                CHRISTIAN HAUX

Christian Haux was born on December 11, 1888, in New Gluckstal, Russia,
near the city of Odessa. He was the youngest son of George and
Elizabeth Haux Sr. He migrated to the United States in 1899, with his
parents when he was eleven years of age.

His parents, 1 brother, Jacob, two sisters, Katherine and Caroline,
took port at Bremerhofen, Germany, and landed in New York. One married
brother, George Jr., had migrated with his wife and family in 1893.
From New York, they came to Bowdle, S.D.-His father, George Haux Sr.,
purchased the Heerwald farm northwest of Java. Christian farmed with his
parents, went to school to learn the English language, read and write.
He was also confirmed in the First Lutheran church near Java.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian M. Haux

On October 29, 1912, he married Katherine Krause. Katherine was the
daughter of Johanas and Louise Krause, who had homesteaded in Walworth
county, South Dakota, where Katherine was born on April 10, 1890.

In 1913 they built a farm two miles northwest of Java. The farm was
built by Gottlieb Bamesberger, who was married to Christian's sister,
Katherine. Mr. Bamesberger was a carpenter by trade.

Christian was fond of horses and farmed with horses until the drought
years in the early thirties, when tractors were put into use.

He was active in community affairs, was a member of rural school board
for many years. He was a member of the Board of Directors of the Java
Equity Exchange. He was active in the Lutheran church, and was
treasurer at the time of his death on June 30, 1942.

His wife, Katherine continued to live on the farm until 1945, when she
retired. She lived with her father, Johanas Krause until 1949 when she
built her own home. Katherine passed away April 10, 1968.

                GEORGE HAUX SR.

George Haux Sr., was the youngest of four sons born to Johonas Haux,
who lived in Russia near Odessa. Elizabeth was the daughter of George
and Magdaline Merkel, who also lived near Odessa Russia. George Haux
Sr., was born in Neugluckstal, Russia and migrated to the United States
in 1889 on a ship named the F. Messler, which sailed from Port
Bremerhofen, Germany. They arrived in New York and then traveled by
train to Bowdle, S.D. With George and Elizabeth came four of their
children, Jacob, Katherine, Caroline, and Christian. Their eldest son,
George Jr., had migrated with his wife and family to the United States
in 1893.

George Haux Sr., bought the Heerwald farm northwest of Java. The
purchase of the farm included all the livestock and with the help of
his family he farmed for many years. George was a charter member of the
Java Lutheran Church and donated the land for the Java Lutheran Church
Cemetery.

Some members of Elizabeth's family came to the United States, also one
brother, Jacob, farmed near Eureka, and later moved west of the
Missouri River, where he farmed.

George Haux Sr., lived his last years with his daughter Katherine,
(Mrs. Gottlieb Bamesberger).

        Mr. and Mrs. George Haux, Sr.

                GEORGE HAUX JR.

George Haux Jr., was the son of George Haux Sr., and Elizabeth Merkel.
He was born in 1864 and died in 1933. He married Katherine Jesser and
she was born in 1865 and died in 1925. They were both born in Russia
and got married in Russia. They immigrated to America in 1893 with his
family of four: John, Rudolph, Jacob and Katie, six weeks old. Four
children were born in America: Henry, William, Minnie and Lydia .

They homesteaded about 5 miles northwest of Java moving a few years
later to a farm four miles northwest of Java, one mile south of the
homestead. They retired from active farming in 1918 and moved to Java,
where they remained for the rest of their lives. After they retired,
George did carpentry and cement work.

        George Haux, Jr.        Mrs. George Haux, Jr.

                JACOB J. HAUX

Jacob J. Haux was born in Neugluckstal, Russia on Jan. 11, 1890. At the
age of three, he came to America with his parents, George and Katherine
Haux, two brothers, John and Rudolph, and one sister, Katie, six weeks
old. Two sisters, Minnie and Lydia and one brother, William were born in
America.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob J. Haux

His parents homesteaded 4 miles northwest of Java, and there he helped
with the farming and also attended school, finishing the fourth grade.
In the summer of 1908 he worked for George Bender on the threshing
machine. Later he worked as a hired hand on a farm in Linton, N.D. In
the fall of 1910, he went to work on a farm 2-1/2 miles northwest of
Java, as a hired hand for his uncle Jacob Haux Sr. In 1911 he worked as
a hired hand for Mr. Jackson. In 1912 he went to Martin, N.D., and
worked on the threshing machine. In 1913 he started farming for
himself, six miles northeast of Selby. On Dec. 19, 1915 he was united
in marriage to Ida Hetterle. Ida was born to Emanuel and Christina
Hetterle on a farm 5 miles northeast of Selby, on May 16, 1895. The
family consisted of 2 boys and 2 girls. Ida, as young girl, had to
help with the field work and many were the days she had to walk behind
the plows.

Jacob and Ida spent their married life on the farm until they retired
and moved into Java. They farmed 2 years on a farm 6 miles northeast of
Selby, then they moved to another farm 4 miles north of Selby. In 1918
they moved onto Jacob's parents' homestead and later bought it.

The Jacob Hauxs have a family of three children: Oscar, born Nov. 9,
1919, married JoAnne Scriptnor, they have two children, Danny, living
at Salem, Idaho, and Sandra married to David Leonard, residing at
Richmond, Calif. Oscar now married to Deloris Williams, living at San
Joaquin, Calif.

Helen (Mrs. Harold Pfitzer) born Aug. 30, 1924, now living on the home
farm, northwest of Java. They have 3 sons Rodney, born Dec. 7, 1942,
married Sept. 5, 1965 to Shirley Mickelson and they have 3 sons, all
living in Omaha, Neb. Charles, born Oct. 6, 1946, married Oct. 12, 1967
to Maureen Penry, they have 3 children, one daughter, 2 sons; now
residing at Englewood, Colo. Randy, born Dec. 15, 1953 now attending
S.D.S.U. at Brookings, S.D.

Frances (Mrs. Clifford Silveson) born Dec. 10, 1929, they have 5
children, Clifford Jr., serving in the Army and now stationed at
Germany. Callie, Reed, Jan and Cody, all living at home at Billings,
Mont.

In 1944, Jacob and Ida purchased a home at Java, where they are
presently living now, and leading a retired life.

        The Ludwig Helm, Sr. family. Mr. Helm, Ludwig Jr., Christine,
        Caroline, Mrs. Helm. Christ, in front, was wearing a "Vote
        for McKinley" button.

                LUDWIG HELM SR.

Ludwig Helm Sr., was born in 1863 and came to America in 1889 from
Neudorf, Russia. He died in 1940. He married Margret Hieb in 1886 at
Neudorf. She was born in 1863 and died in 1922.

They homesteaded 6 miles north of Java. In 1907, he bought the farm
formerly known as Rosella, trading post, post office and store. He
retired to Java in a home he built on Helm street.

Their children were:

Ludwig Jr., born in 1887 in Neudorf Russia, married Katie Hemrich in
1913. Birth unknown. Children, 1 baby died in infancy. Deceased in 1961.

Caroline, born in 1889, died in 1919, married Edward Nusz, 1907;
children, Christina, married Jake Henne; Henry and Harry.

Christine, born in 1891, married John L. Naasz, 1909; John Naasz, died
1947; Children, Edna, married Jake Delzer; Elsie, married Jacob
Schower; Milbert, married; Ervin, married; Twins, Lucile and Lucinda,
married; Gerhardt died as a small child.

Christ L., Born in 1893, married Rosie Lutz in 1913; Rosie Lutz, born
1893, died 1972. Children, Arthur, born 1914; Ervin, born 1919; Inez,
born 1921; Violet, born 1925; Larry, born 1938.

                CHRIST L. HELM

Christ L. Helm was born 6 miles north of Java, in Walworth county in
1893. He married Rosie Lutz in 1913. She was born in 1893 and died in
1972. They were married in a country church north of Java, known as
Peace Lutheran church. He farmed in partnership with his father, Ludwig
Helm Sr., and in 1918 bought the farm from his father, known as
Rosella, trading post, postoffice and store before it was sold to
Ludwig Helm in 1907.

In 1920 they moved to Java, and bought a share in the Java Oil Co. In
1923 he moved back to the farm.

In 1936 he moved to Java and oldest son, Arthur took over the farm. In
1945 he bought the Selby Livestock Sale Barn. In 1947 he moved to the
new home he built in Selby. In 1951 he sold the sale barn and bought a
plot of land on the east edge of Selby; part of this plot was turned
into a trailer court and part a livestock yard, where he bought for
Armour Packing Co.

        The Christ L. Helm family. Ervin, Christ, Arthur, Violet, Mrs.
        Helm, Inez.

Their children were as follows:

Arthur, born 1914 married Frieda Sieler, in 1935. Children, Janice,
born 1936, married Morris Kosters in 1956; Ruby G., born 1938, married
Henry G. Kosters in 1957; Dwight, born in 1952, married Yvonne Mitchell
in 1973; Leslie, born in 1956, is at home.

Ervin C. Helm, born in 1919, married Elma Berreth in 1945; children,
Nel, born in 1948; Dean, born in 1951, both unmarried.

Inez, born in 1921, married Frank Huppert in 1941 children, lone, born
in 1942 married John Hartman.

Violet, born in 1925, married Edward L. Morris in 1941; children, Paul,
born in 1946, married Mary Victoria Roman in 1971.
