have three children George married Betty Thurn of Bowdle. They have
three children and live in San Diego and Palm Springs, Calif George
graduated from NSC and the University of Colorado in Boulder. Richard
married Thelma Coons of Yakima, Wash. They have three children and live
in Bellevue, Wash. Richard graduated from NSC and the University of
Washington.

In recent years Mr. and Mrs. Nies spent their winters in California,
with their children or in Palm Springs. They enjoyed the climate, the
friendly people and the many interesting things the state offers,
however when spring came, they wanted "to go home".

Mr. Nies passed away June 14, 1973, shortly after returning from Palm
Springs. A Java High school scholarship has been established in his
memory.

Mrs. Nies passed away at her winter residence in Palm Springs, Calif.,
December 26, 1974.

        Mr. and Mrs. Peter L. Nies

                THE NOBLE FAMILY

The Cornelius Noble family came to the United States from Alkmaar,
Holland in 1874. They came by steamboat which took several weeks to
cross the Atlantic Ocean. They landed in New York, then traveled by
train to Aberdeen, S.D., then to Ipswich S.D., where the railroad
ended. Here they purchased a wagon and team of oxen and other necessary
tools for farming. They arrived at the Java area, which was then a
Holland settlement, May 1, 1884.

Mr. and Mrs. Cornelius Noble and their four sons settled on land two
miles southwest of Java. Their first home was a small house with only
the bare necessities. There were no comforts or luxuries. Their time
and energy was spent in working the land and providing food and
clothing.

Pioneers had to travel by ox team to the nearest railroad to buy the
staple food and clothing and seed. Also to sell the grain they had
raised.

Philip, Peter, Gerrit and Nick all attended school nearby, but school
was held only part of the year.

As the years went by Philip married Grace Harris and acquired the farm
one mile south of Java. Peter Noble married Anna DeVries in 1899. He
purchased the Van Alst farm and filed on a tree claim three miles
southwest of Java. Gerrit Noble married Alice Corson and farmed his
father's farm two miles southwest of Java. Nick Noble farmed 3 miles
south of Java. He passed away Oct. 25, 1909 of heart failure while duck
hunting near Spring Lake. This farm was then claimed by Grandfather
Noble.

In 1905, Grandmother Noble became ill. She was given all the love and
care that could be given in those days of no hospitals. She passed away
in October of that year.

        The Noble family. Left to right, John, Dick, Peter Noble
        (father), Cornelius, Anna Noble (mother), Tina, Jansje,
        Cornelius Noble (grandfather).

It was here near Java that they all went through the hardships of
pioneer days. There was much worry about prairie fires, Indian
uprisings, droughts, snow blizzard, black blizzards and grasshoppers.
Neighbors, in early days, joined in rabbit and coyote round-ups to
prevent crop destruction and loss of stock. Then again, neighbors got
together with teams and wagons and drove to the Missouri River to pick
buffalo berries, grapes and plums. They also helped one another with
harvesting and threshing and in times of illness.

It was in the 1930-40 years of the depression days that brought on the
greatest hardship and worry to the health of these early pioneers.
Philip Noble passed away March 23, 1932; Peter Noble died Nov. 1, 1937;
and Gerrit Noble died March 21, 1938.

Philip and Grace Noble family:

Philip Noble - Cathryn Wosnuk, Anacordes, Wn.: Ronald Noble.

Grace Noble McMacken-Alcott, Sinton, Texas: Jimmy McMacken, Sue Grace
McMacken.

Elizabeth Noble Coture (deceased), Washougal, Wn.

Nick Noble (deceased) - Clara Wolf, Camas, Wn.

Laura Noble - Timon Oltman, Spearfish, S.D.: Lorna Oltman .

Peter and Anna Noble family:

Tina Noble, now lives in Panorama City, Olympia, Wn.

Dick Noble, deceased

Cornelius Noble - Addie Lutz (deceased), Kent, Wash.: Jane Noble Abel,
Joan Noble Riddle.

John Noble, deceased.

Jansje Noble - Oliver Wilson, Bonners Ferry, Idaho: Donald Wilson,
George Wilson, Peter Wilson.

Gerrit and Alice Noble family:

Leonard Noble - Esther Olson (deceased), Hot Springs, S.D.: Robert
Noble, Jean Noble Schuchardt.

                NICK AND CLARA NOBLE

Nick's granddad was born in Holland, and came to America with his wife
and sons, Nick, Peter, Garret and Philip. They homesteaded on the farms
which have been known as the Noble Brothers farms.

Nick grew up in Walworth county. He was born on a farm one mile west of
Java, and moved in 1917 to Java, where his father, Phillip, ran the
Java Equity Machine shop.

Clara Wolf was born 21/z miles west of Java, where her folks had a
farm. They moved to town in 1918 to go to school.

Nick and Clara were married Jan. 1, 1932 in Java. Nick worked on W.P.A.
They worked at Lake Hiddenwood at the time. They left South Dakota in
1940, to live in Lakeland, Florida. They stayed until July, 1943, then
they went back to South Dakota for a short visit, then left for
Vancouver, Washington, to work in the shipyards. They were both welders
until the end of the war.

        Mr. and Mrs. Nick Noble

They then came to Camas, Washington, to work in the Crown Zellerbach
Paper Mill. Nick died Jan. 28, 1969, and Clara still lives in her home
in Camas.

        Christ W. Nusz family. Left to right, top row, Walter, Donald,
        Ferdinand, Theodore, Albert. Middle, Hulda, Engelina, Edna,
        Lillian. Front, Esther, Mrs. Nusz, Christ W. Nusz and Elsie.

                MR. AND MRS. CHRIST W. NUSZ

Christ Nusz was born on April 6, 1888 at Freeman, South Dakota. His
parents, Mr. and Mrs. Heinrich Nusz Sr., immigrated from South Russia
to Freeman in 1875. Christ was thirteen years old when he moved to a
farm six miles north of Selby in Walworth county in 1901.

In 1907 he married Margaretha Pleinis from the Java area. Mrs. Nusz was
born in 1889 and her parents were Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Pleinis Sr., who
lived north of Java. She attended school in Highland District north of
Java. In 1901 she worked in the first restaurant in Selby. She worked
for Captain Newton Kingman for $2.50 a week, and she also worked for
the D. Reillys. He was with the Walworth County Bank. Mr. and Mrs. Nusz
lived in the Java area most of their early married life except for a
few years spent near Hettinger, North Dakota.

When Christ's father moved into the town of Selby, they moved to the
home place near Selby and farmed the land. In 1920, Christ and his
brother, Robert bought a threshing machine and threshed for the entire
neighborhood.

By this time there were seven children in the Nusz family and Mrs. Nusz
was very busy washing clothes on a scrub board, baking all of the
bread, raising vegetables, and canning all of the vegetables as well as
the beef, pork and chickens. She also sewed all of the clothes for the
entire family .

The depression years came and there were many dust storms, but they
continued planting grain until the grasshoppers ate everything. There
was no feed for the cattle, so they raked up thistles, mixed them with
old straw and salted it down for feed for the animals. Times were very
hard, so when a daughter, Mrs. Fred Stadel, who had moved to California
earlier, found a farm for her parents near Cottonwood, California, they
had an auction sale and moved in October of 1936.

Mr. and Mrs. Nusz were the parents of eleven children. Five sons,
Ferdinand, (deceased), Albert, Theodore, Donald and Walter. The six
daughters are: Esther, (Mrs. Fred Banek), Engelena, (Mrs. E. R.
Stuhlmiller), Elsie, (Mrs. Fred Stadel), Hulda, (Mrs. A. E. Berreth),
Edna, (Mrs. Jake Orth), and Lillian, (Mrs. Argo Giese).

Mr. and Mrs. Nusz celebrated their sixty-third wedding anniversary in
1970. Mr. Nusz passed away on January 31, 1971 and Mrs. Nusz continues
to live in her home in Woodland, California. Most of her children live
near this vicinity and spend much of their time with her.

                FREDRICK OBENAUER SR.

Fredrick Obenauer Sr., born April 17, 1860 in Hoffnungstal, South
Russia and lived with his parents until he grew to young manhood. In
1888, he married Karolina Sackman, who too, was living in the South
Russian area. In 1890 this couple obtained enough passage fare to make
the long trip by boat to come to America. Fredrick and Karolina and one
baby son arrived in the United States Port and took a train to the
Eureka area. They remained there only a short while and moved to the
Java area. They settled 6 miles northeast of Java, where they filed a
claim which is now farmed by Calvin Sandmeier. Fredrick started farming
with oxen and mules, as did the rest of his friends and relatives. They
became members of the Hoffnungstal church, which was built by a few
members.

The Obenauer family were as follows:

Fredrick, married Rosie Schlepp, had 8 children.

Adam married Lydia Wolff (both deceased) had 2 sons and 1 daughter

Two children died in infancy

John married Kathrine Gross had one son. John passed away in 1963 and
Kathrine resides in Lodi, Calif.

Maggie married Ralph Erickson (deceased) and Maggie resides in
Aberdeen, S.D.

Bertha married Richard Ketterling (deceased) had one daughter and
Bertha resides in Mercer, N.D.

Fredrick and Karolina raised their children through many hardships and
in 1920 they purchased a home in Eureka, and his son. Fredrick Obenauer
Jr., took over the old homestead.

                FREDRICK OBENAUER JR.

Fredrick Obenauer Jr., was born Oct. 26, 1894 while his parents lived
on a farm 6 miles northeast of Java, which is now being farmed by
Calvin Sandmeier. He grew to a young man while helping his father farm.

Fredrick Jr., married Rosie Schlepp, Jan. 9, 1916 and lived near his
father's homestead. When his parents moved to Eureka, Fred Jr., took
over his father's homestead and lived there for 8 years when he too
moved to Eureka.

Eight children were born to the Fred Obenauer Jr., family and were:

Ida married Peter Schmitt had 2 daughters and they live in Leola, S.D.

Hertha married Rueben Fischer had 2 sons and live in Barstow, Calif.

Violet married Ray Trauder had 3 daughters and 2 sons and live in
California.

Janice married Elmer Opp, had 3 daughters and 2 sons and live in
California.

Elsie married Mr. Ellis had 2 daughters and 1 son and live in
Lancaster, Calif.

Ervin married Ruth Weidrich, had 2 sons, Floyd and Dallas and live in
Eureka

Leroy married and has 3 daughters.

Edna died as a small baby and one child died in infancy.

        Mr. and Mrs. Carl Oster

                CARL OSTER

Carl (Karl) Oster was born Aug. 24, 1841, in Gluckstal, Russia. He was
confirmed and grew to manhood in the Russian area. On Feb. 21, 1863,
Carl was united to Rosina Wagner at Klostitz, South Russia.

Carl and his wife, Rosina made their home at Hoffnungstahl, South
Russia, where all their children were born. Seven children were born to
the Oster family who were: Katharina (Mrs. Christian Pfitzer), born
Nov. 25, 1863 Christian born 1865 - Christiana (Mrs. Gottlieb Goetz),
born 1867; Magdalena (Mrs. Fred Buechler), born March 19, 1874; Johann,
born Sept. 12, 1877; Samuel, born Nov. 22, 1878; and Adam, born July 5,
1881.

Carl and his wife and his family of unmarried children undertook the
trip to come to America. In 1889, they settled in Walworth county and
homesteaded on a farm 81/z miles northeast of what now is Java. He
became a naturalized citizen on April 10, 1897. Some years later Carl
and his wife moved to Java, where he resided until his death on Dec. 27,
1909.

        Mr. and Mrs. Adam Oster and foster son, Jacob Oster

                ADAM OSTER

Adam Oster was born in Gluckstal, South Russia, on Nov. 28, 1863. He
married Eva Schwent on June 10, 1891.

Adam and Eva and their foster son came to America in 1901. They
homesteaded east of Java on farm where they continued living for 14
years. They bought a farm two miles north of the homestead near Horse
Shoe lake, where Adam lived until his death on Jan. 15, 1934. Eva Oster
continued to live there with her foster son and his wife until 1948 at
which time the family moved to Bowdle, S.D. She continued living with
them until both of their deaths. In 1963, Eva moved to the Eureka
Nursing Home, where she remained until her death on Jan. 19, 1967.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Oster

                JACOB OSTER

Jacob Oster was born at Gluckstal, South Russia on April 5, 1891. At
the age of five years, his father passed away and he made his home with
an uncle and aunt, Mr. and Mrs. Adam Oster. In 1901, the entire family
came to America and settled in the Walworth county area, and
homesteaded on a farm 8 miles east of Java.

On Jan. 5, 1915, Jacob married Katherine Buechler and the young couple
farmed on the homestead. In 1934, the Jacob Oster family moved to the
Adam Oster farm near Horse Shoe lake, and lived with Mrs. Adam (Eva)
Oster.

Jacob and his wife, Katherine retired from farming in 1948. They bought
a home in Bowdle, where Mrs. Adam Oster continued living with them. A
retired life was enjoyed until their death: Jacob passed away March 20,
1957, and Katherine passed away Feb. 1, 1963.

There were 12 children born to Jacob and Katherine Oster. Five of these
children died in infancy and the remaining living include: Erna (Mrs.
Emil Sonnenfeld) has three children, now widowed resides at Sioux
Falls. Theophil married Irene Goetz, presently living in Aberdeen, S.
D., have two daughters. Alma (Mrs. Erwin Sonnenfeld) reside on a farm
northeast of Java have one son and one daughter. Helmuth, married
Isabel Kurle and reside at Billings, Mont., have three children.
Gerhard married Dorothy Schmidtgall and reside at Bowdle, S.D., have
one daughter. Renta, (Mrs. Elroy Jung) residing at Auburn, Wash., with
four children. Waldemar, married Alberta Senn and presently live at
Albany, Ore. They have two children.

                SAMUEL OSTER

Samuel Oster was born Nov. 22, 1878 in Hoffnungstal Bessarabia,
Roumania. According to Parochialschein (passport record gemeinde
Hoffnungstal, T. Peters Pastor zu Klostitz), he moved with his parents
to America the sixth of April, 1889 and became a U.S. citizen through
parental naturalization. He married Minnie (Mina) Kies, and settled on
a farm about 8 miles northeast of Java. Included in farming, he took
pride in raising tobacco. Of the early days of pioneering in the Dakota
territory, he was one of the few farmers in the state who has
successfully raised tobacco. In a soil and environment far from its
native habitat. For many years he experimented with the growing of
tobacco on his farm, northeast of Java. A careful understanding of the
plant and diligent cultivation produced unusual results. For many years
he raised tobacco on his farm for his own and his neighbor's use.
Moving to Java in 1919, he discontinued planting of tobacco, until
later in his life, when he again experimented on a lot near his house.
"It took many a barrel of water during the dry periods to keep the
plants growing". But the results were worth it. He cured the partly
green leaves in a barn upstairs. From a small plot he produced enough
of the leaves for a year's supply for his own use in his pipe. Wherever
he went you could smell his tobacco. It was strong! His wife passed
away in 1933 and he in 1951.

        Sam Oster and tobacco plants

                JACOB OCHSZNER

Jacob Ochszner was born April 8, 1871 at Worms, Russia. He was one of 15
children. At the age of 13, he, with his parents, immigrated to the
United States and settled on a farm in Hutchinson county, near Yankton,
S.D., and lived there until 1889 when he moved on a farm 5 miles
northwest of Java. He married Elizabeth Wolf, Jan. 4, 1894. This
remained their home until his retirement in 1920, at which time they
moved into Java.

She passed away Dec. 13, 1928. He married Eva Pregitzer in 1929 and
they made their home in Java, until he passed away Sept. 30, 1952.

                EVA OCHSZNER

Eva Ochszner was born Dec. 13, 1889 at Gluckstal, South Russia to Karl
and Johanna (Kiesz) Ritter. She was one of 11 children. At the age of 9
years, she, with her parents, immigrated to the United States and
established their home on a farm seven miles south of Eureka, S.D. In
1907 they moved to a farm 1 mile west of Mound City, where she lived,
met and married Christian Spiedel in October of 1909.

They lived on a farm northwest of Mound City until 1912 when they moved
to the wide open spaces of Montana, about 50 miles northwest of Miles
City. The settlers out there were far apart, no close neighbors.
Nothing but prairie and sky, as far as the eye could see. The only
living things in abundance were wild life, especially coyotes,--and oh
yes, lots of sagebrush and rattle snakes. The sagebrush was used for
heating the homes and also for cooking.

        Jacob and Eva Ochszner

Two children were born to this union, one daughter and a son, who died
in infancy. In the spring of 1916, Mr. Spiedel passed away. Then she
and her daughter moved back to South Dakota. Later she married John
Pregitzer in the fall of 1916. They made their home on a farm 2l/2
miles northwest of Java, where they lived until the fall of 1928, when
they moved into Java, where Mr. Pregitzer passed away in December of
that year. One son was born to this union, who died at the age of 7
years.

She married Jacob Ochszner in October of 1929. They lived in Java until
his death in 1952. She stayed on in her home until the spring of 1971
when she entered the Good Samaritan Center in Selby, where she still is
a resident. Her daughter, Mrs. Calvin (Edna) Baumann lives at Belle
Fourche, S.D. Her two granddaughters are: Mrs. Erwin (Dalles) Zeller
of Aberdeen, S.D.; Mrs. Rueben (Darlene) Job, Selby. Four great
grandchildren are: Mrs. Dave Hasselbery of Minneapolis, Minn.; Cindy
Zeller of Aberdeen, S.D.; and Paul and Jason Job of Selby.

                MR. AND MRS. FREDRICK C. PERMAN

For many decades, the Perman family lived in the colonies between the
Dnjestr and the Bug Rivers, located in the Black Sea area of South
Russia.

They, like many generations before them, went to colonize at the
invitation of Catherine the Great. It was not until 1809 that Ludwig
Perman and his family left Busenbach, District of Karlsruhe, Grand
Duchy of Baden, Germany. They were among the original founding families
of Kassel in South Russia.

In 1871, the colonists were introduced to representative local
government by Alexander II. This then, is the same year in which
Fredrick Perman came into the world.

He was born Sept. 6, 1871 to Christoph and Katharine Weisert Perman, in
the village of Neugluckstal. His mother died in an accident caused by
run away horses when he was seven years old. His father later married
Katharina Feickert.

It was about the time he returned home, after serving in the Russian
Army, that he had heard about the Homestead Act in the United States.
It provided that an alien, over 21, could obtain 160 acres of land if
he intended to live on it. One hundred sixty acres of land for the
landless farmer is a great attraction.

Fredrick married Rosina Bauder in the village of Freudenthal and
shortly immigrated to America. The passport was in three languages:
French, German and Russian.

        Mr. and Mrs. Fredrick C. Perman

After crossing Europe to Bremen, Germany they boarded a steamship for
New York. A year later, a newspaper article carried the story that the
ship was sunk after it struck a whale.

As they approached New York harbor, they could see the Statue of
Liberty, which was just ten years old. They stopped at Ellis Island,
"The Gateway to the New World". The island was used as an immigration
station. From New York, they took the railroad to Freeman, S.D.,
arriving on March 18, 1894.

  Fredrick was 23 and Rosina (nee Bauder) was 20 when the decision to
leave was made. They came along with 8 other families. Those listed
were Rosina's parents the Jacob Bauder family, Christoph Perman, his
wife Katharina, their son Christian 13, and Christoph 10, Adam Mertz,
Jacob Mertz, John Feickert, Adam Schlecht and Gottlieb Oster families.

Mr. Perman filed on a homestead in the southern part of McPherson
county, where a sod house was built. Three children were born here.
Jacob F., Aug. 1, 1894; Christoph F., May 2, 1896; and Katherine, Feb.
6, 1898.

Winter came early and always stayed late, often into April. The
blizzards raged for days and the temperature dropped to 30 below zero
and more.

In 1900, Fredrick purchased land in Glenn Township, 11 miles north of
Hoven, S.D. There Eva, Feb. 12, 1900; Rosina, May 30, 1902; Edward F.,
Aug. 25, 1904; and Emma, Aug. 26, 1906 were born. This is where the
family grew and prospered. In March of 1907, they moved on what was
known as the Jergenson farm. Wilhelmina, Sept. 6, 1908, William J., Aug.
12, 1901, and Henry G., July 24, 1912, were born there.

About 1912, they moved to Java, S.D., and Mr. Perman went into the
machinery business with his brother-in-law, Henry Sonnenfeld. Later, in
a blacksmith business with a partner, Jacob Merkel. And again with
Andrew Lutz, another brother-in-law, in a tavern.

Their first automobile was a high wheeler International, two cylinder
with four posts. Around 1925, they owned a Hupmobile touring car.

Fredrick and Rosina retired about 1928 when they built a home in Java.
While they lived in Java, they celebrated their Golden Wedding
Anniversary.

Mr. Perman served the community in many ways through the years. He was
one of the early converts to power farming. The home in Java was sold
in March of 1946 and they moved to Selby. Mrs. Perman passed away Jan.
1, 1959 and Mr. Perman on May 2, 1963.

Presently Jacob F. Iives in Sacramento. Katherine married Adolph Rieger
and is living in San Francisco; Eva married Jake C. Bieber, Gettysburg;
Rosina married John Goltz, Mobridge; Edward F., Selby; Minnie married
Albert Wahl, Selby; and William J., Java. Christoph F., died Oct. 14,
1923, Emma Wagner Feb. 22, 1966, and Henry G. Nov. 20, 1959.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob F. Perman

                MR. AND MRS. JACOB F. PERMAN

One of the early business men of Java was Jacob F. Perman. He was the
first child of Fredrick C. and Rosina (nee Bauder) Perman, born in
McPherson county, on the homestead, ten miles north of Bowdle, S.D. on
Aug. 1, 1894. He lived there until he was approximately six years old,
when the family purchased a farm in Glenn township in Walworth county.
He helped his father on the farm, went to school, and took religious
instructions as was the custom. Later, he went to Eureka Lutheran
college at Eureka, S.D.

On February 3, 1917, during a blizzard which lasted several days, Jake
and Katherine Thurn were married at the Perman farm on the hill. Later
that fall, in October, they moved to Portland, Ore., where Jake worked
in the shipyards. While they lived there, Eugene F., was born on Aug.
5, 1919. Jake, being a farmer at heart, came back to help his father
put the crops in. They lived on the farm for three years. Harvey was
born on Nov. 10, 1920 and Adeline on April 4, 1922.

The family moved to Lowry in the fall of 1922, where Jake sold farm
machinery, cars and grain. The business was sold to G. G. Schock in
December, 1925, at which time he purchased the building a block off
Main street in Java from the Farmers Equity Exchange. This building was
also used for community dances. As the business grew, he moved to the
Main street of Java, across from the Fred Bauer store. He had the
International Harvestor business, auto dealership, and service station.
He served on the school board, and the family were members of St. Paul
Lutheran church.

The house was moved from Lowry to Java. Genevieve was born there on
Oct. 28, 1929.

During the years of 1934 to 1937, the family operated the movie theatre.

The latter part of 1937, the family moved to Moorhead, Minn., until the
spring of 1940 when Jake went into business selling parts and machinery
in Eureka. They retired in 1946 and moved to Sacramento. Calif.

Eugene F., married Irene Pudwill, retired from the Navy and is
presently employed at McClellan AFB. They have three daughters: Joan,
Nancy and Carol. Harvey H., married Lois Rosenow, and he is an
Orthopedic Surgeon. They have four children: Guy, William, Barbara and
Jimmy. Adeline M., married Harry Weston, and is employed by a bank as a
lending officer. They have two sons: Bradley C. and Craig D. Weston.
Genevieve, married Duane Euren and is employed as a secretary at
McClellan AFB. She has two sons: Barry and Richard Euren. All live in
Sacramento California.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Pfitzer

                CHRISTIAN PFITZER

Christian Pfitzer was born Sept. 8, 1863 in Hoffnungstal, Bessarabia,
Roumania. His wife, Katharina Pfitzer (nee Oster) was born Nov. 25,
1863 in Hoffnungstal, Bessarabia, Roumania. Both were confirmed in 1878
in Kloestitz and married Feb. 20, 1884 in Kloestitz. This union was
blessed with five children: Margaretha, Christ Jr., Matheus (Matt),
Rosina and Julius. The first three were born in Roumania and the last
two were born on a farm near Java.

After serving in the Czar's army, Christian Pfitzer moved his family to
America, landing at Port New York on June 18 1892, and received his
final naturalization papers in 1900.

They moved on a farm 15 miles northwest of Bowdle which was their town
until the start of Java in 1900. In 1909 they moved to Java so their
youngest son, Julius could attend the Java school. Christ Jr., and
Matheus (Matt) stayed on the farm. Margaretha, the oldest had already
been married to Adam Eisenbeisz and Rosina died in infancy.

In 1918, during the flu epidemic Katharina, Mrs. Christian Pfitzer,
passed away. The following year, he married Christiana Goetz (nee
Oster), a sister of his first wife. He was among the members of the
first St. Paul Lutheran church in 1897, along with Karl Schnaible,
George Haux Sr., John Lutz Sr., Fred Spiry Sr., Philip Spiry Sr., George
Wolf and Matheus Wolf. He was one of the eight that built the first St.
Paul Lutheran church near Java, which later was moved to town, being a
member for 45 years. His first wife, Katharina, was also an active and
religious member.

Christian Pfitzer was well-known in Walworth and surrounding counties,
as he was, at one time, one of the most widely known cattle and
livestock buyers in the territory being associated with the Pfitzer,
Helm and Schlaht firm well known by Commission firms in South St. Paul.
Later he was also associated with Jake Henne and Fred Spiry and with
Jake Weil and Art Lane of Aberdeen, and Charlie Brown of Onida. He had
lived a retired life, however, for a number of years before his death
of a sudden heart attack on March 15, 1940, at the age of 76 years, 6
months and 28 days.

His father, Johann Pfitzer was born Aug. 16, 1833, and his mother
Margaretha Pfitzer was born May 1, 1829.

In Dr. Stumpp's list of settlers in Bessarabia the following entry:
Pfitzer, Georg (born 1805) emigrated from Durrmenz, District of
Muhlacker, Wurtternburg, Germany, to Hoffnungstahl, Bessarabia.

Also mentioned emigrants from Wurtternburg Germany to Russia from
1806-1819' a Johann and Karl Pfitzer, being founders of the Colony
Hoffnungstahl in 1843. Their big town or city being Odessa before
Hoffnungstahl. If family tradition was followed as usually was done in
naming the first born or at least one of the children: The one emigrant
Johann, may have been the grandfather or great-grandfather of Christian
Pfitzer.

        Mr. and Mrs. Matheus J. Pfitzer

                MATHEUS J. PFITZER

Matt J. Pfitzer was born Aug. 21, 1892 in Hoffnungstal, Bessarabia,
South Russia. He was the son of Christian Pfitzer and Katherina Oster
Pfitzer. He migrated to this country with his parents as an infant in
1893 and settled on a farm 5 miles northeast of Java. He had 2
brothers, Christian and Julius, and 2 sisters, Mrs. Adam Eisenbeisz
(Margaretha) and Rosina Pfitzer, who passed away in infancy.

Maria Hepper Pfitzer was born June 20, 1894 at Gilead, South Russia.
She migrated to this country with her widowed mother, Mrs. Jacob Hepper
in May of 1909. The family consisted of four brothers and sister: John,
Christina, Jake, Louise, Molly, Carl, Philip and Rose. Shortly after
coming to this country, her mother was united in marriage with Jacob
Berg and settled in the Swan Lake area.

Matt and Maria were united in marriage at Java, on April 9, 1912 by
Rev. George Kirchdoerfer. After their marriage they settled on a farm 6
miles northeast of Java. In November, 1919 they moved in to Java. In
March, 1920, Matt went into business with his brother Julius and
brother-in-law, Adam Eisenbeisz. The business was purchased from Carl
Hepper and was known as the City Billiard Parlor and Barber Shop. They
operated this business until 1928 and the partnership was dissolved.
During the operation of this business, they also operated a Pfitzer and
Eisenbeisz threshing rig, and also did side-walk farming. In the spring
of 1931 they moved back to the Pfitzer farm. They changed from a Java
to a rural residence several times until 1952, when they purchased the
Sam Oster residence.

Eight boys were born to Matt and Maria; Art and Bernard ( deceased in
infancy ) Gehbardt, Chris, Harold, Marvin, Robert and Wayne.

Gehbardt, born Feb. 8, 1912, married Hulda Wolf and they have one
daughter, Lynda. Gehbardt and his wife, Hulda, are now living in Java,
where Gehbardt is the school custodian.

Chris J., born April 3, 1917, married Lillian Zeller, and they have 2
sons and 1 daughter, Dennis, Neal and Karen. Chris and Lillian live in
Java, where Chris is a rural mail carrier .

Harold, born May 7, 1920, married Helen Haux and they have 3 sons,
Rodney, Charles, and Randy. Harold and Helen live on a farm 4 miles
north of Java.

Marvin, born June 22, 1928, married Betty Spiry and they have 2
daughters and 1 son, Ivy, Gwen and Kent. Marvin and Betty live in Java,
where Marvin is a rural mail carrier.

Robert, born April 5, 1930, married Joyce Rosin and they have 1 son and
1 daughter, Craig and Tamara. Robert and Joyce live in Java, where
Robert is owner and manager of the Java Red Owl store.

Wayne, born Dec. 24, 1932, married Arlene Buechler, and they have 2
sons and 1 daughter, Perry, Penny and Kelly. Wayne and Arlene live in
Minneapolis, Minn., where Wayne is employed by the Anoka Gas Co.

Maria Pfitzer passed away on March 23, 1958.

On October 16, 1958, Matt married Louise Litz Pfitzer, and they resided
in Java until the time of his death, Jan. 26, 1965.

        Mr. and Mrs. Julius Pfitzer

                JULIUS PFITZER

Julius Pfitzer was born Sept. 9, 1902 on his father's homestead, 5E!
miles northeast of Java. He was the son of Christian and Katharina
Pfitzer. In 1909, Julius started school, but was detained because of
his mother's death in 1918. He, himself missed quite a lot of school
because of the flu epidemic but had finished the first year of high
school.

In 1919, Julius and his brother Matt and brother-in-law, Adam
Eisenbeisz bought the City Billiard Parlor and Barber Shop. In 1921, he
went to the Twin City Barber College and completed a course in
Tonsorial Arts, which profession he followed for 14 years. The Pfitzer
and Eisenbeisz partnership also crop farmed and did custom threshing
for several years.

In 1925, Julius was united in marriage to Amelia Fiechtner, who was
born Dec. 13, 1903, to Gottlieb II and Karolina Fiechtner. She grew to
womanhood in the Java area and attended rural school and later attended
high school, where she graduated. Three children were born to this
couple who are: Gerald, married and residing in Minneapolis, Minn.,
Orville, married and teaching school at Britton where he resides; Julie
Ann (Mrs. James Leibel) residing at Aberdeen, S.D.

Julius was appointed Postmaster of Java on July 19, 1935 and took
office Aug. 16, 1935, which office he held until his retirement on Oct.
31, 1969. Previous to that, he had served as a sub rural mail carrier
since 1931. He has also served many other offices in the Java
Community. Being City Police Officer for a number of years; member of
the Java Board of Education for 15 years; member of the City Council
for 12 years, also Justice of Peace for a few years and was City Auditor
of Java, 1959-1975; he was Chief of the Java Volunteer Fire Dept. for
20 years and still a member; was President of the Java Chamber of
Commerce for 14 years. In 1960 he was named to the S.D. Baseball Hall
of Fame for the contribution to the sport for nearly 40 years as
manager of the city and Legion teams. This recognition and honor was
given him at the Fourth Annual Meeting Night at Faulkton, S.D., with
300 in attendance at the S.D. Amateur Baseball Hall of Fame Dinner. Of
this honor he is very proud, being a born lover of baseball. At
present, he is serving as chairman of the Walworth County Zoning and
Planning Commission; he also is Civil Defense Director, passing a test
and holder of certificate in Civil Defense from the University of Ver-
million, S.D. Both he and his wife have been members of the St. Paul
Lutheran church choir and i are life long members of the church.

                MR. AND MRS. JACOB PLEINIS, SR.

Jacob Pleinis Sr., and his wife, Magdalena, nee Landenberger, were
immigrants from Russia. They, and their two oldest daughters, Marie and
Magdalena landed in New York on May 3, 1889 on the ship "Marsala".

Mr. Pleinis filed for land five miles north of Java and with the help
of his brother-in-law, Carl Schlepp, who had come to this area two
years before, he was able to buy an ox, a cow and he borrowed Mr.
Schlepp's break plow. He then broke up a few acres of sod and planted
wheat, potatoes and some other vegetables. He also dug a well and built
a two-room sod house. With the help of neighbors, the house was built
in three days. The timber for the roof was obtained from the Missouri
river area, which was twenty-five miles west of the homestead. It took
two days to go by horse and wagon to get the river wood.

There were many hardships and there were Indian scares. The family had
very little money, but got along by milking cows, raising beef, pork
and chickens, and planting large gardens.

Mr. Pleinis became well known in the Java area. When the railroad came
through Java, he was a carpenter's helper and helped build some of the
first buildings in Java as the Hofmeyer Store, the Hotel, and the Cook
and Lakeman Store. He was a school board member and served as a county
assessor and a county commissioner. After he moved to town in 1924, he
was the janitor of the Lutheran Church and he served as the town
marshal for several years.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Pleinis, Sr.

Mr. and Mrs. Pleinis were the parents of ten children. The eight
daughters were: Marie, (Mrs. Martin Fiedler), Magdalena, (Mrs. Fred
Rosin), Margaretha, (Mrs. Christ Nusz), Katherina, (Mrs. Gottlieb
Leidholt), Martha, (Mrs. Charley Spiry), Mathilda, (Mrs. John Flemmer),
Emma (Mrs. Henry Haux), and Ruth, (Mrs. John Warner). The two sons,
Jacob, married to Emily Kalmbach and John, married to Marie Kalmbach
were the musical members of the family as they both played violins as
well as other instruments.

Mrs. Pleinis passed away in 1918, and Mr. Pleinis passed away in 1943
at the age of eighty-four years.

                ANDREW RABA

Andrew Raba was born at Oberwarth, Hungary, Sept. 10, 1881, and at the
age of 11, he came to the United States. He lived with his father on a
farm southeast of Selby.

He married Theresa Bertha, June 5, 1906, and they were the first couple
married in St. Anthony's Catholic church. They lived to celebrate their
Golden 50th anniversary.

When they first married in 1906, they homesteaded eleven and a half
miles southeast of Selby. Their first child, Theresa was born there. In
1908, they moved and lived with his father-in-law Ladislaus Bertha, on
a farm located 10 miles southeast of Selby. It was here that their
family was blessed with five more children: Martin, Gabriel, Ladislaus,
Anna and Paul.

Andrew built a new modern home along with complete farm buildings in
1918 and moved there in the spring of 1919. Moving at that time was
with horse and buggy. It was in their new home that the family was
completed with four more children: Philip, Mark, Katherine and Irene.

He was the first one in the community to have electricity. He enjoyed
horses, so he traded and sold them. He farmed with horses up until
1943, when he bought his first tractor.

Mr. Raba lost his wife on May 3, 1961, and at the time of his death, he
still resided on his farm. When Mr. Raba passed away in June, 1970, he
had 75 grandchildren and 50 great grandchildren.

                STEVE RABA


Steve Raba was born near Budapest, Hungary, where he grew up and
received a good Hungarian education.

Mrs. Raba died in 1887, leaving her husband with seven small children
to care for. Mr. Raba kept his home for a couple of years. Finding it
hard to make a living for his family, because of Hungary's over-crowded
population, Mr. Raba decided to come to the new land which everyone was
hearing so much about.

He left his five youngest children with their grandfather and they were
later taken by an aunt, due to their grandfather's death. Steve Raba
and his oldest daughter, Julia started for the United States. Landing in
1890, they came directly to this part of the country through the
influence of friends. He homesteaded on land in Walworth county and
made plans to prepare a home for his family.

In 1893 with that money Mr. Raba had saved and with what he borrowed
from Emory Bruckner, he sent for his four remaining children: Agnes,
age 13; Andrew 11; John 9; and Rosie, 7 years of age. Mike having come
over before with a friend.

Mr. Raba lived on his farm southeast of Selby until 1909, when he sold
it to his son, John. In 1910 Steve Raba moved to Canada, where he died
some years later at the age of 86.

                CHRIST W. RAU JR.

Christ W. Rau Jr., was born on Dec. 16, 1891 on a farm north of Java,
where his parents Christ Rau Sr., and his wife, Christina (Weller) had
homesteaded. Christ Jr., helped with the farming and when his older
brother, Gust entered the service, most of the responsibility was left
up to Christ.

Christ grew to young manhood and remained with his parents, helping
them farm until 1916. On Jan. 9, 1916, Christ was united in marriage to
Louisa Biel, who was the daughter of Fred Biel, born Aug. 22, 1892 in
Odessa, South Russia. The young couple moved on a farm one mile east of
his parents homestead.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christ W. Rau, Jr.

To this couple were born six children while living and working on their
farm north of Java. They were: Eugene, born Feb. 18, 1918, and married
Loretta Eberhart, have two sons and two daughters, now own and operate
a farm north of Java. Viola, born Dec. 18, 1920, married Andrew Munsch,
have one daughter, (married). They reside on a farm north of Java.
Clara, born July 22, 1924, married Wilmer Munsch, have one son and they
reside at Mobridge, S.D. Norma, born Oct. 5, 1927, married Eugene
Ackerman and have 5 sons. They now reside at Greeley, Colo. Olivia,
born July 6, 1929, married Edwin Munsch (deceased), have two sons.
Olivia resides at Clear Lake, S.D. Herbert, born Feb. 6, 1934, married
Shirley Oberlander, have one daughter and 3 sons. They now own and
operate the homestead north of Java.

                GUST RAU

Gust Rau was born May 12, 1895 and lived on a farm with his parents,
north of Java, which they homesteaded. When Gust was four years of age,
his mother passed away and a year later, his father remarried.

Gust has many memories of his father and mother, traveling with the
buggy, and delivering cream, butter and eggs to a little town near by.
He also remembers the daily chores, his brother, Christ and himself
would have slopping the hogs. Helping his blind stepmother in the
kitchen, was another achievement for Gust, as he was very handy in
cooking, especially after his sister was married and left home.

        The Gust Rau family

The activation of Java ensued Albert Lange's store, creamery and
postoffice to be moved to town, which was located near the Rau farm.
Mail was delivered by Gottlieb Friemark, who was the mailman and his
deliveries were to Eureka, and back until the postoffice was moved to
Java. A small schoolhouse was located near the Rau farm, which was
attended by Gust and the neighboring children. their first teacher was
Fred Krause, who taught there for a number of years. Gust finished the
fifth grade before he quit school.

In 1917, Gust started to farm for himself and started to build himself
a little home, but was obstructed by the draft. At the age of 21, he
was drafted into the Army and on June 3, 1918, he left for Camp Funston
until July 7, when the 89th Division arrived in London. Their Division
was shipped to Camp Descouge, France, for more training and later served
in the front lines for three months until Armistice was signed. His
Division was sent to Speicher, Germany until May 9, and sent on to
France, where they received their orders to be shipped back to the
United States. He arrived in Camp Mills, New York on May 24, 1919, then
was sent to Camp Dodge, Iowa, where he received his discharge.

After returning home, Gust built a small home, with some help from a
friend, John Weller and relatives. In a short time, Gust bought himself
a new Ford car and escorted the girls around.

On Dec. 14, 1919 Gust was united in marriage to Martha Hirsch in the
Reformed Country church by the Rev. Nusz. The young couple remained
only a short time on this small farm before moving to the farm which is
now owned and operated by the Leslie Rau family. Thirteen children were
born to this couple, one child died when 5 weeks old, and the remaining
are 6 daughters and 6 sons:

Merilda (Mrs. Rodger Christenson) lives in Portland, Ore.; Lavida (Mrs.
Rueben Eberhardt) residing on a farm northeast of Java; Adeline (Mrs.
Wilbert Schnabel) residing on a farm northeast of Eureka; Lorraine
(Mrs. Albert Metzger) residing on a farm near Eureka; Ruth (Mrs. Marvin
Mutschler) residing on a farm south of Eureka; Janice (Mrs. Lloyd
Sieler) residing in California; Ervin, married Lenora Renz, living
north of Java; Stanley married Viola Aman, living south of Eureka;
Perry married Bernice Ehresman living north of Java; Harvey married
Evonne Jeschke, living north of Java; Leslie married Ramona Kezzler,
living north of Java; Clarence (deceased), married Meta Holzworth.

Gust and Martha's prayers were answered many times, living during the
depression and drought, trying to raise a large family. In 1949, Gust
and Martha bought a home and moved to Eureka, S.D., to live a retired
life. Many memories are being reminisced by the couple.

                JOHN ROSIN FAMILY

John Rosin was born March 25, 1858 at the village of Hoffnungstal,
Kherson District, South Russia. Here he was baptized and confirmed in
the Evangelical Lutheran church by Pastor Becker and grew to young
manhood.

On June 18, 1877, Mr. Rosin was united in marriage with Christina
Schick, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Wilhelm Schick.

After seven years of married life, the Rosin family left South Russia
and immigrated to America, settling first at Scotland, Dakota
Territory. Later the family moved northward, settling northwest of
Bowdle, and for some years lived on a farm 31/2 miles northeast of
Java, which in later years was the farm of the John Naasz family.

The Rosin family were members of the Neu Glueckstal church (also known
as Buechler church), east of Java, and Mr. Rosin was also a charter
member of St. John Evangelical Lutheran church of Bowdle.

        The John Rosin family, back, left to right, Carrie, Margaret,
        Katherine, Mary. Middle, Father John Rosin, William, Frederick,
        Mother Christina Schick Rosin. Front, twins, Louisa and
        Magdalena.

Being desirous of owning his own land and hearing of the inducements
offered by the Canadian government to attract settlers to Canadian
lands, Mr. Rosin elected to remove to Canada, and on March 8, 1909 at
public auction, sold his personal property, and on March 16, of the
same year, boarded the train at Java, along with his family and others,
to begin the journey to Canada. They soon arrived at Olds, Alberta,
Canada. During that summer they moved to their homestead in Alpha
School District in the Hand Hills, near Hanna, Alberta, Canada.

In 1924, Mrs. Rosin passed away and in 1930 John Rosin moved into the
town of Hanna, where he lived until his passing on Sept. 9, 1937.

Mr. and Mrs. Rosin were the parents of twelve children, two of whom
survive, Mrs. Lydia Griffith of Lloydminister Sask., Canada, and Mrs.
Louisa Hermans of Milbank, S.D. Two great-grandchildren of the John
Rosins' live in the near area, Mrs. Adam Lutz on a farm near Artas, S.
D., and LeRoy Bender of Java.

                MR. AND MRS. WILLIAM ROSIN

William Rosin was born February 21, 1880 in Hoffnungstal, South
Russia. His wife Dorothea Stoecker was born in the same village on
March 13, 1882. They were married there in October, 1906.

Mr. and Mrs. Rosin and their two small children, Tillie and John left
Russia on December 9, 1909, as Mr. Rosin wanted to escape from the
compulsory military service imposed on every able bodied man. Having
served four years in the Russian Army and the Russo-Japanese war, he was
anxious to find a better place for the family to live.

On December 15, they, with Mrs. Rosin's sister, Rosina Stoecker, Mrs.
Rose (Christ) Gab, now living in Eureka, South Dakota, boarded the ship
"Estonia" and arrived in New York on January 1, 1910. They came to
South Dakota by train settling north of Java, in Walworth county, making
their home that winter and the first summer with his brother, Fred and
family who had come to America a few years earlier.

        Mr. and Mrs. William Rosin

Making a living for his family was not easy, as they had very little to
start with. In the fall of 1910, they rented a farm near Java and
resided there for four years, after which they moved to a farm north of
Selby where they lived until 1943 when they retired and moved to Selby.
Mr. Rosin passed away in December, 1952. Mrs. Rosin lived in Selby
until her death in November, 1968.

Their five children were Tillie, (Mrs. William Sauer) living on a farm
east of Java, John, living in Selby, William who farms northeast of
Selby, Clara (Mrs. Harold Fiedler) of Aberdeen, and Fred of Aberdeen,
South Dakota. They had 16 grandchildren.

        George W. Ryan

                GEORGE W. RYAN

George W. Ryan was the father of Mrs. Peter L. (Ethel) Nies, Mrs.
Joseph (Helen) Zarka, and Mrs. Robert (Mabel) Wagy. He lived in Java
from the year 1908 to 1938.

Mr. Ryan, great, great grandson of John Ryan who immigrated from
Ireland in 1710, was born in Fairview, Erie county, Pennsylvania Jan.
30, 1851. He married Mary A. Zindel, June 14, 1881.

In April 1883, he came to Dakota Territory, motivated by the land boom
in progress and the encouragement of the eminent journalist Horace
Greeley, who said, "Go West young man and grow up with the country".

He rode the train to Aberdeen, the only "Hub of the universe west of
Boston". Upon arrival he learned, the only patch of land left and still
obtainable was in the neighborhood of Swan Lake, which had been
described in real estate brochures, as a beautiful lake and river
flowing westward until it formed a junction with the Big Muddy, right
where the great city and beautiful capital of the wonderland state was
sure to be located.

According to the advertising media of the day, a strip of timber two
miles wide was said to extend the whole length of Swan River. Abundant
game, fish, wild fruit, yellow sulfur springs, black sulfur springs,
beaver dams, mill sites, and dam sites were in evidence. How could
anyone pass up a situation like this?

Mr. Ryan acquired an oxen team and supplies and headed west for the
land of beauty and promise. Fortunately one member of the party knew
how to drive an ox team.

You can imagine their surprise when they arrived at Swan Lake. The
whole prairie was as barren of vegetation as a floor. Not a tree in
sight. The prairie grass had been burned off slick and clean for
hundreds of miles in every direction. The ox driver aptly described the
situation as a "damn sight". To quote Mr. Ryan "We couldn't go back and
we couldn't go ahead".

After a period of adjustment, he proved up on a preemption claim which
apparently was some type of experiment in obtaining land. A parody on
"The Old Oaken Bucket" recalls this ordeal in part, to quote a portion:

"How dear to the heart are the scenes of preempting,
 When fond recollections present them to view,
 The filing, the proving, the swearing so tempting
 And every loved spot that my preemption knew."

Mr. Ryan bought and traded land until he was established on a farm
southeast of Java. He brought his family, (wife and daughter, Mabel
Abagail) to Dakota in June, 1885.

Before moving to Java he lived in Bangor, which was the county seat. He
was the County Auditor for four years from 1903 to 1907. This was
during the time when the old courthouse was demolished, and the
fixtures and records were illegally carried to Selby. His house was
moved from Bangor to Selby with some kind of tractor. During the move,
the family stayed in and traveled with the house. The house was placed
in the southwestern part of Java and was occupied during the summer
months by Mrs. Peter L. (Ethel) Nies.

Mr. Ryan was always active in community and political affairs. He
received his college education in Ohio and taught school in
Pennsylvania and Dakota Territory. He represented Campbell, Walworth,
Potter, Hide and Hand counties in the last territorial legislature,
which was held at Bismarck, Dakota Territory in 1889. He was a member of
the South Dakota Senate in 1911. He was one of the founding members of
the Java Equity and was treasurer of Ryan School district for 26 years.

All of the daughters attended grade school when on the farm. Helen
attended high school in Java, where she graduated in June 1909. At that
time it was a two year school Both Ethel and Helen attended Northern
State Normal in Aberdeen, and taught in the Spring Valley, Ryan and Swan
Lake schools.

Mabel Ryan, married Robert Wagy, moved to North Dakota, and raised a
large family. Helen Ryan married Joseph Zarka, who later on was a World
War I soldier and served in Europe. Ethel E. Ryan married Peter L. Nies.

Mrs. Peter L. (Ethel) Nies was the last remaining member of the Ryan
family, and had fond memories of living near and in Java, recalling
shopping at the Lange, Bauer and Gershin stores. She remembered
visiting with the Hall girls at the Hall Hotel and dining in the
Lakeman Cafe.

She had memories of being in Java on Saturday and Wednesday nights to
do the shopping and to visit with all the friends who congregated in
the stores while the children attended the movie or played on the
sidewalks.

During these years, Java was a real active town and an air of
friendship and concern for others prevailed.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Sauer

                CHRISTIAN SAUER

Christian Sauer was born Sept. 6, 1878, in Bersola, South Russia, the
first son of Christian Sauer Sr., a carpenter, and his wife, Carolina
Fiechtner. Very early in his life the family moved from Bersola to
Hoffnungstal, where the young Christ was baptized. A later move was
made to Kassel, where the received his religious instruction and was
confirmed at the age of 14.

In 1891, the elder Christian passed away. Two years later, Caroline
Sauer, with her sons, Christ and Jacob, immigrated to the United
States. Their first location was Bridgewater, S.D., where they stayed
only four months, relocating to a farm three miles north of Java. Here
they stayed for a short time, but a transient life seemed inevitable
for this family and they moved again.

The next move, in 1894, was to Santa Rosa, Calif. Here they stayed for
several years, and the widowed mother remarried. In 1897, they returned
to Walworth county, South Dakota, and homesteaded land 6 miles
southeast of Java.

During these years, Christ was employed by various farming neighbors.
It was while helping in this way that a serious accident occurred that
had some pleasant consequences. A barn containing horses was afire
and Christ bravely attempted their rescue, incurring first degree burns
on his hands and lesser burns on other parts of his body. He was taken
to the home of Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Fiechtner and was nursed there by
their daughter, Louise. She became his bride in 1901.

Christ and Louise homesteaded the farm 6 miles east of Java, and
encountered the severe trials and hard times familiar to most pioneer
families of this period.

During those years, there was never-ending, back-breaking work to do,
and a farmer had to be skilled in many trades if his farm was to
prosper. Christ met this requirement with capable versatility. He had
learned from his father the basics of carpentry; he could butcher meat
to the satisfaction of his neighbors, and he proved himself many times
as a competent emergency dentist.

Although the farm demanded long hours and hard work, the spiritual life
was not neglected. Christ served as a deacon in the church, and in the
absence of the minister would read the sermons and conduct the service.

Christ and Louise were blessed with a family of six children: Rose
(Mrs. Christ Sulzle), now deceased, was born in 1902. She had five
children and 19 grandchildren. Christ Jr., born 1903, now lives on a
farm northeast of Roscoe, S.D. He has six children and 15
grandchildren. Katie, born in 1905, is married to Reinhold Sackman and
lives in Java, S.D. William, born in 1907, farms the land homesteaded
by his father. His wife is the former Tillie Rosin, Selby, and they
have four children and fifteen grandchildren. Louise, born in 1908,
arrived in the world under unique circumstances. Assisting her mother
during the birth was her great-grandmother, Louise Spiry, for whom she
was named. Louise is married to John Rosin of Selby, and has two
children and three grandchildren. Viola, born in 1915, married Henry
Humann (now deceased) of Eureka, and they had 5 children and 6
grandchildren. Viola now resides in Timber Lake, S.D., with her second
husband, Alf Malloy.

Christ Sauer was active in community affairs and enjoyed the high
regard of his neighbors. He lived a full and productive life until his
death, Nov. 6, 1933.

His widow stayed on at the farm with her son William and his family
until 1935, at which time she married Adolph Stangl of Java. Mr. Stangl
died in 1968, and Louise Sauer Stangl has continued to make her home in
Java. Now at 91 she is active with her garden and caring for her home, a
superb example of strength and resourcefulness to all those who know
her.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Sauer

                JACOB SAUER FAMILY

Jacob Sauer was born on Jan. 21, 1890 at Hoffnungstal, South Russia. He
was the son of Christian and Carolina (Fiechtner) Sauer. His father
passed away when Jacob was 2 years old. Shortly after his death,
Carolina, with her two children, Jacob and Christian II, came to
America to live with her parents. In 1892, they came to America and
moved in with her father and mother for a number of years.

Jacob grew to manhood while living in the Java area where he stayed
with his older brother Christian II and his family. On Nov. 15, 1910,
Jacob married Frieda Wolff, daughter of Matheus G. and Fredricka
(Jenner) Wolff, who was born on Dec. 21, 1888 at Marionsberg, South
Russia. Frieda came to America with her parents who homesteaded 2 miles
northeast of Java.

The young couple moved on a farm near her parents which was then the
Link Hughes homestead. Farming and raising livestock was their
livelihood and many hardships were endured by this young couple. Three
boys were born to Jacob and Frieda while living on this farm. Five
years later, the couple moved on a farm northwest of Java, where a
fourth son was born. Three years later, misfortune struck the family
when Jacob passed away. Shortly after Jacob's death, Frieda and her
four small children moved to Java, where she purchased a home and still
resides there at the present time. Frieda encountered many conflicts
trying to support her children. She became a seamstress and provided
her family with all the necessities and they all received their
education at the Java School. Later, her sons married and left to make
their homes elsewhere. Frieda's sons were:

Gerhardt, born May 1, 1912; married Josephine Dusel and reside at
Eugene, Ore.; they have 2 sons, Roger married and Dennis (deceased) was
in the Service and died of a drowning accident while stationed at
Seattle, Wash. Arnold, born June 16, 1913; married Ellen Aman and
reside at Minneapolis, Minn.; they have one daughter Loren, teaching at
Los Angeles, Calif., and one son, Gerald, married and employed at
Minneapolis. Walter, born Dec. 16, 1914 married Lois Best and reside at
Bellevue, Wash. They have one son, Robert. Herbert, born May 8, 1917,
married Leota Wolf and reside at Hopkins, Minn. They have one son Jim
employed at Minneapolis, Minn., and one daughter Lynda married Jim Burh
and live at St. Paul, Minn., where they are both employed.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Schlaht

                JACOB SCHLAHT

Jacob Schlaht was born Sept. 6, 1873 at the village of Neugluckstal,
Odessa District, South Russia, the son of Michael and Magdalena Opp
Schlaht.

In 1885, young Jacob, together with his parents and brothers and
sisters, left South Russia and immigrated to America, settling on a
homestead southwest of Eureka, Dakota Territory.

Hardships were great during those first years in a new land, and
coupled with a six year drought in the 80's and 90's; yet the family
survived. During this period Jacob grew to young manhood and early in
1902, along with brothers, George and John, established a business in
the booming new town of Java, in Walworth county, the business being a
saloon, pool hall, and refreshment parlor, known as Schlaht Brothers.

On July 18, 1905, Mr. Schlaht was united in marriage with Margaret
Rosin, second eldest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Rosin of near Java,
the ceremony being performed by Rev. George Kirchdoerfer at Java.

In June of 1907, the Schlaht Bros., Jacob and John, erected a modern
new business building, and remodeled an existing building, adding new
up-to-date fixtures, and generally expanding the business, completing
the same just in time to hold a Grand Ball in conjunction with the
usually fine and gala Fourth of July celebration staged by the City of
Java. Also in July, 1907, Mr. and Mrs. Schlaht commenced the erection
of a fine new residence in the east part of town.

On the 23rd day of January, 1909, the partnership of Schlaht Brothers
was dissolved, and Jacob continued the saloon business until Feb. 25,
1910, on which date he sold his business holdings in Java.

The family remained in Java until 1911, when Mr. and Mrs. Schlaht moved
to a farm in Campbell county, 9 miles east of Mound City, and resided
there until 1939, when they moved to Eureka, S.D. In 1944 they again
moved, this time to Aberdeen, S.D., where more steady employment was
available. Later, however, Mr. Schlaht was forced to retire by an
increasingly painful hip ailment.

The family circle was broken on Dec. 9, 1955, when Mrs. Schlaht passed
away, attaining the age of 71 years, having been born Nov. 8, 1884, at
Scotland, Dakota Territory, growing to young womanhood in the Bowdle and
Java area. Mr. Schlaht remained in Aberdeen, living in the family
residence until his passing on April 11, 1958, reaching the age of 85
years.

Mr. and Mrs. Schlaht were the parents of two children, both of whom
survive, Alvina Bender of Aberdeen, and Edmund Schlaht of Eureka, S.
D., and two grandchildren also survive, Mrs. Adam Lutz of near Artas,
S.D., and LeRoy Bender of Java.

                JACOB SCHLAHT

Jacob Schlaht was born on a farm about 15 miles northeast of Java,
where he grew to manhood and farmed with his parents. In 1917 he was
united in marriage to Helen Schuh, who was born March 3, 1897. She grew
to womanhood in the Java area. After their marriage they made their home
in Montana in the Miles City area, where they made their home for some
time and three sons, Wilbur, deceased, Robert, and Reuben were born to
them. After a time, the family left the farm and moved to California,
where they made their home, where Mrs. Schlaht is still residing, but
Jacob has passed away.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Schlaht

                AUGUST C. and OTTILIE E. SCHLEPP

August C. Schlepp was born on a farm 5 miles north of Java on Aug. 20,
1890, to Karl and Katherine Schlepp. He moved to Java at the age of 18
years and worked for the firm of Bauer and Stoller General Merchandise
for 2 years. He entered Molar Barber College in Minneapolis, Minnesota,
graduating as a master barber in 1910. He operated a recreation parlor
and barber shop for many years, converting it to a drug store and
barbershop in 1927.

During his lifetime he was active in many civic affairs, serving as
treasurer of the Java School District for 12 years, City Assessor for 3
years, City Auditor 7 years, and City Mayor for 18 consecutive years.
He was instrumental in obtaining the waterworks and sewer systems in
Java during his term as Mayor. He also served as president of the Java
Chamber of Commerce, Treasurer of the St. Paul Lutheran Church for many
years and in 1950 was appointed chairman of the executive committee for
the city's Golden Jubilee Festival. In 1961 he was elected Chairman of
the Walworth County Executive Committee for the Dakota Territory
Centennial.

In 1956 he was elected Walworth County Treasurer and served in that
capacity for two terms and Walworth County Register of Deeds for two
terms, serving until December 31, 1964 when he retired from active
office.

Mr. Schlepp was an accomplished violinist, playing with various musical
groups and organizations during his lifetime. He conducted the school
band and orchestra for a number of years, directed the municipal band
for 20 years and a charter member of the Civic Symphony Orchestra at
Aberdeen, S.D., for many years serving as concertmaster of that
organization for a number of years.

August and Ottilie (Bauer) Schlepp were united in marriage on April 21,
1912 at the Congregational Church in Java. They resided in the City of
Java all of their married life, Mr. Schlepp passed away June 23, 1966
and Ottilie Schlepp on April 6, 1971.

The children of August and Ottilie Schlepp are the following: Lenore
(Jack) Bieber, Gilbert G. Schlepp, Percy Schlepp, Juliet (Milton)
Wendling, Lawrence Schlepp and Kathleen Mewing.

Lenora and Jack Bieber, Warner, S.D., James, Jeanette, Sally, Peggy,
Julie and Lenora Kay.

Gilbert and LaRayne (Job) Schlepp, Java, S.D., Barbara and Carolyn.

Percy and Vera (Schanzenbach) Schlepp, Denver, Colo., Dennis.

Lawrence and Joyce (Vojta) Schlepp, Arvada, Colo., Kenneth, Rebecca,
Greg, Debbie, Connie and Todd.

Juliet and Milton Wendling, Aberdeen, S.D., Michael, Jackie and
Elizabeth.

Kathleen Mewing, Gettysburg, S.D. Paul, Colleen and Glen.

        Mr. and Mrs. Emanuel Schlepp

                EMANUEL AND LYDIA SCHLEPP

Emanuel Schlepp was born May 11, 1895 in Hoffnungstal, South Russia and
came to America with his parents, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Schlepp. The
family came to the Dakota Territory and homesteaded three miles
northeast of Bowdle, where he grew to young manhood.

On July 24, 1918, Emanuel was drafted into the Army, serving in the
Belgium and France sector during World War I. After his discharge and a
few years later, he was united in marriage to Lydia Schuh. She was born
Sept. 3, 1903 to John and Fredericka Schuh at their home, which was
located five miles northeast of Java.

After their marriage, Lydia and Emanuel lived in Bowdle where he was
employed. Three years later they moved to Mahto, S.D., where they
began farming and continued farming for 14 years. They returned to the
Java area and farmed 5 miles northeast of Java. A few years later they
bought a farm four miles east of Java, where they farmed until 1952
when they sold their farm and moved to Java, where they purchased a
home.

In 1953 they purchased Schlepps Cafe which they operated until 1960
when they retired in Java, where they reside at this time and are
living a retired life.

Four daughters were born to the Schlepp family and they all received
their education in the Java school.

Agnes married Virgil Haskell (deceased) and has four children, Gregory,
Cheryl and Rhonda all employed in Rapid City, and Scott attending Rapid
City Central. They are all living in Rapid City.

Juanita married William Johnson; employed and living in Albuquerque,
New Mexico.

Sallie married Harvey Weisgram and both employed and living in
Mobridge. They have one daughter who is attending the Mobridge school.

Gail married Robert Goeman who is employed at Western States Life
Insurance. They have one daughter, Mary and one son, Mike, both
attending grade school and they all reside in Aberdeen, S.D.

                JOHN J. SCHLEPP SR.

John J. Schlepp Sr., was born in 1837 in Hoffnungstal, South Russia,
where he grew to manhood. He was united in marriage to Magdelena
Miller, who was born in 1840 also in the South Russian area.

        Mr. and Mrs. John J. Schlepp, Sr.

This young couple made their home in South Russia, where John worked at
the carpenter trade and Magdelena was a midwife, a service which she
continued for the early pioneers after migrating to America.

In 1889 Mr. and Mrs. Schlepp and two sons, David and Adolph along with a
married daughter Mrs. Fredricka Schlepp (Schuh) and a son John left
their home in Russia and migrated to America. They landed in New York
and from there, they made their way westward to Dakota Territory and
homesteaded in the Java area and farming became their livelihood.

In 1909, the Schlepps purchased a home in Java, where they retired
until their death. Mr. Schlepp passed away in 1913 and Mrs. Schlepp in
1927.

                JOHN J. SCHLEPP JR.

John J. Schlepp Jr., was the eldest son of John J. Schlepp Sr., and
Magdelena. He was born May 28, 1863 at Hoffnungstal, South Russia. On
Dec. 12,1885, John was married to Helena Doebele at Kloestitz, South
Russia.

In June 1889 John, his wife Helena, their eldest daughter Pauline (now
Mrs. Reinhold Naasz) and their infant son immigrated to the United
States. The son died enroute and was buried at sea.

John was one of the oldest settlers in the Dakotas. The family
homesteaded in Emmons county near Linton, N.D. In 1891, they moved to
Walworth county and made their home on a farm seven miles northeast of
Java.

To the John J. Schlepp Jr., family thirteen children were born: Pauline
(Mrs. Reinhold Naasz) resides at Miles City, Mont.; the infant
son who died enroute at sea; Helena (Mrs. Rudolph Walther)
deceased, previously resided in Java; Samuel married Nathlia Schlepp,
deceased, then married Christina Wolf, deceased, then married Magdelena
Goetz and now reside in Mobridge, S.D. - Magdelena (Mrs. John Pfaff)
resides in Miles City, Mont.; Rosie (Mrs. Fred Obenauer) deceased,
previously resided at Eureka, S.D. Edward, married Justina Wolf, wife
deceased, now resides in Beverly Hills, Calif.; Lydia (Mrs. Adolph
Pfaff) husband deceased, she resides in Java; John Schlepp married
Elizabeth Wolf, wife deceased, resides in Aberdeen, S.D.; Ann (Mrs.
Mike Schmierer) resides in Bowdle, S.D.; Kathryn (Mrs. Ted Spiry)
resides in Mobridge, S.D.; Jacob married Elizabeth George wife
deceased, resides in Selby, S.D.; Mathilda (Mrs. Benjamin Halverson
Webber) resides at Miles City, Mont.

        The John J. Schlepp, Jr. family

John and Helena and family resided on their homestead seven miles north
of Java for thirty-three years. He had filed his claim in 1891 and to
retain their claim, improvements had to be made. John and his family
plowed the fields with a one horse plow. They had to dig a well by hand
with a spade and build a sod house for shelter. In order to obtain
supplies and materials necessary for improvements on the land, they
traveled by wagon to the nearest town, which was Eureka, S.D.

In 1922, the parents retired from active farming and moved to Java,
where they made their home. The homestead was left to the sons Edward
and John to continue farming.

In 1936, the Schlepps celebrated their Golden Wedding Anniversary after
having survived the many hardships and the depression of thirty-two.
They contributed a great deal to the settlement of the Java area before
John died on April 6, 1938 and his wife Helena, died in 1952.

        The John Schmeichel family

                JOHN SCHMEICHEL

John Schmeichel was born Jan. 6, 1859 at Gluckstal, Russia, where he
grew to manhood. He married Sophia Straub and eight children were born
to this couple. The Schmeichel family came to America and settled in
the Mound City area.

After his wife's death, John married Elizabeth Quenzer and seven
children were born to this couple. The Schmeichel family homesteaded
seven miles southeast of Mound City. Many hardships were endured with
this large family.

In 1900, the Schmeichel family moved to Selby, where John went into
partnership with John Nusz and Albert Lang. In 1905, the family moved
to Java, where John went into partnership with Philip Spiry and owned
and operated The German State Bank. The Schmeichel family remained in
Java for four years and then decided to move west. In 1909 the family
moved to McLaughlin, S.D., where they remained until their death. John
died February, 1923 and his wife Elizabeth died in 1966.

        Mr. and Mrs. Michael Schmierer

                MR. AND MRS. MICHAEL SCHMIERER

Michael Schmierer Sr., was born on July 21, 1862 in Kleinneudorf,
Russia. His wife, Katherina Kirschenmann was born on August 8, 1866 in
Neu Glueckstal, Russia. They were married in 1889 and their first
daughter, Elizabeth, was born in Neu Glueckstal. They came to America
in 1892 and lived in Naper, Nebraska for four years. During this time a
son, Gustav and a daughter, Emma, were born.

In 1896, the family loaded their belongings onto one wagon and moved to
South Dakota. They stopped at a suitable place and homesteaded ten
miles northeast of Java in Adams Township. The plow was unloaded to
break sod from which the house was built. Within the next eight years,
three more sons were born. They were Michael Jr., in 1898, Emil in 1902,
and John in 1904.

The family bought the Fred Eisenbeisz place and moved one-half mile
farther east. Here, Michael Sr., died in 1911, but Mrs. Schmierer
continued to live on the farm with her children. During the year 1918,
the flu epidemic claimed the lives of daughter, Emma, and son, Emil.

The youngest son, John, entered the seminary and became a pastor of the
American Lutheran Church. He served parishes in both North and South
Dakota and retired recently after serving 45 years in the ministry. He
was married to the former Marie Bieber in 1930. They now reside in
Bowdle, South Dakota.

In 1920 Michael Jr., was married to Anna Schlepp. He took over the home
place to which in the course of years a few more quarters of land were
added. His mother moved to Bowdle and resided there until her death in
1943.

To the Michael Jrs., three sons were born: The oldest Reuben, moved to
Java, where he was married to Eileen Fiedler; Theodore, the youngest,
married Erna Goetz and moved to Aberdeen. He passed away in July 1,
1962. That left Elmer, married to Martha Lorenz, living on the farm
with his parents. In September, 1970, the Michael Schmierers moved to
Bowdle, where they are now residing.

The Schmierers also raised a niece of Mrs. Schmierer's. She is the
former Alvina Walther, now Mrs. Albert Quenzer of Artas, S.D. She came
to live with them at the age of six years after her own mother, Mrs.
Rudolph Walther, passed away.

Recollections of much hard work and thriftiness can be recalled by each
of the three generations that lived on the original homestead.

                MR. AND MRS. CHRISTIAN SCHNAIBLE

Christian Schnaible was born November 2, 1879 at Hoffnungstal, South
Russia, a son of Karl Schnaible and Elizabeth (Oster) Schnaible. He
spent his early boyhood in Russia. At the age of 5 years he came to the
United States with his parents and settled near Freeman, S.D. After
five years, the family came to Walworth county and homesteaded north of
Java, where he grew up and acquired what schooling he could which would
be equivalent to the present day third grade .

On March 22, 1903, he was united in marriage to Magdalena Naasz of
Java. Magdalena was also born in Hoffnungstahl, South Russia on October
22, 1882, where she resided until May 5, 1893, when her parents came to
the United States and settled on a homestead near Java.

The young couple, Christian and Magdalena Schnaible, operated a grocery
and dry goods store in Java for a number of years and in 1924, the
family now consisting of four sons moved onto a farm north of Java.
Later in 1939 they moved back to Java on account of Mrs. Schnaible's
health. He was then employed as a carpenter for some years. Mrs.
Schnaible passed away March 9, 1947.

After her death, he continued to reside in Java, and in 1950 he was
named to the position of custodian of St. Paul Lutheran church in Java,
and caretaker of the cemetery, duties which he carried out
conscientiously until his death on August 19, 1954.

They were blessed with five children. The children are: Berthold and
Otto of Java; Arnold of Lodi, California and Traugott of Lynwood,
Calif. One daughter, Delilah, passed away in 1919 at the age of seven.

The Schnaibles were members of St. Paul Lutheran church, and Mrs.
Schnaible was one of the organizers of the Ladies Aid, which is now
known as ALCW. Mr. Schnaible was also a member of the Java City Band.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Schnaible were considered honest and upright
citizens and good neighbors. Their door was open to all and they had
many friends.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Schnaible

                JOHN SCHNAIBLE SR.

John Schnaible Sr., was born Oct. 30, 1871 at Hoffnungstal,
Bessarabia, South Russia and grew to manhood while living with his
parents.

On Jan. 29, 1893, John was united in marriage to Mary Weber, who was
born Jan. 26, 1873 at Kloestitz, South Russia. The young couple had two
children, died in infancy; and in 1902, a daughter, Mary (Mrs. W. J.
Roesler of Selby) was born in the South Russian area.

One year later, John Sr., and his wife Mary and baby undertook the long
journey to come to America. Influenced by his older brother, John and
his family came to the Dakota Territory and came to Java, where he
lived with his brother for a short time.

The Schnaible family rented a farm east of Java, which was owned by
Deable. While making their home on this farm, two sons were added to
this family. They were John Jr. (Java) and Oscar (Java). A few years
later they moved to a farm south of Bowdle, where they again were
engaged in farming. Another son, Emil (Bowdle), joined the Schnaible
family, before moving on a farm ten miles south of Java.

In 1911, the Schnaible family bought the Henry Baer farm where they
farmed until they retired. Many hardships were endured during the
depression and drought. Three more children were born: two daughters,
Lily (Mrs. Emanuel Sieler, Java) and Olga (Mrs. Oscar Fiedler, Java)
and one son, Fred (Selby).

In 1943, John Sr., and Mary bought a home in Java and moved to town,
where they lived a retired life. Only two years of leisure life was
enjoyed by John before his death on May 3, 1945. His wife, Mary, passed
away five years later, on July 2, 1950

John Jr., and his wife own and operate his parents' farm.

        The Karl Schnaible family. Back, Christ and Pauline. Middle,
        Father Karl, Rose, Mary, Mother Elizabeth. Front, Henry and
        Lydia.

                MR. AND MRS. KARL SCHNAIBLE

Karl Schnaible was born March 31, 1857 at Hoffnungstal, Bessarabia,
Roumania. His wife, Elizabeth Oster was born in the same locality. They
were married Feb. 15, 1879. They wanted to get away from Russian rule,
so they, with 3 children came to the U. S. in 1885. They settled on a
farm near Freeman, S.D., where they resided for four years.

In 1889, they came to Walworth county and bought land northwest of
Java, where they lived for 10 years. Their first home near Java was a
sod house, which was replaced by a larger roomy home some years later.

They moved to Java in 1909, where they resided until his death August
1930, at the age of 73. He was ill for many years but was always able
to be up and about. He was thrifty and not afraid of hard work and
always had large herds of sheep and cattle. After moving to Java, he
still helped out at the farm. He was a resident of Walworth county for
41 years. They celebrated their 40th wedding anniversary, March 1929.

Elizabeth Schnaible continued to live in the Java home until unable to
care for herself. She moved to the Eureka Nursing Home, where she
passed away at the age of 93.

There were nine children in this family and one adopted child. Three
passed away in infancy and are buried in a small family cemetery north
of the original farm: Henry, 1896; Magdalena and Elizabeth 1895.

Christ married Magdalena Naasz. Pauline, Mrs. Fred Krause. Rose, Mrs.
Christ Wolf. Mary, Mrs. Emanuel Baer. Lydia, Mrs. George Wolf. Henry,
married Elizabeth Zeller. Edward (adopted), married Rose Wolf.

All except Mary, Mrs. Emanuel Baer and Edward have passed away as the
years have gone by.

        Mr. and Mrs. Henry Schneider

                MR. AND MRS. HENRY SCHNEIDER

Henry Schneider was born to Peter Schneider and Katherina Fuhrman on
Sept. 20, 1888 at Java, South Dakota.

Peter Schneider and Katherina (Fuhrman) Schneider were married in
Rohrbach, Russia. Peter was born on March 10, 1856 and Katherina on May
16, 1857. Their oldest son, Peter Jr., was born in Russia before they
came to America in 1885. Arriving at Menno, S.D., they purchased a
team of oxen, wagon and supplies, which left them without money, and
made the trip to Eureka, S.D. They stayed with Katherine's sister,
Mrs. Fiock. They then moved to a farm 8 miles north of Java, S.D.

There were 8 children born of this marriage: Peter Jr., Medicine Hat,
Alberta, Canada, deceased; Katherine, Mrs. John Hoffman, Long Lake,
deceased; Margaret, Mrs. Ed Blumhart, Ashley, N.D., deceased; Henry,
Java, Selby, deceased; John, Java, S.D.; Elizabeth, Mrs. Fred
Hochstetter, Java, deceased; William, Java, Selby Rest Home; Johana,
Mrs. Jake Naasz, Java, deceased.

After Peter Sr., died on Sept. 22, 1917, Katherina moved to Java, into
what is now known as the Kast house in 1920. Because of a lot of snow
and no roads she had to have her things moved with a team of horses and
sled. She passed away on June 27, 1942.

Henry Schneider was married to Katherine Hauck on Sept. 14, 1911 at the
Sutley Reformed Church.

Henry and Katherine lived on a farm 8 miles north of Java after their
marriage. Seven children were born to them and all lived on farms in
the Java-Selby community. They moved to their new home in Selby in 1949.

Albert, Nov. 6, 1912, Mobridge, S.D.; John J., June 5, 1914, Java, S.
D.; Edna, Mrs. Oscar Himrich, Aug. 29, 1916, Selby, S.D.; Emma, Mrs.
Ervin Haux, Dec. 4, 1917, Java, S.D.; Rinel, Sept. 29, 1920, Java, S.
D.; Irene, Mrs. Rueben Bieber, Mrs. Arthur Fiedler, June 5, 1923, Java,
S.D.; Theodore, June 29, 1926, Java.

Henry passed away on Nov. 13, 1966, and Katherine is still living in
