her home at Selby. At this writing, she has reached the age of 82 years
and is still able to care for herself. They were blessed with 15
grandchildren and 19 great grandchildren.

        Mr. and Mrs. Carl Hauck

Katherine, the wife of Henry Schneider, was the daughter of Carl Hauck
Jr., and Elizabeth Steinbach, who were married at Berlin, South Russia,
and came to America with his folks, Carl Hauck Sr., because of the
strict laws there. They settled on a farm at Scotland, S.D., for a
short time, then moved to a farm 12 miles northwest of Java, S.D. A new
sod house was built on their farm and as a small girl, Katherine helped
make the many mud blocks it took to build it. The family of Carl Hauck
Jr., were:

Fred, Idaho, deceased; Katherine, Java-Selby, Mrs. Henry Schneider;
Carl, Lodi, Calif., deceased; Bertha, Mrs. Henry Brockel, McLaughlin, S.
D.; Theodore, Lodi, Calif., deceased; Elizabeth, Java, S.D., deceased;
Alvina, Java, S.D., deceased; Hertha, Mrs. Sam Spiry, Java, S.D.;
Henry, Mobridge, S.D., deceased.

                MR. AND MRS. HENRY J. SCHRETER

Henry J. Schreter was born in Hoffnungstal, South Russia on November
27, 1885. He came to America with his grandmother and uncle John
Schnaible at a young age. His grandmother became very ill on the ship
coming over. She died and was buried in Canada, so Henry was on his own
at a young age. He worked for his uncle Karl to pay for his trip to
America. After that, he worked a number of years for Ed Jerguson,
southwest of Java, which land is now owned by Orville Walz.

In 1907 he was united in marriage to Marie Baer in a ceremony performed
by Pastor Geo. Kirchdoerfer in the Lutheran church at Java. She was
born Oct. 16, 1886 in Grimm, Russia, a daughter of John H. Baer and
Margaretha Herman Baer. She came to America with her parents in 1893.
The Baer family came to Freeman, S.D., and later moved to Walworth
county and filed claim on the land now owned by John Schnaible Jr.,
south of Java. After their marriage they lived on a farm south of Java
owned by Helen Riecks (relatives of I.C. Pratton). After a year they
moved on the Ed Jerguson farm where they lived a number of years after
which they moved on a farm four miles north of Lowry, S.D., which they
later bought from Jake and Wm. Schott.

        Mr. and Mrs. Henry J. Schreter

To this union 7 children-were born: Henry E. (married to Magdalene
Bieber of Java); Marie (Mrs. Gustave Bieber) of Mobridge, S.D.; Esther
(Mrs. Lester Reed) of San Francisco, Calif., Lillian (Mrs. Gerhardt
Walz) of Bowdle, S.D. Albert (married to Janet Caswell) of Mobridge,
S.D.; Herman (married to Irene Derwald) of Artesia, Calif.; Elsie, who
passed away in 1937 at the age of 15 years from an appendectomy.

Mr. Schreter was a charter member of Zion Lutheran church of Lowry, S.
D., and helped build the church in 1914.

Mr. and Mrs. Schreter moved to Java in 1948 and were members of St.
Paul Lutheran church. Mr. Schreter passed away on Nov. 11, 1950 and
Mrs. Schreter passed away Jan. 4, 1959.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Schuh, Sr. and family

                JOHN AND FREDRICKA SCHUH

John Schuh Sr., was born Sept. 23, 1864 in Hoffnungstal, South Russia,
where he grew to manhood.

On Dec. 10, 1887 he was united in marriage to Fredricka Schlepp, who
was also born in Hoffnungstal on March 31 1868. This couple made their
home in Russia for about two years where the oldest son, John, was born.

On May 16, 1889, the family along with several other families left
Russia and migrated to America to seek new homes. They landed in New
York City and plans had been made before leaving Russia that upon
arriving in America they would continue west to the Dakota Territory.
From New York, they journeyed by rail as far as Bowdle, where the road
ended. In this area relatives and friends also from Russia, had arrived
and settled sometime earlier.

The Schuh family homesteaded five miles northeast of what is now Java,
in Adams District, Walworth county. Here a farm was built and grain and
livestock farming became their livelihood. Among other hardships and
tragedies, these pioneers experienced a severe diphtheria epidemic in
1900 that took the lives of many of their children. The home of the
Schuh family was also saddened by the death of three children within a
few days caused by the epidemic. A tornado swept through the area in
these early years doing considerable damage to some of the farm
homes. Distance from towns and lack of fast transportation caused
hardship during the long cold winters. The pioneer spirit, however,
prevailed and as time moved on, conditions improved.

As more and more families arrived from the old country and settled in
the area, people realized that even though visiting pastors would
occasionally hold church services in the homes, provisions for their
spiritual needs had to be provided for with the community. Some of the
families formed a Lutheran congregation and built a church in 1897
which was called Hoffnungstahl Gemeinde.

Twelve children were born to the Schuh family, six boys and six girls,
namely: John, Java, deceased; three epidemic victims were: Magdalena,
Jacob and David; Elizabeth Wolf, McLaughlin, deceased; Helen Schlaht,
Lodi, Calif.; Fred, Java, deceased; Adolph, Java; Lydia Schlepp, Java;
Christ, Lodi, Calif., deceased; Ida Perman, Java; Bertha Kunz,
Watertown, S.D.

Adding to the homestead, the family remained on the farm until 1922
when the parents retired to a home, built in Java. Here, Mrs. Schuh
passed away in 1928 and Mr. Schuh passed away in 1934.

                JOHN AND EMMA SCHUH

In 1889, John Schuh Jr., eldest son of John and Fredricka Schuh, was
nine months old when his young parents began their journey across the
Atlantic Ocean from their native Russia to their new home in America.

John's parents staked out their claim eight miles northeast of Java in
Walworth county. Here, he and his brothers and sisters attended country
school and grew to young adults. When he was a teenager, one of his
favorite pastimes was to pedal his bike along the bumpy country road to
the town of Java, where he would purchase an ice cream cone. On these
excursions, he was often accompanied by his cousin and neighbor, Sam
Schlepp.

In April 1913, when John was 25 years old, he married Emma Boehler, the
oldest daughter of Gottlieb and Eva Boehler. Shortly afterwards they
moved their home a few miles east of his parents. He had purchased this
small farm, known as the Adam place from his father. He leased the
adjoining land. To supplement his meager farm income, he worked each
fall for a threshing rig.

Emma had worked since she was 12 years old; sometimes as a cook's
helper and also as a housemaid for families in the area. At one time,
she worked for the family of the late E. B. Adams, an early Walworth
county attorney.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Schuh, Jr. and family

The young couple worked hard to improve their home, but were hampered
by drought, crop failures and depression. In 1936, when all the
surrounding land was sold to a neighbor, the Schuhs were forced to give
up their home and move to a rented farm.

In 1950 they moved to their home in Java. Here, Emma died suddenly in
October, 1953 at the age of 57 years. John lived alone until a
paralytic stroke caused him to become bedridden and his last two years
were spent in the Selby Nursing Home, where he passed away in 1965 at
the age of 77 years.

John and Emma Schuh were parents of two sons: Ellsworth and Raymond;
and one daughter Loretta. Ellsworth, and his wife, Freida live on a
farm east of Java which was the George Naasz homestead. Raymond now
lives in California, near the oldest of three children. Loretta, Mrs.
Cornelius Schnaible, lives on a farm northwest of Java. She is the
mother of 3 sons.

        Daniel Seif          Mrs. Daniel Seif

                DANIEL AND KATHARINA SEIF


One of the Sutley Community pioneers, who saw this section grow from a
prairie wilderness to a regime of fine farms and growing towns was
Daniel Seif . He was born on Nov. 11, 1859 at Galion, Ohio, and came to
the Dakota Territory in 1885, with a brother and a cousin to stake some
land. His grandparents, Jacob and Margret (Conewett) Seif came from
Baden, Germany in 1829 and settled in Ohio. Daniel's parents, Michael
and Catherine (Peaster) Seif were farming in Ohio when Daniel, his
brother, Joseph and cousin Levi headed to the Dakota Territory. They
took up land near the Campbell-Walworth county border and settled in the
Sutley area where Daniel built his first sod house. This farm is now
owned and operated by the Herbert Schneider family. Daniel remained
here and farmed and in the winter months taught school.

Daniel married Katharina Bauman, born Aug. 12, 1864 and was the
daughter of Heinrich and Christina (Ernst) Bauman. They were married
Dec. 3, 1889, and lived on Daniel's farm for several years before
moving back to Ohio. While living there, their first two children,
Gertrude and Mary were born. In 1898, the family moved back to Dakota
and homesteaded on a farm 15 miles northeast of Java. Here they lived
and farmed for most of their lives. Three more children were born to
them, Emma, and a set of twins, Fred and Martha.

Farming was done by breaking plow, hand plow and some seeded by hand as
most of the settlers had done until they were able to purchase
horse-drawn machinery. Grain was cut with the scythe and stacked on
piles, corn was planted by hand and weeded by hoes, which was done by
the children. Cookstoves were used for heating, cooking and baking.
Twisted straw, cow chips and dried manure was stored in buildings and
used for fuel in the winter months. Trips were made in the fall to the
Missouri River where wood was gathered and fruit for canning and
jellies. Oil in a dish was their first source of lights and later
kerosene lamps were used. Their early bed was made of wood; ticking
filled with cornhusks or straw which served as a mattress and quilts
and pillows were made of ticking and filled with feathers. Wells were
dug by hand, cisterns were common until windmills came into
existence. Milking was done by hand, which almost included everyone in
the family. The milk was cooled and the cream was skimmed off for
cooking. Later the cream separator took the chore out of cooling and
skimming.

The Daniel Seif children attended school in Thompson District, known as
the Delker school. They also had a little grocery store and post office
near their home for a number of years. During sickness and when home
remedies didn't succeed, Dr. O. H. Gerdes from Eureka, was called.

Five children were born to Daniel and Katharina. They were: Gertrude
(Mrs. Gustav Hirsch) deceased and had lived at Eureka, S.D., Mary (Mrs.
Edward Hirsch) Java, S.D.; Emma (Mrs. Gideon Bieber) Sioux Falls, S.
D., and Fred Seif, who remained single. He lived on the farm until he
retired when he moved to Aberdeen in 1974.

Daniel passed away at his home in June, 1936, and his wife, Katharina,
passed away at the Eureka hospital in June, 1955.

                JOHN AND ROSINA SONNENFELD

John Sonnenfeld arrived in the United States in 1884 and spent some
time in Menno, S.D., where he worked for an uncle until the spring of
1885, when he and his wife, Rosina Fischer Wolf and sons, George and
Henry started for a new homestead by oxen. They built a sod house on
this homestead, but getting no water on that place, he sold it and
moved to Walworth county in 1890 in Adams Township northeast of Java.

The third week in June,--June 19, 1894, they finished building a
two-room house constructed of lumber. Mrs. Sonnenfeld had painted the
front room with lime and stippled it with bluing as was the mode of the
day. One evening the same week at about seven o'clock, Mr. Sonnenfeld
came to the house, warning his wife to take the children and come with
him to the root-cellar, as there was a storm approaching from the
southwest. Never having any experience with a South Dakota twister, she
refused his request and continued with her housework duties. Mr.
Sonnenfeld and Christ Sackman, who made his home with Sonnenfelds,
went outside to check on the storm. Just then the tornado struck and
unable to get to the house, they dropped to the ground and crawled to
safety and fortunately were able to get to the root-cellar. The tornado
was followed with a drenching rain and almost complete darkness. By the
flashes of lightning they saw that the house was gone and began to
search for the family. They found the body of the oldest son, John, 5,
who was killed by the twister, then about the distance of a city block,
they found the rest of the family huddled together in a lake bed. The
younger son, Jake, 3, was unconscious from a head wound. Mrs.
Sonnenfeld had a severely cut leg and head injuries. By all the
misfortune, there still was a protecting hand that shielded George and
Henry, as they were unhurt.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Sonnenfeld, Jacob J. and Emma

Carrying the dead youngster and the unconscious Jake and supporting
Mrs. Sonnenfeld between them, they made their way to the Adam Wagner
homestead, about one-half mile west. The Wagners were living in a
dugout at the time. All their belongings were wet including their
clothing. The night turning cold, and everything wet and nothing to
kindle a fire with, it was very miserable and everybody was waiting for
the dawn of morning to seek relief. When Mr. Sonnenfeld came to Bowdle
the next day, Dr. Edwards was out of town and a young Dr. Murdy
accompanied him back. Dr. Murdy stitched Mrs. Sonnenfeld's leg and did
what he could to help but infection set in. Dr. Edwards made several
trips out and finally had to restitch the leg. When the people of the
area heard of the misfortune of John Sonnenfelds, they gave them
bedding, utensils, furniture, etc., and after building a sod house,
they were able to set up a home again.

In August, 1896, a girl, Emma, (Mrs. John Klein) was born. Mrs.
Sonnenfeld never regained her health and was a semi-invalid the rest of
her live.

In 1918 the couple retired from the farm and moved to Java, where they
resided when Mrs. Sonnenfeld passed away in 1929 and Mr. Sonnenfeld in
1936. Jake lived on the farm until he retired.

Johannas Sonnenfeld, father of John Sonnenfeld, came to America in 1887
and also settled near Menno, S.D., before coming to Walworth county in
1889. With him came two sons: Fred and Henry, and 1 daughter, who
married Karl Kost, Jan. 12, 1888. Johannas Sonnenfeld was in the big
blizzard and froze his feet so badly that the small toes on both feet
had to be amputated.

                JACOB SONNENFELD FAMILY

Jacob Sonnenfeld, the son of John and Rosina (Fischer) Sonnenfeld, was
born April 21, 1891, in Adams Township, Walworth county, South Dakota.
He grew up on the homestead 9 miles northeast of Java, and lived there
until his retirement.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Sonnenfeld

Adams Township was heavily populated in the early years. According to
the tax list, there were 52 farms in 1902-1905. Of these, only 14
remain occupied. Everyone in the area did all their trading in Java, as
there were no cars to travel to any distant places. Horses were the
main means of travel, and most people were happy to have these. In
1912, Jake and his father bought a two-cylinder Buick at an auction
sale, which was chain driven. It had a side crank to start it and a
battery ignition system. They used this car until 1915, when Jake
purchased a Model T Ford, one of the first cars around Java, and was a
lighter car than the Buick.

Jake started to farm with his father, John, in 1914, which was an
eventful year, as he also married Martha Schlaht on June 4, 1914. They
were the parents of five children: Two died in infancy; Emil, Erwin and
Irene. John Sonnenfeld retired and moved into Java in 1918, and Jake
and his family continued to live on the homestead. His wife, Martha,
passed away on December 28, 1932.

Maggie Kranzler and Jake Sonnenfeld were married on September 19, 1933,
and are the parents of one son, Donald, born November 6, 1934.

As was the case with all the pioneers, the Sonnenfeld family remember
good and bad years. In 1934, they too experienced one of the worst
droughts Jake can remember. There was no hay and no pasture. They had
to ship in hay to feed the few head of cattle that they had left. To
help offset the lean years, there are also memories of very good crop
years.

Being civic-minded, Jake served on the Adams Township school board for
many years. In thinking of these days, he remembers that most of the
children walked to school. The teacher received wages of $25.00 per
month and had charge of 25 to 30 pupils. Later the wages were raised to
$28.00 and $35.00 per month. In later years, some good teachers
received as much as $100.00 per month.

Jake and Maggie retired in 1940 and moved to Java, in the home where
his father, John Sonnenfeld resided, and they are still living there.

Emil, who married Erna Oster, continued to live on the homestead after
his parents retired until 1956 when they moved to Pierre. Emil passed
away April 13, 1967. Erna is currently living in Sioux Falls. They are
the parents of three children: Kenneth, who married Kaye McEldowney,
has 1 son, and make their home in Worthington, Minn. - Loretta who
married Clarence Schumacher, has 4 children and live in Sleepy Eye,
Minn.; Annetta, who married Dave Munger, has 1 son and they reside in
Casper, Wyo.

Erwin, who married Alma Oster, lives northeast of Java, and still owns
a fourth section of land that was homesteaded by his great grandfather
in 1890. They are the parents of two children: Janice, married Arlyn F.
Bieber, and they make their home in Kent, Wash.; Quenton, married
Sharon Dalton, they have one son and make their home in Lakewood, Colo.

Irene, who married David Rankin, made her home in Coos Bay, Ore., until
her death, Sept. 19, 1948. They were the parents of two children: One,
who died in infancy, and a son, Allen Rankin, who lives in Corpus
Christi, Texas.

Donald, who married Darlene Voegle, lives in Minneapolis, Minn., and
they have one daughter.

                PHILIP SPIRY SR.

The ancestry of the Spiry family originated in the Province of
Alsace-Lorraine, France. The great-grandfather (of Philip Spiry, Sr.)
was an army officer and was accused of being partially responsible for
Napoleon's defeat. He was charged with treason and convicted to be
executed; but with the assistance of his friends, he escaped prison and
fled to Germany. He was a surveyor by trade and later employed by the
Russian government to assist the crown land which had been granted by
Czar Alexander I of Russia, to his wife, Czarina Catherine II, a former
German princess.

The land was granted for German colonization with the privileges of
religious freedom, tax exemptions for ten years, and exemption from
military service. Each family was allotted a portion of the crown land
in tracts of about 100 acres.

The grandfather, Friederich Spiry was the only son of the family and
was born in 1826 in Kassel, a German colony in the Ukraine of South
Russia; he was united in marriage to Louisa Jesser, a native of
Wuertenberg, Germany. Five children were born to this union: Karl,
Philip, Fred and Karolina, all of whom homesteaded and resided in
Walworth county; and their oldest sister, Barbara, who resided in
Portland, Ore.

Philip Spiry Sr., was born in Kurdumanoff, South Russia, March 20,
1865, and came to America in 1885 with his brother Fred. They arrived
in Scotland, S.D., in a penniless and half-starved condition, having
had their last meal in New York City three days prior to their arrival
in Scotland. A kind-hearted merchant, Henry Sieler, noticed the
half-starved lads in front of his store and finally questioned them.
After hearing of their circumstances, he invited them into a restaurant
and bought them a meal, their first meal in South Dakota. They were
invited into his store; and before the end of the day, both of the boys
landed their first jobs on farms.

They were employed as farm laborers in the vicinity of Menno and
Scotland until they learned that free government land was available to
be homesteaded in Walworth county. In March of 1888, they and two other
families arrived at Ipswich in an emigrant car; at that time, Ipswich
was the end of the railroad.

He brought with him one ox, one horse, a farm wagon and a breaking
plow, while Fred had two ponies. From Ipswich, they proceeded in a
westerly direction and finally arrived at Hiddenwood Creek; they
followed the creek looking for water weeds (which would indicate
shallow water) . They knew that well-drilling equipment was not
available for miles. Then they proceeded to dig a well with a spade and
found a plentiful supply of water at the 6-8 foot depth. Their sod
house was completed in a few days at the cost of $24.

        Philip Spiry, Sr.       Mrs. Philip Spiry, Sr.

Philip married Rosina Oster on Dec. 5, 1889, at Bowdle, and homesteaded
on a farm 3-1/2 miles north of Java. In the year 1896, he was elected as
county assessor and served for two years . In 1900 he entered the farm
implement business in Java in partnership with his brother, Fred. In
1902, he was elected county treasurer and served to the end of 1906.

In the spring of 1903, the Spiry family moved to Bangor, the county seat
of Walworth county. In the spring of 1905, they moved their residence to
Selby to what is now known as the Coleman Wagner home. A short time
later litigations arose and the court ordered the county seat to be
returned to Bangor. A new frame building was erected, and the county
records were returned; again it became necessary for him to make his
daily trips to Bangor until the end of his term.

In the fall of 1906, the Spiry home was sold to E. C. Wilson and in
March of 1907, the family moved to Java. He started the German State
Bank in 1906 and renamed it the American State Bank during World War I.
He was actively engaged in the banking business until poor health
caused his retirement. After the death of John H. Wolff in 1918, he
purchased the Ford Garage, which was known thereafter as Spiry and Sons
Ford Garage. His sons managed the garage for many years.

In 1924, he served as City Mayor. He was treasurer of the Board of
Education, president of the board of trustees and during the 31 years
of residence in Java held many other offices. At one time there was
some action to name the new town "Spiry".

He passed away at home on Tuesday evening, Jan. 28, 1936, after being
confined to bed for several months with a lingering illness. His family
are as follows: His wife, Rosina Oster, and eleven children: Magdalena
Zweigardt Aman (Spokane); Philip H. (deceased); Charles Frederick
(Fred), deceased, John E . deceased ; Bertha Hay (Aberdeen); Samuel
(Java); Rosie Hochstatter, deceased; Dr. Arthur W. (Mobridge); Theodore
(Mobridge); Annette Heezen (Buchanan, Michigan): and Edwin Alvin (rural
Selby).

        Mr. and Mrs. C. F. (Fred) Spiry

                C. F. (FRED) SPIRY

Carl Frederick Spiry, better known to his many Java friends as "Fred",
was born on Dec. 16, 1892, on a farm three miles north of Java and was
the second oldest son of Philip and Rosina (Oster) Spiry. On August 30,
1914, he was married to Rosa (Rosie) Zeller. They had one child,
Harold, who served as superintendent of the Java City schools for many
years and as a business teacher in Aberdeen and Mobridge.

For several years, he was associated with his father and brothers in
the operation of the German State Bank, later known as the American
State Bank. After the death of John H. Wolf in 1918, Philip Spiry Sr.,
purchased the Ford Garage, and Fred took over the managing end of the
garage business. The garage, known as Spiry and Sons Ford Garage, was
under his management from 1918 until 1940. Associated with him in the
operation of the garage were his brothers. After Fred's death, the
garage was operated for approximately 20 years by his wife, Rosie, and
his brother, Sam. In his earlier years he also was an active
participant in the operation of the Spiry Meat Market, which was
operated by Fred and several of his brothers. Active, in some capacity,
in the operation of the bank, garage, and meat market were brothers,
Fred, Sam, Johnny, Philip, Arthur, Ted and Alvin.

He served as a member of the Java City Council for somewhat over ten
years, was a member of the Java Volunteer Fire Department and school
board, and was prominent in Republican circles in Walworth County for
many years.

His wife, Rosie, and her sister, Lena operated the Central Hotel in
Java for several years prior to 1914. Mrs. Spiry was active in
community affairs,--extension clubs, Republican circles, the Spiry
Garage, the church choir, the G & L Grocery and other service
organizations.

Fred passed away on April 4, 1940. Serving with him on the Java City
Council at the time of his death were A. C. Schlepp, Mayor; John Lutz,
Ed Gill, Louis Boehler, Fred Bauer, C. L. Hicks. Fritz Clement. and
John Schlaht.

        Fred Spiry, Sr., family. Standing, William Spiry, Sam (de-
        ceased), Philip (deceased), Emil Spiry, Ed Wolf. Front, Minnie
        (Mrs. Ed Wolf, deceased), Mr. and Mrs. Fred Spiry (deceased),
        Nettie, (Mrs. Leon Kast).

                FRED SPIRY SR.

In the spring of 1885, Fred Spiry Sr., age 17 and his brother Philip
Spiry Sr., age 20 immigrated to the United States from Kurdumanoff,
Russia. They arrived at Scotland, S.D., penniless and hungry. Their
last meal had been 3-1/2 days earlier at New York. A kindly merchant
noticed the two boys, and questioned them. After hearing their story,
he invited them to a restaurant and they had their first meal in South
Dakota. This merchant acquired farm jobs for the boys that same day.

They were employed as farm laborers in the vicinity of Menno and
Scotland, until March, 1888, when they learned that free government
land was available in Walworth county. They left their jobs and arrived
at Ipswich, S.D., because there the railroad ended. Fred had two
ponies and Philip had one ox, one horse, a farm wagon and a breaking
plow. From Ipswich, they traveled westward to Hiddenwood creek and dug
a well and built a sod house, which cost $24.00.

In 1890, Fred married Justina Ernst from Roscoe, who had also been born
in Russia. They built their first home, a sod house 31/2 miles
northeast of Java. They lived in this house until 1900, then built the
house that is still on the farm and is now owned by Otto Mueller. While
living in the sod house they experienced the 1889 blizzard, which
lasted 4 days. Fred and Philip were in partnership in the implement
business in Java, for some time. Later Fred's profession was
auctioneering, which covered a large area. He also served as county
commissioner for a number of years, plus running his farm.

Mrs. Spiry was a busy farm wife and did her best in raising her family.

Seven children were born to them. One died in infancy. The children
were: Emil, now living in Bowdle, S.D.; Minnie (Mrs. Ed Wolf,
deceased), Philip (deceased), Sam (deceased), William, who lives in
Renton, Wash., and Nettie (Mrs. Leon Kast), who lives in Java. They had
12 grandchildren.

In 1921 they decided to retire because of Mrs. Spiry's ill health and
moved to Java. Fred continued his auctioneering for some time.

Mrs. Spiry died Sept. 10, 1924, age 54; and Fred passed away, March 12,
1931.

        Mr. and Mrs. Samuel E. Spiry

                MR. AND MRS. SAMUEL E. SPIRY

Sam E. Spiry was born May 3, 1900 to Mr. and Mrs. Philip Spiry Sr., on
a farm 3-1/2 miles north of Java. At the age of two years, the family
moved to Bangor, where his father was Walworth county treasurer. When
the courthouse was moved to Selby, the family also moved to Selby where
he continued to work at the courthouse. The Spiry family moved to Java
in 1906 where Mr. Spiry went into banking business. The family
consisted of twelve children: Maggie, Bertha, Rose, Annette, Philip,
Fred, John, Samuel, Arthur, Ted and Alvin. One son passed away in
infancy. In 1918 the Spirys purchased the garage from Mrs. John H.
Wolf, which was later known as Spiry and Sons.

Hertha Hauck Spiry was born Nov. 23, 1906 to Mr. and Mrs. Karl Hauck
Sr., on a farm twelve miles northwest of Java in Campbell county. Hertha
and younger brother Henry moved to Java with their parents in 1919. The
family consisted of four boys, Fred, Carl, Ted and Henry and six
girls, Katherine, Bertha and Hertha. Alvine, Lizzie and Alvina passed
away at an early age.

Sam and Hertha were married on Sept. 9, 1924 in Java. Sam and his
brother Fred owned and operated the Spiry and Sons Standard Oil garage
for many years. After Fred passed away, his wife Rosie and Sam
continued to operate the garage until 1961 when it was sold to Francis
Jensen and Sam retired from the garage business. Four children were
born to Sam and Hertha: The first son passed away in infancy, Wallace,
Betty and Tim.

Wallace was born on Dec. 1, 1931. He married Bonita Moser, Oct. 18,
1953. They have three children, Daniel, Vickie and Steven. They live in
Rapid City, S.D., where Wallace is manager of the Metropolitan Life
Insurance office.

Betty was born on Oct. 23, 1935. She married Marvin Pfitzer on Feb. 12,
1956. Four children were born to them: Lori, passed away in 1961 of
cancer, Ivy, Gwen and Kent. They live in Java, where Marvin is employed
as a rural mail carrier.

Tim was born on Nov. 16, 1945. He married Virginia Schmierer on Oct.
2, 1966. Three children were born to them: April Ann, who passed away
in infancy, Michelle and Sherri. They live on a farm near Bowdle.

Sam has been a volunteer Fireman for many years and also in his early
years played the bass horn in the Java-Selby band. Hertha has belonged
to the Java A.L.C.W. for 43 years and the Thimble Sewing Club for 35
years. At the present time, they are living in the home they've
occupied in Java since 1931. Sam and Hertha recently celebrated their
Golden Wedding anniversary.

                THE JACOB STIEGELMEIER FAMILY

The Hartmans, Amans, Mosers and Stiegelmeiers left South Russia in the
early 1880's. Their homes were Bergdorf, Odessa and Gluckstal, on the
Black Sea. They were on the road to Brehmerhafen, Germany about 15
days, then on to America. They arrived at Eureka, S.D., in late
September 1880. They met a man, Mr. Biebelheimer, who located them
about 20 miles southwest of Eureka, which was the Sutley area. Sutley
was also their postoffice. Those must have been the times.

Jacob and Rosina Stiegelmeier lived under a wagon box 3-1/2 ft. wide,
2-1/2 ft. high and 10 ft. long, with my two sisters, Eva and Rosie, the
first winter. Their basic food was flour and salt. For meat it was
prairie chickens and rabbits mostly. My dad walked to Ipswich, pulling
a small sled, to get supplies for the family. The trip usually took 3
days. He would walk behind a sled or wagon coming from Winona which was
about 6 miles west of now Pollock, on the Missouri river.

Every settler had a single-shot 12 gauge shotgun. That gun was a must
when they left the Eureka area because of the Indian scare. Although at
that time the Indian scare was a thing of the past, the settlers were
wary because of the stories they had heard.

I asked my mother on time what impressed her most about America and she
told me that seeing the Statue of Liberty upon arrival in New York was
so awesome that many people, including her, cried like children. One
thing she said was sad: When our group had to part from others in New
York, because so many people did not pass the eye checkup tests and had
to be held back. Fortunately everyone in this group had good eyes and
was passed on through the lines and on they went to South Dakota. The
second year, my dad and neighbors built sod houses and living was
better.

The four worst stories that were told, were the time in 1905 a bull
killed Peter Hartman, grandfather of Pete Hartman now living in Bowdle.
The massacre of the minister, his wife and 4 children at Winona.
Supposedly this crime was committed by Indians. Hal Tracy from Pollock
told me that several old timers on their deathbed admitted that they
were the killers, not the Indians. The tornado in 1902 left the
settlers with bare ground, not even the sod houses withstood that
storm. After that they used rock with the clay sod to withstand the
storms. They held. The fourth and worst event the folks talked about
was where in North Dakota a lawman killed the Wolf family, all but a
small child. He was arrested and convicted of the crime and was sent to
prison for life.

Some of those things were gruesome, but the prairie fires my folks
talked about weren't too pleasant either. !About every 4th year, the
fires would take everything and generally came from the southeast and
burned all the way to the Missouri river. The people had to work hard
plowing fire guards to save grass.

We were a happy bunch. We didn't mix too much with people 8 - 10 miles
away because travel was either on foot or horseback. For pastime in
winter evenings, we would sit around the kitchen table, eat apples and
chew Russian peanuts and, of course, the big boys would tell stories of
how strong and brave they were. I wasn't so big and my job was not to
tell the tall tales,--instead I listened.

The group were Sabbath (Saturday) keepers. The church was about 11
miles northeast of Java near the Jacob Oswald farm

In my early days the 4th of July was the big day of the year, going to
Java, because Java nearly always celebrated with a parade, ball game,
foot races, sack races, 3-legged races, dances, etc. One thing sad was
we had to be home at sundown, to milk cows and feed calves. When the
4th of July was on Saturday we had to stay at home.

I could mention many of the business places this thriving community had
but it would be repetitious because Emma has done that already in her
history. She remembers better than I do.

I feel that I was always happiest in the Java community when I was
young. I traveled extensively but for some reason I was always glad to
be back home. The people have been really good to us, and if I made
some small contribution to the Java people and community around, I
surely got paid four-fold for it, and to all young people coming up in
line to build and prosper I would gladly say: "May the sun shine softly
upon your faces, may the wind be always at your back, and may the rains
fall gently upon your crops." Thank you. --Emma and Ben Stiegelmeier.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Stadel, Sr.

                MR. AND MRS. JACOB STADEL SR.


Jacob Stadel was born in south Russia in 1865. He served in the Russian
Army for several years. He was united in marriage with Magdalena
Laemmle in January, 1889. She was the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Christ
Laemmle Sr. They immigrated to America right after their marriage and
settled on a homestead 14 miles west of Eureka, S.D. They lived there
until 1906 when they moved to a farm 9 miles northwest of Java. where
they lived until 1927 when they moved to Java. Mr. Stadel passed away
in December, 1942 and Mrs. Stadel passed away in September, 1956. They
raised 9 children. Christina Stadel (Naasz) passed away in 1919,
northwest of Java; Pauline Stadel (Lutz) passed away in 1965 in
Cornelius, Ore.; Jacob Stadel Jr., passed away in Java, in 1958;
Magdalena Stadel (Nelson) lives in Woodland, Calif.; Regina Stadel
(Schell) lives in Woodland, Calif.; Matilda Stadel lives in Mobridge,
S.D., Emma Stadel (Elshire) lives in Rapid City, S.D.; Martha Stadel
(Mauch) lives in Selby, S.D.; and Fred Stadel lives in Woodland, Calif.

        Mr. and Mrs. Adolph Stangl

                ADOLPH STANGL

Adolph Stangl was born June 12, 1873 in Gross Eberharts, Austria, to Mr.
and Mrs. Moritz Stangl, where he grew to manhood. In 1898 he came to
the United States on the Kaiser Wilhelm, one of the larger ships on the
ocean at that time. He engaged in farming for his two brothers, Joe and
Anton Stangl for a few years, then he homesteaded and began farming on
his own. On Nov. 7, 1902, he married Magdeline ( Merkel ) Aman at
Bangor.

The couple farmed in the Spring Lake area, east of Java, until they
retired to the town of Java in the fall of 1918, where they built a new
home.

Mrs. Stangl had three children from her first husband John Aman, who
had passed away: Amelia (Mrs. Everett Sebert), Selby, S.D.; Julius
Stangl, who lived at Java; and John Stangl, Morgan Hill, Calif. One
daughter, Mrs. Bertha Stulken of Selby, S.D., was born to Mr. and Mrs.
Adolph Stangl. On Nov. 30, 1933, Mrs. Adolph Stangl died. He lived
alone in his home till Nov. 9, 1935, when he married Louise Sauer. They
continued to make their home in Java until his death, Jan. 7, 1968.
Mrs. Louise Stangl is at the present time still living at their home in
Java at the age of 91 years.

He was also survived by three grandchildren and 9 great grandchildren.

                MR. AND MRS. ANTON STANGL

Anton Stangl was born in Gross Eberharts, Austria, on Dec. 6, 1866, and
immigrated to the United States in the spring of 1884, settling first
near Little Falls, Minn., where he worked as a lumberjack and on an
uncle's farm. In the spring of 1888, he came to Dakota Territory,
joining his brother, Joe, who had come two years earlier, in what is
now Spring Lake Township of Walworth county. These two brothers worked
together for a number of years, tilling the soil and establishing a
farmstead. Anton broke his first sod with a hand plow and a team
composed of a mule and an ox.

        Mr. and Mrs. Anton Stangl

Two outstanding events often recalled by these two brothers from the
first years of their life here, were the blizzard of 1888 and the
Indian scare of 1890. On this latter occasion, they, too, being warned
by the people going by their place on the Bangor road, hitched their
team to a wagon and joined their neighbors in the trek to safety in
Bowdle,--after carefully hiding their ten-gauge shotgun in the weeds so
the Indians wouldn't find it!

1897 was an eventful year for Anton for he became a United States
citizen, and he was married to Wilhelmine (Minnie ) Brandt from north
of Bowdle. She had been born on March 6, 1881 in Wisconsin, and had
lived in Minnesota for a time before coming to South Dakota. Nine
children, six boys and three girls, were born to this couple. Two of
the sons died in infancy; Irving in 1969 and A. Donal in 1974. The two
remaining sons, Albert and Orville, continue to reside on the original
homestead. Still on this farm is the old "claim shanty" in which Joe
and Anton lived, the lumber for which was hauled by an ox team from
Ipswich. It is now used as a storage shed. The three daughters are
still living and are residents of Walworth county. Frances (Mrs.
Raymond Lee) lives on a farm south of Selby, Vina (Mrs. Ralph Sawinsky)
in Selby, and Emma (Mrs. J. Herman Fiedler) in Java.

As was the case with most pioneers, this couple worked hard to
establish a home and increase their farm operation. In 1899 two 40-acre
plots, which adjoined the homestead and tree claim, were purchased from
the government. The two brothers continued to live on the same farm
until 1904, when Joe sold out to Anton and moved to a new place a mile
away.

Deciding to retire in 1926, Anton and Minnie turned the operation of
the farm over to their oldest sons, Donal and Albert, and purchased a
home in Java. Their residence here was short-lived for that same fall
Minnie suddenly became ill and died on October 1. Anton decided to
return to the farm, where he continued to live until his death in June.
1946.

                JULIUS STANGL

Julius Stangl was born Oct. 2, 1897 to Mr. and Mrs. John Aman in a sod
house east of Java. His father died Nov. 21, 1898 when Julius was 1
year and 2 months old. His mother married Adolph Stangl on Nov. 7, 1902
at Bangor, south of Selby.

They moved to the homestead and lived in a two room claim shack. Julius
remembered many things about his growing-up years. Both good and bad,
ups and downs.

In 1918, Julius and John, his brother, took over the farm home of their
parents, Mr. and Mrs. Adolph Stangl. They built a new home and moved to
Java. The boys did some batching for a few years and besides farming
they also did some trapping. They bought their first car with money
from furs and skins. They trapped and had enough left to drive the car
for one year.

        Mr. and Mrs. Julius Stangl

In 1921, there was a double wedding: Julius married Isy and John
married Eunice Whitney, sisters from Mobridge, S.D. They farmed
together until 1923 when Julius bought John's share in livestock and
farm machinery. John and Eunice and their son moved to Aberdeen, Wash.

Julius and Isy farmed the home place for 24 years. They had no
children. In the fall of 1945, they sold their livestock and farm
machinery and bought the Fred Permann home in Java, and spent the
winter months in Oregon, visiting friends and relatives. When they came
back, they moved into their home.

Mrs. Julius Stangl died in June 1949. Julius lived alone for eight
years, took care of his yard and raised a lot of nice flowers, fruits
and vegetables and also worked as hired help on the farms of Reinhold
Moser, Art and Herbert Bieber.

In 1957, he married Hilda Diegel of Roscoe, at the home of his sister,
Mr. and Mrs. Everett Sebert. Julius passed away Sept. 6, 1974, and his
wife resides in Java.

        Mr. and Mrs. Martin Thorson

                MARTIN THORSON

Martin Thorson was born in 1873 and came to America in 1886 with his
parents, George and Emma Thorson and two sisters, Turina and Ida. They
came to Walworth county where his father filed claim on two quarters of
land near the Spring Lake area. They lived there until Martin became of
age, then filed claim on a quarter of land ten miles southeast of Java.

His sisters both passed away while quite young during the flu epidemic.
Martin's father married Minnie Rathke in 1905 and they moved on his
father's farm. He was one of the directors of the Java Equity Exchange
for a number of years. He was also a school board member of the Ryan
school, where four of his children attended school. Five children were
born to the Martin Thorsons, who were: Gladys, Loyd, Melvin, Ida, and
Lorraine.

In 1911, a larger house was built on his land and they farmed for many
years as Martin's greatest interest was grain farming and also raising
cattle and horses. In 1918, they bought a house in Java, which at one
time belonged to Clyde Stewart: In 1919 they returned to the farm, but
only remained there for year as Martin's health prevailed them to
move to California. In 1920 the Thorsons moved to Compton Calif., and
five years later they moved to Riverside, where they bought an orange
grove and started raising oranges. Two children, Dorothy and Clinton
were born to the them. Martin was a director of the Orange Packing House
Association for 25 years. In 1965, Martin passed away at the age of 88
years. Mrs. Thorson still resides at Riverside with her two oldest
sons. The Nies family has been farming the Thorson land for over fifty
years.

        The Karl Wahl family

                KARL WAHL

Karl Wahl was born March 3, 1863 at Hoffnungstal, Bessarabia, Roumania
in South Russia. He was united in marriage to Louisa Wahl at Kloestitz,
a village in Roumania, in 1889. Three months after their marriage the
young couple accompanied other members of her family to America to seek
land and find a new home. They spent eight days on ship to New York,
three days on the train to Eureka, S.D. On arrival there, they secured
food supplies, a wagon, two oxen and a cow and settled on a prairie
homestead. They lived in the open with a wagon box turned over for
protection and storing their food supplies and sleeping at night. They
used the wagon box for work during the day until a sod house could be
built. Three years later they moved on their homestead, which was
located about 6 miles northwest of Java. It was there they built
an adobe brick house, which is still there now.

Their story varies little from that of hundreds of other immigrants
that came to this area with nothing. Also many battled hardships such
as summer and winter storms, Indian scares and other problems.

They were baptized and confirmed in the Lutheran faith and attended
church in the little country church and later St. Paul Lutheran church
in Java.

In 1926, they moved to Java and built a new home of the west part of
Java, which is now owned by John Schneider. Karl Wahl passed away Oct.
14, 1930 at the age of 67 years. Louisa continued to live in Java until
1944 when she moved to Selby and lived for 22 years. She moved to the
Good Samaritan Center in 1966 and remained there until her death, Dec.
30, 1968 at the age of 99 years, 2 months and 19 days.

Twelve children were born to Karl and Louisa Wahl. The oldest son, Fred
lives at the Selby Good Samaritan Center; Edward moved to Aberdeen, S.
D., and passed away in 1968 Emila, Mrs. Alex Adolph, lives in Roundup,
Mont.; George lives in Kent, Wash.; Julius passed away in 1921, while
living in Java; Carl lives in Leola, S.D.; Albert lives in Selby, S.
D.; Adam moved to Woodland, Calif., and passed away in 1964; Rose, Mrs.
Ellis Hoffman lives in Fresno, Calif.; Lydia, Mrs. Bob Hepper, lives in
Santa Barbara, Calif.; Louise, Mrs. John Mutschler, lives in Hot
Springs, S.D.; and Alvina, Mrs. Larry Ebert, lives in Boise, Idaho.

                MR. AND MRS. FREDERICH WALZ

Frederich Walz was born in Krien, South Russia, on Dec. 15, 1865 and
came with his parents, Christian Walz and his wife, Josephine Beiler
Walz, to America in 1873. They settled around Freeman, South Dakota.

In 1885, he married Margaretha Becker, who was born in 1867 in Russia.
She came to this country in 1883 to Freeman, S.D. They made their home
on a farm northeast of Freeman for a number of years. In the spring of
1900, they and their three children, Pauline Walz Baer (deceased),
Christian Walz (deceased), Ferdinand Walz, living in Bowdle, and Miss
Magdalena Becker, a sister of Mrs. Walz, came to Bowdle which was the
end of the railroad at the time, by train with all of their belongings.
They moved to a farm 10 miles southeast of Java, in Walworth county,
which he bought in the fall of 1899. The house is the original home,
which was a landmark for the travelers at the time. There were four
more children born to this family. They are: Mrs. Elsie Gorne, Roscoe,
S.D.: Edward Walz, Eureka Lutheran Home; Fred O. Walz, Bowdle, S.D.;
and Theophil Walz of Minneapolis, Minn.

Frederich Walz was well known as a thresher. He first threshed by horse
power, then by steamer. Later on, he owned one of the first Mogel
Kerosene threshing engines which also pulled an eight bottom plow.
Frederich was a stockholder of the Java Equity.

He died during the flu epidemic of 1918, and his daughter-in-law, Mrs.
Christian Walz, (Pauline Miller) died the following day. Their funeral
was held the same day at Trinity Lutheran cemetery.

        In front, Mr. and Mrs. Frederich Walz and Ferdinand; standing
        behind, Christ and Pauline.

The mother with her five youngest children and her sister, Miss Becker,
continued to farm until they were of age and went on their own. Some of
the farmland is still owned and farmed by grandchildren.

Mrs. Walz continued to live on the home place with her son, Fred until
1952 when her health failed her. She spent some time with her other
children. The last remaining years were spent at the Eureka Lutheran
Rest Home. She passed away in January, 1958 at the age of two days less
than 91 years.

        Mr. and Mrs. Ferdinand Walz and children, Orville, Norwin
        and Leota.

                MR. AND MRS. FERDINAND WALZ

Ferdinand Walz was born in 1897 at Freeman, South Dakota. Emilie Bieber
Walz was born in 1903 at Eureka, S.D. They both grew up in Walworth
county south of Java, S.D. and they were married in November, 1921.
Their first home was northwest of Hoven, S.D. on the Herman Bonen farm
for one year. They then moved to what is now the Herbert Bieber farm
south of Java. Here their three children were born: Leota, Norwin and
Orville.

On Feb. 16, 1926, the Axel Borg farm was bought. Two years later, they
built a home and moved to the new farm. Here is where the three
children grew up. Leota married Robert Elkins and lives at Richardton,
N.D. They have one son. Norwin married Darlene Albrecht, lives at the
homeplace and they have five sons. Orville married Delores Schuh and
lives on Edwin Jurgerson's homestead. They have three daughters.

Ferdinand and his brother, Christ, owned a threshing rig and did a
great deal of threshing every fall. Ferdinand was quite active in
church and community affairs. He was a school board member of the Ryan
school district for many years, and was also a director of the Java
Equity Exchange.

In the fall of 1953, they built a home in Bowdle, where they are
presently residing.

                JOHN WITLOCK SR.

John Witlock Sr., was born in Holland in 1884. He immigrated to the
United States, when he was a young man. He farmed south of Java. In
1922, he married Louise Schuh, who was born in 1899 at Tysplitz, South
Russia and immigrated from Russia with her parents when she was two
years old.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Witlock, Sr.

After their marriage, the young couple moved on a farm south of Java.
Many hardships were endured by the couple and in 1931, they moved to
the former Gershin farm east of Java, where they farmed for several
years. They lived on various farms in the Java area while raising their
four children and having them receive an education.

Mrs. Witlock passed away in 1949 and shortly after her death, John Sr.,
moved in with his brother-in-law, Mr. and Mrs. G. H. Schuh. John was
employed as a buyer for the Mobridge Livestock for many years. This
gave him an opportunity to visit with his friends and also gave him an
opportunity to be in contact with livestock business, which he
thoroughly enjoyed. Schlepp's Lunch at Java, was his favorite stopping
place for coffee and visiting. John spent several years with his
daughters before moving in the Good Samaritan Center at Selby in 1968.
He passed away in 1971.

The Witlock children were: Cornelius (Casey) married Evelyn Kleinemas
and live at Groton, S.D. They have one son who graduated from Groton
high school, and one daughter attending Groton High. John Jr., married
Ann Mueller, residing in Java, and have 6 children: David, married
Vivian Witlock and has one son, Scott and one daughter, Stephanie;
Douglas married Becky Ackerman reside at Selby, and have one daughter,
Stacey; Becky employed at the S.D. Highway Dept. at Pierre, S.D.;
Denese and Debby, twins, are employed at Pierre, S.D.; Jeanne
graduated and is attending Nursing School at Pierre. Helen, (Mrs.
Harold Nies) own and operate a farm northwest of Bowdle; have 3
daughters, Patty, graduated from U.S.D., Vermillion, and is presently
teaching at Denver, Colo., Cindy and Bobby are both attending
college. Ann, (Mrs. Harold Schreter) own and operate a farm 12 miles
south of Java; have 4 children: Suzette attended Dental Technician
School at Watertown, and is presently employed at Denver as a Dental
Technician; Bryce, a senior at Java High; Nancy attending Java Grade
school, and Tim. who will be attending Kindergarten.

                MR. AND MRS. CHRIST A. WOLFF

Christ Wolff, son of Mr. and Mrs. Matt Wolff, was born July 11, 1890, 3
miles northeast of Java, where he grew to manhood.

On Jan. 15, 1915, he was united in marriage to Elizabeth Schuh, who was
born June 19, 1895, 5-1/2 miles northeast of Java to John and
Fredericka Schuh. They farmed about 3 years 9 miles northeast of Java,
and then moved 6 miles south of Java, they farmed until 1921. At
this time, they purchased land 5 miles east of McLaughlin, where they
built their farm home.

Mr. Wolff passed away on Dec. 24, 1953 at which time Mrs. Wolff sold
the farm and built a home in McLaughlin, where he resided until her
death on July 7, 1966. To this marriage 2 daughters were born.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christ A. Wolff

Mrs. Hertha Overbeck and Mrs. Lucille Smith, both living in California;
also 5 grandchildren and 3 great grandchildren.

                GEORGE WOLF SR.

George Wolf Sr., was born in 1833 in Marionberg, South Russia. He grew
to manhood in the southern area and was united in marriage to Katherina
Gugel.

George's activities prevailed his appointment for Judge for the South
Russian Territory. He served as judge for many years.

To this couple were born seven children, who were grown to adulthood
before coming to America. Three sons: George Jr., married Margretha
Schmidt; Matheus G., married Fredricka Jenner; John, married Rosina
Schmidt. Four daughters: Eve, (Mrs. Fred Bickel) Rosina, (Mrs. David
Schlepp Sr.), Elizabeth, (Mrs. Jacob Oschner), Magdelina, (Mrs. Matheis
J. Wolf).

George and Katherina migrated to the United States with other relatives
in 1890 and homesteaded in Walworth county, six miles northeast of Java.

In 1907, they bought a home and moved to Java, which was later owned by
the John Hofer family. Three years later, his wife passed away and
several years later, George Sr., remarried. George passed away in 1926,
at the age of 93 years.

        George Wolf, Sr.

        Mr. and Mrs. Matheis J. Wolf

                MATHEIS J. WOLF

Matheis J. Wolf was born in 1857 in the South Russian Territory. After
he grew to manhood, he was drafted into the Russian Army and served in
the Military Service for six years. In 1883, Matheis was united in
marriage to Magdelina Wolf in a town located in the South Russian area.

Six years later, Matheis, his wife and three children, along with a
number of other friends and relatives, migrated to the United States.
In July, 1889, they landed in New York, and traveled to the Eureka area.

Matheis and his family homesteaded northeast of Eureka, S.D., where
they and a few neighbors erected a sod house and barn. After six years
of misfortunes and drought, the family moved in with his mother, who
was living on a farm northeast of Java. The Matheis Wolf family
remained there a short time before moving on to a farm 6 miles
southeast of Java, where they again built a sod house and barn, trying
to make their livelihood in farming.

During the next ten years, eight more children were born to the Wolf
family, which prevailed the family to lease a larger farm and put all
the children to work. They remained on this farm for three years, then
returned back on the farm 6 miles southeast of Java. At this time, some
of the older children were married and left home.

After most of the children were gone from home, Matheis and Magdelina
bought a home in Java, now the John Wolff residence. In 1925, they
moved to Java, where they led a retired life until their death. Matheis
passed away on Sept. 4, 1944 and his wife, Magdelina passed away on
Feb. 17, 1948.

                WILLIAM WOLF

William Wolf was born on March 8, 1900 on a farm 6 miles east of Java,
where his parents, Matheis and Magdelina and family lived. William
attended Alamo country school when he became of age and after he
finished school, he helped his father with the farming.

On August 27, 1923, William was united in marriage to Katherina Mayer
in the Lutheran church of Java, where they still are devout church
members. This young couple took over his father's farm and farmed for
many years. Two children were born to William and Katherina. One
daughter, Francis married Leonard Joachim, and they have one daughter,
Judy, also married. They reside in Bowdle. One son, Lloyd died when
three years old.

        Mr. and Mrs. William Wolf

In 1935, depression and drought forced the family to sell the farm and
move to Java where he was employed as a farmhand. In 1949, the Wolf
family moved to Bowdle, where he owned and operated the Wolf Dray Line
for eight years. Later, selling his Dray Line, William was appointed as
Chief of Police in Bowdle, and held that position until 1965, when he
retired.

As of now, William and his wife live a retired life and reside in
Bowdle, S.D. However, he occasionally helps with the farming in the
Java area. William (Bill) still considers Java his home town, being
that he was born the year that Java originated.

        John H. Wolf        Mrs. John H. Wolf

                JOHN H. WOLF

John H. Wolf came to America with his parents at the age of 9 from the
Odessa area in South Russia. A very large group came at that time. They
settled northeast of Java near the Matt G. Wolf place. My father owned
the farm that was later known as the Arend Lakeman farm, just 1-1/4
miles east of Java. I was about four years old when my parents moved
into Java.

My mother (Rosina Schmidt) was a baby when she came to America with her
parents in 1883. They settled in the Artas, S.D., area. Father and
mother were married at Java in 1902.

I was born in 1904 on the farm and so was my sister, Rose (Mrs. Albert
Bickel) 2 years later. My father opened a tire shop north of Bauer's
store in Java. Later, he moved across the street on the corner, where
he opened a garage and a bowling alley. He had the Ford agency until
the time of his death, Oct. 21, 1918. Ed Hughes was my father's
bookkeeper. The garage was sold to Philip Spiry and sons of Java. My
brother, Walter was born in Java in March, 1913.

Java was a prosperous town in the early 1900's. My father was the first
Ford dealer in the area west of Aberdeen. He was a great booster for
our community. Java then had three garages, also two banks, four
grocery stores, two lumberyards, one creamery and three or four cream
buying stations, one newspaper, one implement shop, one doctor one
dentist, one drug store, one theatre, one bowling alley one blacksmith
shop, one livery stable and one hotel.

My mother operated a hotel in the building on the corner just north of
the present postoffice. This hotel was noted for its wonderful meals.
My mother died in July, 1934.

The first school in Java was located on the block where the Marvin
Pfitzer, Russell Bender and Henry Bender homes are now. The new school
was built in 1921 south of Main street and across the highway. From
that time on, we have had a 4-year accredited high school. I graduated
from high school in 1924 and was married to Ben Stiegelmeier in June,
1926. We have two daughters, Joy Lynne (Mrs. John B. Tooley) of
Columbus, Neb., and Beverly Jeanne (Mrs. Wayne S. Schnaible) of Java;
and 5 grandchildren. Joy and John have three sons, Pat, Steve and Chris
Tooley and Beverly and Wayne have a son and daughter, Perry and Kelly
Schnaible. I have lived in Java all my life and it's a great community
with many friendly people. -- Mrs. Ben (Emma Wolf ) Stiegelmeier.

        The Fredrick Wolf family in 1918

                FREDRICK WOLF

Fredrick Wolf, eldest son of Fredrick Wolf and Elizabeth Myler, was
born at Marienberg, South Russia, Dec. 15, 1868. He lost his mother in
boyhood. His father remarried Eva Kurle. In 1890, Fredrick, his father,
mother and family, immigrated to the United States, where they settled
northwest of Java.

In 1896, Fredrick homesteaded a quarter of land northwest of Java,
where he lived until his death.

In 1897, Fredrick was married to Justina, daughter of John and Rosina
(Rollop) Henke, of Fredonia, N.D. She was born July 19,1877 in the
Kulm area of South Russia. She was 4 years old when her parents
migrated to America.

This family was the parents of 6 daughters. All born at home. They
attended nearby country schools and the Peace Lutheran church north of
Java . The girls were: Karolina, who died in infancy; Rosina, Mrs. Ed
Schnaible of Java; Natalie, Mrs. Philip (Stadel) Spiry of Java;
Justina, Mrs. Ed Schlepp, deceased; Elizabeth, Mrs. John Schlepp,
deceased; and Amelia, Mrs. Christ Schuh of Lodi, Calif.

For many years the family lived in sod houses. The first one was very
small with only 2 rooms. Later with the help of the oldest daughters, a
larger one was built. In 1917 they built a 9 room frame house, where
they lived until Mr. Wolf's death in 1919. Mrs. Wolf moved to Java,
where she built a new home. She lived here until her death in 1956. She
had married John Goetz in 1939. He preceded her in death.

Mr. and Mrs. Wolf have 23 grandchildren. Their original homestead land
is now farmed by a grandson, Cornelius Schnaible.

                GEORGE H. WOLF

George H. Wolf was born on Oct. 1881, and was the oldest son of
Fredrich and Eva (Kurle) Wolf. He had 8 brothers and sisters and 5 half
brothers and sisters.

His grandparents moved from southern Germany to a Colony of Bergdorf,
which was located a few miles from the seaport of Odessa on the Black
Sea.

George H. Wolf came to the United States with his parents in 1890 by
2nd class ship. From New York, they came to Eureka, S.D., where his
mother's sister (Mrs. Adam Merkel) lived. A short time later, the
Fredrich Wolf family filed a homestead claim and built a sod house.
Raising grain and livestock and milking was their livelihood. In later
years the boys would cut ice by hand in a creek nearby and hauled it by
horse to Java, where it was sold.

George's father, Fredrich had been a teacher in the old country, and
served as Walworth county commissioner for a few years until 1900.

George and all his brothers built their homes in Walworth county. In
1904 George married Katherine Himrich. This young couple settled on
some land 3-1/2 miles west of Java, where George built a home and farmed
for many years. To George and Katherine were born 3 sons and 3
daughters. The Wolfs remained on the farm until the fall of 1917, when
they bought a home and moved to Java, (now the Neumiller residence),
where they remained until their death. George passed away on March 3,
1940 and Katherine, his wife, passed away on March 31, 1969.

The Wolf children were: Dorothea, born Jan. 7, 1905, unmarried, taught
school for a number of years; passed away, April 28, 1931. George J.,
born Nov. 29, 1912; unmarried; died May 18, 1938. Katherine, Mrs.
Albert Lenz, born March, 1906; husband deceased and Katherina resides
at Gettysburg, S.D. Susanna, Mrs. Alvin Spiry, born Nov. 28, 1915,
reside on a farm 3 miles east of Selby, have 3 sons: Vaughn, married
Lou Pies and have one daughter, Twyla, and reside at Selby, S.D.
Fredrick (deceased) married Marlene Schutts and had 2 children, Pamela
and Sandra, who reside at California. Roger, married Shirley Mueller, is
a teacher at Roscoe, where they reside; have 3 children. Emil
(deceased) married Alice McQuire, have one son, Bruce and 2 daughters,
Sharon and Rebecca. Herman married Dorthea Jenson, have one daughter,
Dorothy and 2 sons, Michael and Mark.

        Mr. and Mrs. George H. Wolf

        The Jacob F. Wolf family

                JACOB F. WOLF

Jacob F. Wolf was born Sept. 4, 1882, in Odessa, South Russia. He came
to the United States with his parents in 1890, and the family
homesteaded near Java, where he attended school and grew up to young
manhood.

Lydia Bauder was born April 17, 1889, near Scotland, S.D. In 1900, the
family moved to Walworth county and settled on a farm southwest of
Java, where she attended school and grew to young womanhood. On Oct.
18, 1908, they were married in St. Paul Lutheran church at Java, and
they farmed 2-1/2 miles west of Java. Four children were born to the
union: Clara, Helen, Lydia and Elmer.

In 1920, they purchased a new home in Java, to make it easier for the
children to attend school, but retained their farming interests until
the time of their deaths. Mr. Wolf Dec. 19, 1957, and Mrs. Wolf, Jan. 6,
1961.

The family began to be active members of the community and church. Mr.
Wolf served on the school board and elevator board and the family
acquired many good neighbors and friends.

Clara graduated from high school in 1931. In 1932 she married Nick
Noble. They farmed near Java until 1941 and then moved to Lakeland,
Florida, for two years and then to Camas, Wash., where they permanently
settled. Mr. Noble died in 1969 and Clara is residing at her home at
144 N.W. 18th.

Helen graduated from high school in 1932. She trained in secretarial
work and was employed at the courthouse in Selby. In 1935, she married
Henry Perman. They made their home in Java, where he operated an
implement business. Later they moved to Selby, where her husband was
elected County Auditor. He passed away in 1959. Helen was then elected
to that position and served in that office for almost 8 terms, when she
died July 5, 1973. Three children were born to that union.

Lydia graduated from high school in 1934. She attended a hairdressing
school and in 1935 opened a beauty shop in Java, and operated there for
the next five years. She married Cliff Shaver of St. Thomas, N.D. They
moved there to engage in farming operations and where they are still
residing. Six children were born to that union.

Elmer attended school in Java and grew up to young manhood. He farmed
the homeplace and the last two years of his life operated the
Recreational Parlor in Java. He died in November, 1968.

Ours has been a good life living and growing up in the community of
Java, and we are thankful for the blessings God has given us.

        Mr. and Mrs. Henry H. Wolff

                MR. AND MRS. HENRY H. WOLFF

Henry H. Wolff was born at Java, March 5, 1902, son of Matheus G. and
Fredericka (Jenner) Wolff. On Nov. 15 1925, he was united in marriage
to Martha Bender, who was born Sept. 1, 1905 at Sutley area north of
Java, a daughter of Jacob and Elizabeth ( Hauck ) Bender. They lived a
few months at the Matheus Wolff farm and in March, 1926, they moved on
a farm 3-1/2 miles northwest of Java. In about 1938, they moved to Java
and are still residing there. He is employed at Rabenberg Inc., at
Selby. To this union 3 children were born: Priscilla, married to Jim
Halloran living in Minneapolis, Calvin H., married to Donna L. Schreter
living on the home place they purchased; JoAnn, married to Virgil
Eisenbeisz, and living in Aberdeen.

                JOHANNES WOLFF

Johannes Wolff was born Oct. 28, 1874 in Marienberg, South Russia. He
was the son of Jacob and Katherina (Meile) Wolff. A daughter was also
born to this union and when at the age of two years, their mother
passed away, their father remarried and in the spring of 1889, the
family came to the United States and settled in Walworth county. They
homesteaded 10 miles northeast of Java. John was 15 years old, which
made him the sole farm hand, helping his father. John had one sister
and 8 half brothers and sisters.

        Mr. and Mrs. Johannes Wolff and Family

On Oct. 14, 1894, Johannes married Maria Banek, who was the daughter of
Christoph and Katherina ( Meier) Banek, who lived 5 miles north of the
Wolff farm. The Banek family came to America from Freidenstahl, South
Russia. They had a family of six sons and one daughter.

Johannes and Maria settled on a farm 4 miles east of his parents, where
they raised a family of 9 children. The Johannes Wolff family were
devout church members of the Baptist church, which was near their home.
The congregation was served by the Pastor from Eureka.

The Wolff's two oldest daughters passed away in the spring of 1918, one
in March and one in April. The oldest of the two, was married at the
time of her death and also left a small son. The other children grew up
on the farm and later married, establishing homes near their parents'
farm.

In 1934, Mr. Wolff became ill and went to Wichita, Kansas, seeking
medical aid. On May 4, 1934, Mr. Wolff passed away at the age of 60
years. This left his wife, Maria and 2 children on the farm. In 1936,
Maria sold her farm and with her two children moved to Java, where she
purchased a home. In October, 1958, Mrs. Wolff passed away at the age
of 81 years. There are 6 of the children living, three reside at
Eureka, S.D.; one at West Fargo, N.D.; one at Michigan, and one at Java.

The children of the Wolff family are: Gust married to Martha Gross,
reside at Eureka, had 6 children, one passed away at age 10 years.
Lydia (Mrs. Fred Gab) resides at Eureka, and had 6 children, one child
died in infancy. Rosina, (Mrs. John Jung) had no children, but fostered
two that were John's brother's children. Rosina's husband passed away in
1968, and she now resides at Eureka. Reinholdt, married Lea Zimmerman
and live at West Fargo, N.D. They have 5 children. Arthur, unmarried,
passed away in 1961. Maria, (Mrs. Alvin Mittleider) live at Muskegan,
Mich. They had 3 children, two died in infancy. Hulda, (Mrs. Gehbardt
Pfitzer) live at Java, where her husband is employed as custodian of
the Java school. They have one daughter, Lynda, who is an R.N. in a
hospital at St. Paul, Minn. Katherine and Amelia are the 2 daughters
who had passed away. Amelia, married Christoph Goetz and passed away
when her son, Robert was born.

        Mr. and Mrs. Matheus G. Wolff

                MR. AND MRS. MATHEUS G. WOLFF

Matheus G. Wolff was born in Marienberg, South Russia, Sept. 21, 1866,
son of George and Kathryn (Gugel) Wolff. On Feb. 14, 1888, he was
united in marriage with Fredericka Jenner, who was born Oct. 5, 1865,
the daughter of John and Rosina (Hoffman) Jenner. He came to America on
April 24, 1890, with his wife and daughter and homesteaded 3 miles
northeast of Java. He was one of the earliest settlers of Walworth
county. He lived on this farm 68 years until the time of his death July
31, 1958. Mrs. Wolff passed away in 1932. The farm has been in his
family until this past summer it was purchased by a grandson, Calvin H.
Wolff, son of Henry H. Wolff.

To this union 10 children were born. The living are Mrs. Frieda Sauer,
John C., Henry H. (married to Martha Bender), Emily (married to John
M. Haux) all of Java. The deceased are Christ A. (was married to
Elizabeth Schuh and passed away in 1953.) Lydia (was married to Henry C.
Fiedler, passed away in 1955.) Mary (was married to Carl Hepper, passed
away April 29, 1971.) Jake, who passed away Feb. 21, 1973. Rose, who
passed away, April 26, 1973. Kathryn, passed away in infancy.

                FREDRICH W. WOSNUK

Fredrich W. Wosnuk was born in Russia, where he grew to manhood and was
married. The young couple, with their three children came to the United
States, and settled in the Dakota Territory. They homesteaded on a farm
northwest of Java near the Schneider and Kundert farms. Three children
were born to this couple when Fredrich's wife passed away. They were:
Barbara married to a Fiechtner - Theodore, who lived in Aberdeen, S.D.,
all his adult life and Simon, who lived at Herreid, and then later
moved to Plevna, Mont.

Martha (Fredrich's second wife) had one child, born in Russia by a
former marriage. Her name was Annice, who married Philip H. Merkel. He
was one of the first blacksmiths in Java. They lived in a home in front
of the Blacksmith Shop (across the street from the old Naasz residence).
This couple had seven children. Alma, was one of the daughters, who
lived in Java, and attended school until 1926 when she graduated. Alma,
married Howard Johnson in 1928, and has one son, Jack Arthur, who is an
engineer with the Schlumberger Oil Co., and resides at Wharton, Texas.
Jack and his wife, June had 3 children: Margret, age 18; Howard, age
17; and Karen age 14. After Howard's death in 1964, Alma remarried in
1974 to Gerald R. Corson, youngest son of James and Kezia Corson, who
were among Java's earliest settlers.

Fredrich and Martha had 8 children (one died in infancy) . They were:
Alec, married, had 3 daughters - he was a successful man, at one time
was a State Bank Examiner. Susan (Mrs. Green) had 3 children by a
previous marriage; Earl, Geneva and Francis. Susan presently resides at
Eugene Ore. Pierre, married, and has 3 children. He owned and operated
the Lowry Bank of Lowry, S.D. At the present time, he resides in the
state of Washington. Henry, married, recently celebrated their Golden
Wedding Anniversary. They have 5 children and reside in the state of
Michigan. John, married. Lost his wife 15 years ago, now resides at San
Diego, Calif. Emma, a registered nurse lives at Eugene, Ore. She was an
Army nurse and was stationed in Hawaii at the time of the bombardment.
Cathryn married Dr. Philip Noble, son of Philip and Grace Noble, had
one child and reside at Anacortes, Wash.

                JOE ZARKA

Joe Zarka was born Feb. 12, 1890 at Oberward Burgaland, Austria. He
lived with his parents until the age of 12, when he set out to earn his
own living, herding cattle, but he wanted to take up a trade, so he
decided to go to school and take up the trade of a shoemaker.

        Mr. and Mrs. Joe Zarka

Joe later was enticed by the announcements of the fruitful land being
offered in America, so at the age of 16, Joe undertook the long trip to
the United States by himself . He came to the Java area and stayed with
his Uncle Ladis Bertha, who lived 10 miles south of Java. He worked for
various farmers until 1910 when he decided to go to Buffalo, Harding
county, where he filed claim and lived there until 1915 when he
returned to the Java area and stayed with the Barclay family, now the
Guy Schuh farm. Again Joe worked as a farmhand until March 15,1918,
when he married Helen Ryan who was born at Bangor, S.D., in 1890.

A month later Joe was drafted into the Service of World War I and was
sent to Camp Sherman, Ohio. He was sent on to New York, before going
overseas, where he served in the Argonne-Meuse sector in France. Was in
battle 32 days when he was wounded and spent 2 weeks in the hospital in
Boataz, France, before he was sent back to the States, where he
remained in the hospital, until he was transferred to Camp Dodge and
then received his discharge and returned back home to Java. He again
rented land and farmed for many years and later bought his own farm in
1940 and farmed until 1947 when he bought a home in Java, and lived a
semi-retired life. While living in Java, Joe was very active in Legion
and city affairs. He was American Legion Commander for 18 years; also
served as District and County Commander and later State Vice Commander-
was on the draft board for 22 years, and on the city board and also
served as City Mayor for 6 years. Mrs. Zarka was one of the charter
members of the Womens American Legion Auxiliary and was president for 5
years. They both were active members of the Presbyterian church. In
1972, Mrs. Zarka passed away and Joe still resides as one of our
pioneers in Java.

                GEORGE AND ROSINA ZELLER

George Zeller was born June 3, 1867 in Bessarabia, South Russia. He
came to this country with his parents and brothers and sister in 1884.
They spent their first winter at the home of an uncle, Jacob Zeller, at
Menno, South Dakota. George and a brother and sister "worked out" that
winter for their board and room.

In the spring of 1885, the family settled in Campbell county, where
they homesteaded on claims. Their first home in their new country was
typical of the time,--a rough hut.

George said his mother cried when she saw her new home in a land where
there were no neighbors, no churches, and no schools. However, during
the first year on the homestead, they built a better home. Also, they
soon had new neighbors.

George told of how oxen were used in threshing their first flax crops.
The flax was piled about three feet high and the oxen were led in a
circle on the pile; tramping the flax until the seed was separated from
the straw.

        Mr. and Mrs. George Zeller

Since there were no churches or ministers in the newly settled
territory, these early pioneers conducted family worship with the
father reading from his prayer book. Later, ministers traveled from
house to house. By 1888, the Hoffnungstahl church had been
established eight miles west of Eureka, and only a few miles from the
Zeller home.

On July 10,1890, George Zeller married Rosina Grenz, the daughter of
pioneer neighbor. Shortly afterward, the young couple established their
home twelve miles northeast of Java. Their first home was a sod house,
with blacksmith shop and barn attached to it. The sod house was warm
and was an improvement over some for it had a wood floor. The first
year they farmed with their team being one horse and one cow. Although
the young couple lived not more than ten miles distance from their
parents' homes, the trip by horse and buggy was not often made,--so the
loneliness and the privations of the new home must have often reminded
Mrs. Zeller of the hardships of her first years in America .

The Samuel Grenz family had come to America in 1885. Rosina told of the
shelter that served while they were building their first house. A hole
in the ground with an inverted wagon box over it protected them from
the elements and animals. The teen-aged girl remembered the comforts
they had left behind to make the trip to America and the first
difficult years remained in her memory.

In 1899 a spacious frame house replaced the sod house. As other farm
buildings were built, the sod barn and blacksmith shop were torn down,
but the sod house remained in use as a "summer kitchen" for many years.

The George Zeller family moved to Java in 1908. They owned the home
later known as the A. C. Schlepp home.

George Zeller bought a machine and implement shop from the Huether
Brothers. This became a family enterprise for his son, Gottlieb, was
bookkeeper and helped set up machinery, and his daughter, Rosie, served
as clerk. In January of 1915, Mr. Zeller bought the Java Branch of the
Selby Implement Company. Buggies and wagons were much in demand, but
they also sold threshing machines and tractors. The Zellers handled
several lines of machinery including McCormick, John Deere, Deering,
Titan, Hart Parr, and Minneapolis. Several of the company names have
long since been forgotten on the farm scene,--such as Titan and Hart
Parr. As a forerunner to present day diversification, Mr. Zeller sold
Eureka flour, getting the flour in 50 and 100 pound sacks by the train
car load. In 1916 he sold the implement business to the Java Equity
Exchange.

In January of 1912, Mr. Zeller bought the Central Hotel from John J.
Wolff. His daughters, Lena and Rosie, operated the hotel for three
years, until Mr. Zeller rented it to the Campbells. He sold the Central
Hotel in November of 1916.

George Zeller owned one of the first automobiles in Java.

In 1917 the family moved back to the farm. During this period, in 1918
and 1919, George Zeller was state representative from Campbell county.

Often during the years while Mrs. Zeller was busy raising their twelve
children, she found time to help neighbors, especially in times of
sickness. During the flu epidemic, she was a constant friend, helper
and comfort to those who needed her. In the days when people found it
difficult to get to doctors, she had a healing hand. Her own family was
seldom ill.

Mr. and Mrs. Zeller and their three youngest daughters moved to Eureka
in 1925. At this time, there was talk of building a new Lutheran church
in Eureka. Mr. Zeller helped make this project become a reality. He was
also among those who saw the need for a good hospital and helped in the
organization of the Eureka hospital.

Throughout his lifetime, George Zeller was active in civic and
community affairs. He served as county commissioner in Campbell county,
served on school boards, and was deacon in church for many years. He
also helped establish several Lutheran churches.

After George Zeller's death in 1942, Rosina Zeller moved to Java to be
near the children residing there. Again she became interested in the
affairs of the community, whether it was a school function or a
quilting party, a hobby she pursued many years.

Their children: Magdalena (Lena) Wolff and Agnes Linder live in
