Cardston, Alberta, Canada; Martha Marquardt and Kathryn Hamann live in
Sacramento, California; George L. Zeller (deceased) lived in California
many years; Bertha Bieber makes her home in Trail City and Java; Rosina
(Rosie) Spiry, Christine Schlepp, and John Zeller live in Java, as did
Gottlieb Zeller before his death in 1965 and Elizabeth Schnaible,
before her death in 1974. Emma Keim died in 1932.

In November of 1952, Rosina Zeller died. For eighty-three years she had
served her family and her community well. The memory of this active
pioneer couple lives on in the minds of all who knew them.

                MR. AND MRS. GOTTLIEB ZELLER

Gottlieb A. Zeller, the oldest of the children of Rosina and George
Zeller, was born Oct. 24, 1890 on a farm near Eureka. When Gottlieb was
two or three years old, the family established their home on a farm 12
miles north of Java, where they lived until Gottlieb was 18 years old.
At that time, they moved to Java, and Gottlieb became bookkeeper at the
Zeller Bros. Implement. During the next several years, he also helped
other ways in the newly formed business especially helping to assemble
the machinery.

Elizabeth Bender (Lizzie) was born May 9, 1893, near Sutley in Campbell
county. She was a daughter of Jacob and Elizabeth (nee Hauck) Bender.
She attended rural school and grew into womanhood on her parents' farm
northeast of Java.

On Nov. 14, 1912, Gottlieb Zeller and Elizabeth Bender were married in
the Reformed church at Sutley. They lived at the Zeller home in Java,
the first winter. The following spring, they moved to the Zeller farm.
That fall, they returned to Java and lived in the present Jacob Himrich
house. In 1916, they again took up farming on the Zeller farm.

        Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Zeller

In 1916, tragedy struck when their second daughter, Lydia, only a year
and a half old, strangled on a sunflower seed. Shortly after they moved
to a farm near Leith, N.D. Crop conditions there were unfavorable for
the next several years and they returned to Campbell county in August
of 1921. During the move from Leith back to the farm near Java, a wagon
load of household goods caught fire and only a few pieces were
salvaged. Gottlieb and Lizzie and their little girls made their home on
the Jacob Bender farm until 1925, when they bought a farm that became
their home for the next 22 years. In 1929, they built a large modern
home.

In addition to his farming activities while living in Campbell county,
Gottlieb was active in school, church and civic affairs. He served as
county commissioner and was an officer in the Ackerman school district
for several years. Also he was a board member of the Java Equity
Exchange in the 1930's and until 1941.

Gottlieb and Lizzie retired from farming in 1947 and moved to Java.
Here, he continued his church activities, being an active Lutheran
throughout his lifetime. Lizzie was a long-time member of ALCW and also
a member of the Java Garden Club and Sewing club. She passed away very
suddenly in January of 1959.

Gottlieb married Mrs. Carrie Geigle in 1960. He was interested in
refinishing furniture and carpentry as a hobby until his sudden illness
that ended in his death in August of 1965.

The living children of Gottlieb and Lizzie Zeller are Agnes, Lillian,
Erwin and Betty (Elizabeth).

Agnes, born June 30, 1914 at Java, S.D. married Sam L. Goehring. They
reside at Lockeford, Calif., where their two sons, Lyle and Ronnie were
born. Lyle married Deanna Black of Stockton, Calif., and have 2
daughters, Debra and Shelia. Ronnie is married to the former Frances
Paoletti of Lockeford, Calif.

Lillian, born April 11, 1919, at Leith, N.D., is married to Chris J.
Pfitzer, living at Java. They have three children: Dennis, Neal and
Karen. Dennis is married to Cheryl Shroll of Rapid City, S.D. They
have a family of three boys: Chris, Jason and Joel, living at Rapid
City. Neal is married to Marlys Schlepp of Java, and reside at
Lakewood, Colo. They have one son, Nathan. Karen and her husband, Bob
Machan of Gettysburg, S.D., live at Omaha, Neb. They have one son, Jon
and one daughter, Teri Lynn.

Erwin was born July 5, 1925 north of Java. He and his wife the former
Dalles Kurle of Java, live at Aberdeen, S.D. They have two daughters,
Sandy and Cindy. Sandy is married to David Hasselburg of Staples,
Minn., and reside at Minneapolis, Minn. Cindy Zeller, attending
college, resides in Aberdeen.

Betty was born Dec. 17, 1933, north of Java. She is married to LeRoy
Biel of Java, and reside at Scranton, N.D. They have two sons, Mike and
Pat. Mike, living at Scranton, is married to Tamara Ness of that city,
and have one daughter. Pat is employed at Bowman, N.D., and is living
with his parents.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Zeller, Nancy, Sydney Ann, and Darrell.

                JOHN ZELLER

John Zeller, the son of George and Rosina Zeller, was born Feb. 16,
1904 at their farm home 12 miles northeast of Java. In 1908 the family
moved to Java, where they operated an implement business. Many of
John's early memories are centered around the implement shop. He
assisted with the setting up of machinery by the time he was 10 years
old.

In 1917 the family returned to the farm. Here, John helped with all
phases of the farm work. They farmed entirely with horses. In those
days, threshing often lasted until late fall and it along with corn
picking and the preparation of the land, kept farmers busy until long
after the fall freeze-up.

Like most Dakota farmers prior to the 1920's, the Zellers hauled grain
to town by wagon. The hauling of grain was usually postponed until all
the fall work was done and these slow trips were often made on brisk,
cold days. A wagon box was considered to hold approximately 50 bushels
of wheat. Many farmers made the trip with two or three wagon loads at
once. They often met up with others hauling grain, so it became a
friendly convoy along the way. After snow was on the ground, sleighs
were used and the trip was much smoother. From the time they moved back
to the farm, John took part in the grain hauling. In 1925, his parents
and three youngest sisters moved to Eureka. John continued to farm.

He married Hazel Walter of Selby on Dec. 24, 1929. She continued
teaching through the spring of 1932, when rural teacher's salaries
dropped below $85.00 per month.

John operated a diversified farm, but his mainstay was hogs and cattle.
He had a small but fine herd of registered Hereford cattle which he
started to build in the depression years.

Mostly because they wanted to send their children to school in town,
the Zellers moved to Java in October of 1947. REA became available to
their farm area within a year or two after their move. Later, school
consolidation meant the Java school bus served their farm community.
Also, the mail route, which farmers of their area had sought to bring
closer to their homes were changed to pass the Zeller farm house, in
contrast to the 2-1/2 miles they had to go to their mail box.

John continued grain farming for a time. He also became interested in
carpentry, which trade he followed for more than 25 years. He served on
the school board while in Campbell county and again after moving to
Java. Recently, John completed two terms as mayor of Java. Hazel helped
to organize the Java Garden Club in 1953. Later, the Altar Guild of St.
Paul Lutheran church became a reality because of her efforts to
establish a rotating flower committee for the church. She went back to
teaching a rural school for three terms. She also taught Sunday school
more than 12 years. Her interest in writing and gardening continued and
in 1958, she began her work on gladiolus. Through her hybridization
program, she has developed and registered numerous gladiolus, her main
work being the development of fragrant glads. In 1967, she organized
Wonderful World of Glads, which is sponsored by the North American
Gladiolus Council. Hazel is a nationally accredited flower show judge
and is flower show and flower show schools chairman for the S.D.
Federated Garden Clubs.

John and Hazel had three children: Nancy, Mrs. Harry Remien of Tacoma,
Wash.; Sydney Ann, Mrs. Kenneth Seyer of Selby; and Darrell, who
married Mary Ann Chapman. He is a lieutenant in the U. S. Air Force,
and he and his family are presently stationed at the Richard Gebour Air
Force Base in Missouri.

                ADAM LENO FAMILY

Adam Leno was born on December 26, 1861, in Helental, South Russia. He
was the son of Heinrich Peter Leno, who was born in the same village.
Heinrich was the son of Peter Leno and the grandson of Johann Leno,
both of whom had been born in Prussia and migrated to Peterstal near
Odessa in 1815

Adam and Christina (nee Hoehn) Leno were married in Helental on
December 20, 1883. In August, 1884, they came to America, landing at
Scotland, Dakota Territory. After staying with friends there for a
short while, they took a train to Aberdeen and from there to the small
town of Ipswich where the railroad ended. With the help of Adam's
cousin, Gottlieb Sieler, they bought a wagon, a breaking plow, some
tools, a pair of oxen, a cow, and a few chickens. They then set out for
Sutley in Campbell county, where they filed on a homestead.

Their first house, including the roof was built of sod. The floor was
made of tamped clay. In the fall and spring, barrels of water and sacks
were always kept handy to combat prairie fires. The Dakota winters were
formidable natural enemies of the pioneers. One of the worst on record
was the winter of 1888. A severe blizzard came early in the fall.
Tunnels had to be dug from the house to the barn and the well to
provide food and water. Adam and seven of his neighbors walked to Ip-
swich, about 65 miles, each carrying home 50 pounds of flour.

        Adam Leno family: Back row, Christina, Henry, Jacob, Peter and
        Johanna. Front, Mr. Adam Leno, George, Mrs. Adam Leno and
        Emmanuel.

In the late fall of 1890, Adam's family and their neighbors experienced
an Indian scare. Stories of horrible massacres of whites by Indians
spread throughout the area. Hardware dealers, it seems, made sure that
the settlers heard these reports in order to promote the sale of
firearms. So, when the rumor of an impending attack spread through the
area, a huge throng of settlers from the Sutley territory fled to
Eureka, the railroad terminal, to escape on trains. Before reaching
Eureka, however, many heard that it was a false rumor and returned
home. The Leno family witnessed a terrible diphtheria epidemic which
broke out in their neighborhood and spread over a large area. Many
did not believe it to be contagious and visited the sick, taking their
children along. As a result, hundreds of children died in the Sutley
area

The homesteaders in the Sutley community experienced some very hard
times. In 1889, the year the two Dakotas became states, they suffered a
grasshopper plague. A heavy frost on the night of July 4, 1894, turned
all the crops black by morning. Drought damaged the crops in 1894,
1898, 1900, 1901, and 1902. Like many of his neighbors, Adam had to
mortgage his homestead. In 1902, he surrendered it and moved to a farm
six miles from Java which was owned by a Mr. Banik, a Java businessman.
In 1903, he and a group of friends and acquaintances who had been
drought victims went to look at some land in western North Dakota which
the government had opened up for homesteading. They filed claims on land
about 28 miles south of Hebron, North Dakota.

In the spring of 1904, they moved to their new locations with covered
wagons and all their personal property including livestock, crossing
the Missouri River on a ferry somewhere near Cannonball, North Dakota.
A small inland village by the name of Willa was established in their new
community to provide mail service as well as a general store and a
blacksmith shop. In the fall of 1910, a railroad was built through the
area about 15 miles south of Willa. By 1932, the Willa Post Office was
closed down and their new address became Burt, North Dakota.

All of the children were born in Campbell county, north of what is now
Java, S. Dak. Henry, the oldest of the children, was born in 1886 near
Sutley, Dakota Territory. Some of their grandchildren and great
grandchildren still live in the Burt community. Many, however, moved
west during the depression of the 1930's. Among these was George Leno
who is the sole survivor of those shown on the picture and who now
lives at College Place, Washington.
